White Paper
Ver.2.1

Abstract




RUP-26-2

Abstract

CHRONOS (Cryogenic sub-Hz cROss torsion-bar detector with quantum
NOn-demolition Speed meter) is a proposed cryogenic torsion-bar interferometer
for precision measurements in the 0.1–10 Hz band. A Sagnac speed-meter
readout, power and signal recycling, and balanced homodyne detection provide
control of the optical response and quantum measurement correlations.
This White Paper describes the science program, detector design, calibration,
and staged experimental development.

The reference sensitivity uses a multi-input quantum model, including
rear-mirror vacuum fields and measurement–back-action correlations, combined
with classical noise. Two operating points are optimized for complementary
power-law-integrated sensitivity objectives. Gravitational-wave forecasts
use this common noise budget and state their source and observing assumptions.
The program includes intermediate-mass black-hole binaries, stochastic
backgrounds, quantum measurement, and terrestrial gravity perturbations.
Dark-matter applications are discussed as response and feasibility studies.
A differential gravitational calibrator provides a proposed Yukawa-gravity
test whose systematic floor is improved by subtracting auxiliary harmonics.
These are conditional projections: experimental reach requires validation
of the noise, transfer functions, environmental backgrounds, and systematic
uncertainties described here.

Chapter 1  Overview of CHRONOS

1.1
Scientific Background and Objectives

The direct detection of gravitational waves from compact binary mergers by Advanced LIGO and Advanced Virgo

[
1
,
2
]

opened a new observational window in astronomy and fundamental physics. KAGRA subsequently extended the global detector network into cryogenic interferometry

[
3
]
.
Current ground-based detectors primarily operate above
\sim 10~\mathrm{Hz}
, while the future space-based observatory LISA will explore the millihertz band

[
4
]
.
The intermediate sub-Hz regime, approximately
0.1
–
10~\mathrm{Hz}
, remains one of the least accessible frequency bands for precision measurement because seismic motion, Newtonian noise, suspension dynamics, and thermal noise become increasingly severe

[
5
]
.

This frequency range is nevertheless scientifically compelling. Macroscopic mechanical systems provide a platform for studying quantum measurement and measurement back action; controlled gravitational sources can probe weak gravitational interactions and possible deviations from Newtonian gravity; and sufficiently sensitive instruments could ultimately access gravitational-wave sources beyond the reach of present terrestrial observatories. In gravitational-wave astronomy, the sub-Hz band is particularly relevant to intermediate-mass black-hole binaries, the early inspiral of stellar-mass compact binaries, and stochastic gravitational-wave backgrounds, and may provide an important link between terrestrial and space-based observations

[
6
]
.

CHRONOS, the Cryogenic sub-Hz cROss torsion-bar detector with quantum NOn-demolition Speed-meter,
is being developed to address this common experimental challenge

[
7
]
.
Rather than optimizing an instrument for a single science target, CHRONOS is conceived as a cryogenic torsion-bar interferometric platform for precision measurements in the sub-Hz band. Its architecture combines freely suspended torsion-bar test masses, Sagnac interferometric readout, vibration isolation, cryogenic operation, and calibrated force and torque actuation within a common experimental framework.

The same detector is intended to support three closely connected scientific directions:

quantum measurement, precision gravity, and gravitational-wave science
.
These programs share a common requirement: the ability to measure extremely small forces, torques, and motions of macroscopic test masses over long timescales.

CHRONOS should therefore not be regarded simply as a lower-frequency version of an existing gravitational-wave detector. Its broader aim is to establish the experimental techniques required for precision interferometry with macroscopic mechanical systems in a frequency regime where conventional terrestrial measurements become increasingly difficult. Quantum measurement, precision gravity, and gravitational-wave detection are treated as successive and complementary applications of a common experimental platform.

1.2
Measurement Principle

The CHRONOS detector is based on the torsion-bar antenna concept

[
8
,
9
,
7
]
.
Unlike conventional Michelson-type gravitational-wave interferometers, which primarily measure translational displacement of suspended test masses, a torsion-bar detector measures angular motion around a torsional degree of freedom.

Several terrestrial experiments have explored this concept in the sub-Hz regime. The torsion-bar antenna (TOBA)

[
8
]

and the Torsion Pendulum Dual Oscillator (TorPeDO)

[
9
]

have demonstrated key elements of torsion-based low-frequency sensing. By exploiting a torsional degree of freedom, the mechanical resonance can be placed at very low frequency, providing a natural route toward sensitivity in the sub-Hz band.

The torsion-bar architecture is central to the CHRONOS strategy. Torsional motion provides a low-frequency mechanical degree of freedom that is sensitive to weak torques and can be monitored continuously using an optical interferometer. The test masses are supported by low-loss suspensions and are ultimately operated under cryogenic conditions to suppress thermal noise.

A defining feature of CHRONOS is the use of a Sagnac speed-meter interferometer

[
10
,
11
,
7
]
.
In a conventional position measurement, quantum radiation-pressure back action becomes increasingly important at low frequencies. A speed-meter interferometer instead measures a quantity related to the test-mass velocity by sampling its motion at different times. This modifies the low-frequency quantum back-action response and provides a route toward quantum nondemolition measurement

[
10
,
11
]
.

The same suspended torsion bars can also be used as probes of controlled gravitational fields. Known time-dependent gravitational torques can be applied using dedicated source masses, allowing the measured response to be compared directly with gravitational models. At later stages, the same detector architecture can be extended toward measurements of tidal motion induced by gravitational waves.

1.3
Science-driven Development

The development of CHRONOS follows a staged science roadmap. Rather than constructing the final detector in a single step, the project progressively establishes the measurement techniques required for increasingly demanding science goals. Each stage is intended both to produce scientific results and to validate technologies required for the subsequent stages.

The initial program focuses on precision measurements of torsional motion and on characterizing the detector response to controlled actuation. A central objective is the demonstration and characterization of the velocity-type response of the Sagnac interferometer. This provides the experimental basis for studying measurement imprecision, radiation-pressure back action, and their correlations, and for developing quantum nondemolition measurement techniques in a macroscopic mechanical system

[
10
,
11
,
7
]
.

The next stage extends CHRONOS toward precision gravity. Controlled gravitational sources can generate predictable periodic forces and torques on the suspended test masses, allowing direct comparison between the measured response and gravitational models. Rotating gravitational field calibrators provide both a calibration reference and a means to search for deviations from Newtonian gravity. In particular, CHRONOS targets Yukawa-type corrections to gravity at meter-scale interaction lengths.

In the longer term, these techniques will be integrated toward sub-Hz gravitational-wave observations. This requires low-frequency vibration isolation, cryogenic suspension, stable long-duration control, high-sensitivity interferometric readout, and reliable calibration to operate as a single system. CHRONOS approaches this goal incrementally, using quantum-measurement and precision-gravity experiments to establish the technologies required for gravitational-wave detection in the sub-Hz band.

Such an instrument would complement both terrestrial interferometers and future space-based detectors such as LISA

[
4
]
.
In particular, access to the intermediate frequency range may enable long-duration tracking of compact binaries and contribute to multiband gravitational-wave astronomy

[
6
]
.

1.4
Detector Concept

The detector design is driven directly by these science requirements. The interferometer, mechanical system, control, calibration, and analysis are therefore treated as components of a single measurement architecture rather than as independent subsystems.

The optical system is centered on a Sagnac interferometer. Counter-propagating beams sample the test-mass motion at different times, producing the characteristic speed-meter response

[
10
]
.
Power recycling and signal recycling are incorporated to enhance the optical response, following concepts developed for advanced interferometric gravitational-wave detectors

[
12
,
13
,
7
]
.
The input optics provide stable laser light together with the modulation and spatial-mode conditioning required for interferometer sensing and control.

The optical configuration must simultaneously provide high sensitivity to the relevant torsional degrees of freedom and sufficient control authority for stable operation. Its design is therefore optimized together with the recycling cavities, readout configuration, and interferometer control system

[
7
]
.

Environmental isolation is equally critical. Ground translation and rotation can couple into torsional motion through suspension asymmetries and cross-couplings among mechanical degrees of freedom. CHRONOS therefore uses a hierarchical vibration-isolation architecture combining room-temperature seismic isolation, multistage mechanical filtering, and cryogenic suspension stages.

Isolation and control must be designed together. Soft mechanical degrees of freedom are desirable for low-frequency isolation, whereas lock acquisition, alignment, damping, and recovery from large disturbances require sufficient active-control authority. The suspension and interferometer control systems must therefore suppress unwanted motion while minimizing the coupling of control noise into the science observables.

Cryogenic operation is introduced primarily to reduce thermal noise and provide a stable environment for long-duration precision measurements. The test-mass substrates, suspension materials, thermal links, and cooling architecture affect mechanical loss, resonant behavior, optical performance, actuation, and control. These elements must therefore be optimized as part of the integrated detector design.

1.5
Noise and Sensitivity

Sensitivity in the sub-Hz regime is determined by several classes of noise, including quantum noise, suspension and substrate thermal noise, coating thermal noise, seismic noise, and Newtonian noise. Newtonian noise is particularly severe at low frequencies because fluctuations in the surrounding mass distribution produce gravitational forces that cannot be removed by mechanical isolation alone

[
5
]
.

The Sagnac speed-meter topology is intended to improve the low-frequency quantum-noise response by suppressing radiation-pressure back action relative to a conventional position-meter configuration

[
10
,
11
]
.
Cryogenic operation reduces thermal noise, while the vibration-isolation and suspension systems are designed to suppress direct seismic coupling.

The baseline CHRONOS design targets a strain sensitivity of approximately

\sqrt{S_{h}^{\rm tot}}\simeq 8.31\times 10^{-19}~\mathrm{Hz}^{-1/2}

(1.1)

at
2~\mathrm{Hz}
for the minimum-PI reference point. The alternative
logarithmic-mean point gives
7.92\times 10^{-19}~\mathrm{Hz}^{-1/2}
there.
The common parameters, full noise curves, complex transfer functions, and
optimization assumptions are specified in Chap.
3

[
14
]
.

1.6
Actuation, Calibration, and Reconstruction

Controlled force and torque injection is a core element of CHRONOS. In addition to conventional suspension actuators, photon-pressure actuators provide contact-free excitation of the test masses through radiation pressure. They can be used to measure mechanical and optical transfer functions and to characterize the detector response without direct mechanical contact.

The Gravitational Field Calibrator provides an independent calibration reference based directly on Newtonian gravity. A controlled moving mass distribution generates a predictable periodic gravitational torque on the test masses. Comparing this excitation with the measured interferometric response provides a calibration method that is independent of conventional actuator models. The same system also enables direct searches for non-Newtonian gravitational interactions.

The final science observables are not obtained from a single photodetector output. CHRONOS is a closed-loop, multi-degree-of-freedom dynamical system comprising the interferometer, suspensions, sensors, actuators, and digital control system. The physical motion of interest must therefore be reconstructed from multiple sensing and control channels.

The analysis framework must determine the relevant mechanical and optical transfer functions, quantify cross-couplings, model the closed-loop detector response, and propagate calibration uncertainties to the reconstructed physical observables. Analysis is therefore an integral part of the detector design rather than a separate post-processing stage.

1.7
Scope of this White Paper

This White Paper presents the baseline framework for the CHRONOS science program and detector design.

It first introduces the CHRONOS Collaboration and the Science Roadmap that guides the development of the project. The principal science goals are then described, with particular emphasis on quantum measurement and precision-gravity experiments, including searches for Yukawa-type deviations from Newtonian gravity.

The subsequent chapters describe the detector systems required to realize these goals: the Sagnac interferometer, input optics, interferometer sensing and control, vibration isolation and suspension, test-mass substrates, cryogenic system, photon-pressure actuation, and gravitational calibration. The analysis framework then describes how signals from these subsystems are combined to reconstruct calibrated physical observables.

The design presented here should not be viewed as a collection of independent subsystem developments. In CHRONOS, the science goals define the observables; the detector architecture determines how they are measured; actuation and calibration establish their physical scale; and analysis reconstructs them from the response of the controlled detector system as a whole.

Within this integrated framework, CHRONOS is intended to evolve from a prototype-scale precision torsion experiment into a platform for quantum measurement, precision gravity, and gravitational-wave science. Its broader objective is to establish the experimental techniques required for precision measurement in the sub-Hz regime and to open this largely unexplored frequency range as a new observational window for fundamental physics.

Chapter 2  CHRONOS Collaboration

2.1
Overview of the CHRONOS Collaboration

CHRONOS is an international scientific collaboration involving multiple
universities and research institutes and is organized to support a long-term
research program in sub-Hz gravitational physics, quantum measurement, and
fundamental physics.

The collaboration brings together researchers from different countries,
institutions, scientific disciplines, cultural backgrounds, and career stages.
In order to support coordinated experimental development, detector operation,
data analysis, theoretical studies, and scientific dissemination, CHRONOS
maintains a common framework for governance, research integrity, management of
research outputs, and professional conduct.

The governance and operation of CHRONOS are defined primarily through three
complementary documents:

•

CHRONOS Organization and Governance

[
15
]
, which defines the organizational structure,
positions, responsibilities, authorities, and meeting structure of the
collaboration;

•

CHRONOS Collaboration Policy

[
16
]
, which defines the rules governing
scientific integrity, research records, intellectual contributions,
authorship, data and software, experimental apparatus, internal
information, publication, internal review, and external communication; and

•

CHRONOS Code of Conduct

[
17
]
, which defines standards for interpersonal
and professional behavior, fair treatment, respectful communication,
prevention of discrimination and harassment, consultation and reporting,
and protection against retaliation.

These documents have complementary functions and together establish the
organizational and professional framework of the CHRONOS Collaboration

[
15
,
16
,
17
]
.

The Collaboration Policy defines CHRONOS research outputs broadly. They include
data, analysis results, figures, manuscripts, presentation materials, software,
source code, hardware designs, technical documents, research procedures,
models, simulations, research records, research ideas, and other outputs
created, developed, acquired, analyzed, or shared within the collaboration

[
16
]
.

2.2
Organization and Governance

The CHRONOS Collaboration consists of the Executive Committee, the Principal
Investigators and members of participating universities and research
institutes, and Emeritus Members. The Executive Committee coordinates the
collaboration as a whole, while each institutional PI is responsible for
organizing CHRONOS activities within the corresponding institution

[
15
]
.

The Executive Committee is organized around six principal positions:

1.

Principal Investigator,

2.

Experiment Coordinator,

3.

Instruments Coordinator,

4.

Science Coordinator,

5.

Publication and Authorship Coordinator, and

6.

Membership Coordinator.

The Principal Investigator chairs the Executive Committee and oversees
CHRONOS as a whole. The PI is responsible for maintaining coherence among
scientific, experimental, technical, and organizational activities, defining
the medium- and long-term direction of the collaboration, coordinating the
work of the coordinators, maintaining communication with participating
institutions, and representing CHRONOS in communications with external
institutions, funding agencies, and collaborators

[
15
]
.

Matters that substantially affect the collaboration as a whole, including
major budgets, inter-institutional responsibilities, long-term strategy, and
serious organizational issues, are discussed by the Executive Committee.
Consensus is sought whenever practical

[
15
]
.

While the collaboration remains relatively small, one person may hold more
than one organizational position. The detailed responsibilities and
authorities associated with the Executive Committee and the individual
coordinator positions are defined in the

CHRONOS Organization and Governance

[
15
]
.

2.3
Coordination of Science, Experiment, and Instrumentation

CHRONOS distinguishes the coordination of scientific objectives,
experimental operation, and instrument development while maintaining close
interaction among these activities

[
15
,
16
]
.

2.3.1
Scientific Coordination

The Science Coordinator organizes and promotes the scientific program of
CHRONOS. The coordinator maintains the scientific goals and overall science
program of the collaboration, receives proposals for new research, data
analysis, simulations, and scientific studies, and evaluates their scientific
significance, originality, feasibility, and resource requirements

[
15
]
.

The Science Coordinator also identifies duplication or overlap among research
topics, develops priorities for scientific activities, and defines the
measurements, sensitivity targets, and instrument-performance requirements
needed to achieve the scientific objectives of CHRONOS.

These scientific requirements are communicated to the Experiment Coordinator
and Instruments Coordinator so that experimental and technical development
remain aligned with the science program

[
15
]
.

The Science Coordinator may establish scientific working groups when needed
and coordinates data analysis, signal modeling, noise studies, simulations,
sensitivity evaluations, and scientific collaboration with external
researchers

[
15
]
.

2.3.2
Experimental Coordination

The Experiment Coordinator oversees CHRONOS experimental projects and on-site
activities and is responsible for planning, coordinating, and reporting
experimental activities

[
15
]
.

The responsibilities include preparation of annual, medium-term, and
short-term experimental plans and coordination of installation, assembly,
testing, commissioning, and operation of the experiment.

The Experiment Coordinator defines schedules, tasks, required personnel,
milestones, and deliverables for experimental activities and coordinates work
among participating members and institutions. The coordinator also tracks
experimental progress, measurement results, technical problems, and follow-up
actions and reports the experimental status to the Executive Committee and
the collaboration

[
15
]
.

Experimental operation, measurement planning, and approval of experimental
configurations are overseen by the Experiment Coordinator

[
16
]
.

2.3.3
Instrumentation Coordination

The Instruments Coordinator oversees the development of the instruments,
subsystems, and facilities required for CHRONOS and leads the prioritization
of instrument-development activities

[
15
]
.

This responsibility includes coordination of development plans for optics,
mechanics, vacuum systems, cryogenics, control systems, data acquisition, and
other technical systems.

Development activities are evaluated according to scientific importance,
technical necessity, urgency, feasibility, cost, staffing, and schedule.
The Instruments Coordinator maintains the overall list of instruments and
subsystems, tracks specifications, responsible persons, budgets, delivery
dates, risks, and progress, and ensures technical compatibility among
subsystems developed by different institutions

[
15
]
.

The Instruments Coordinator also coordinates procurement, fabrication,
modification, outsourcing, transport, maintenance, and upgrades and clarifies
responsibility for the ownership, custody, storage, operation, and maintenance
of equipment

[
15
]
.

Major decisions involving substantial budgets, significant institutional
commitments, or major changes to the overall research plan require discussion
by the Executive Committee and approval by the PI

[
15
]
.

2.4
Scientific Integrity and Research Records

Scientific integrity, transparency, reproducibility, and appropriate
preservation of research records are fundamental requirements of CHRONOS
research activities

[
16
]
.

Members must conduct research honestly, accurately, transparently, and
ethically and must appropriately document the research process and relevant
decisions.

Research must be documented sufficiently to permit appropriate review,
verification, and, where practicable, reproduction. Relevant records include:

•

research methods and assumptions;

•

original and processed data;

•

code and software versions;

•

analysis procedures and significant analysis decisions;

•

uncertainty estimates;

•

apparatus configurations and design changes; and

•

revisions to research results and scientific conclusions.

Important research records must not be destroyed, concealed, altered, or
withheld for the purpose of obstructing verification, reproducibility,
internal review, or formal investigation

[
16
]
.

Honest scientific errors are distinguished from research misconduct. When an
error is identified, members are expected to disclose it promptly and, where
appropriate, correct the relevant records, analyses, presentations, or
publications and notify affected collaborators

[
16
]
.

2.5
Intellectual Contributions and Attribution

Scientific, technical, organizational, and intellectual contributions within
CHRONOS must be recognized fairly and accurately

[
16
]
.

Members must not improperly appropriate another person’s research idea,
analysis strategy, experimental concept, design, software, text, figure,
calculation, research method, or research result.

Significant scientific and technical decisions, assigned responsibilities,
and major contributions should be documented whenever practicable

[
16
]
.

Particular care is required to recognize the contributions of students,
postdoctoral researchers, and other early-career researchers. Unpublished
ideas, research outputs, or scientific strategies shared within CHRONOS may
not be used for external research without the appropriate CHRONOS approval

[
16
]
.

2.6
Authorship and Presentation Rights

Authorship, author order, presentation rights, research credit, and
acknowledgements are determined on the basis of actual contributions

[
16
]
.

They must not be determined improperly solely on the basis of position,
seniority, institutional affiliation, control of research funding, employment
or supervisory authority, age, personal relationships, or access to
institutional resources.

A legitimate contributor must not be excluded from authorship or presentation
without a valid reason. Conversely, authorship must not be granted solely on
the basis of status, authority, or control of funding when no meaningful
contribution has been made

[
16
]
.

Where practicable, authorship and author order should be discussed from an
early stage of a research project and reviewed as the work develops.

The selection of presenters must also be fair. In particular, appropriate
presentation opportunities should be provided to students and early-career
researchers who have actually performed or led the relevant work

[
16
]
.

Disputes concerning authorship, author order, presentation rights, or
contributions are handled on the basis of documented actual contributions and
may be referred to the Publication and Authorship Coordinator and the
Executive Committee

[
16
]
.

2.7
Management of Data, Software, and Internal Information

CHRONOS data, software, designs, technical documents, analysis results,
unpublished outputs, meeting records, and internal discussions are managed as
collaborative research assets associated with CHRONOS research activities

[
16
]
.

Members are required to comply with collaboration access-control,
data-management, and information-security requirements and to appropriately
document analysis procedures, code modifications, and significant technical
decisions.

Creators and contributors of software, code, designs, documents, and analyses
must be properly identified and recorded.

Data, software, and analysis results should be maintained in a reasonably
reproducible form, and records necessary for scientific verification must be
preserved

[
16
]
.

When members change roles or leave CHRONOS, necessary research records, data,
code, and other research outputs must be appropriately transferred.

Unpublished CHRONOS information must not be used for unauthorized personal
benefit, to obtain an improper competitive advantage, to exclude another
contributor, or for an unauthorized external research project

[
16
]
.

2.8
Management of Experimental Apparatus

CHRONOS experimental apparatus and technical systems are regarded as important
collaborative research assets

[
16
]
.

These assets include optical systems, mechanical systems, electrical and
electronic systems, control systems, vacuum systems, cryogenic systems,
sensors, actuators, data-acquisition systems, and other experimental
facilities.

The Instruments Coordinator oversees instrumentation status, technical
development, and technical contributions assigned to participating
institutions. The actual operation of the experimental apparatus, measurement
planning, and experimental configurations are overseen by the Experiment
Coordinator

[
16
]
.

Members must not make modifications that may materially affect the experiment
without the appropriate prior approval. This includes disassembly or
modification of apparatus, alteration of experimental configurations, removal
or replacement of components, modification of wiring or control systems, and
other operations that may affect ongoing measurements, calibration, data
acquisition, or analysis

[
16
]
.

Where appropriate, modifications to the apparatus must be documented,
including the nature and reason for the change, the responsible person, the
implementation time, and the resulting configuration.

Physical access to equipment or the technical ability to operate it does not
by itself constitute authority to modify the equipment independently

[
16
]
.

2.9
Scientific Policy and Research Coordination

The Science Coordinator oversees CHRONOS scientific policy, scientific
priorities, major scientific objectives, and the coordination of scientific
research

[
16
]
.

Research topics, analysis activities, and scientific studies are coordinated
to avoid excessive duplication, unnecessary competition, or conflicts in
research planning.

Changes that may have a significant impact on CHRONOS scientific policy are
discussed by the Science Coordinator with the Executive Committee.

Individual members conduct research in a manner consistent with CHRONOS
scientific policy and approved research plans

[
16
]
.

2.10
Publication, Internal Review,and External Communication

Scientific and technical outputs produced within CHRONOS are subject to an
internal review and approval process before external release

[
16
]
.

The scope of this process includes:

•

journal submissions;

•

preprints;

•

conference abstracts;

•

oral presentations;

•

posters;

•

proceedings;

•

theses;

•

technical reports;

•

press releases;

•

communications with the media;

•

public websites;

•

software repositories;

•

data repositories; and

•

disclosure to external research groups or third parties.

Before external release, the relevant output must undergo the scientific,
technical, authorship, and publication review procedures established within
CHRONOS

[
16
]
.

The Publication and Authorship Coordinator manages publications, conference
presentations, technical documents, and other public dissemination of CHRONOS
results. The coordinator records proposed outputs, coordinates writing teams
and internal reviewers, tracks review progress, and confirms that authorship
and author order reflect substantive contributions

[
15
]
.

Where appropriate, the Science Coordinator participates in the review of
scientific content, while the Experiment Coordinator and Instruments
Coordinator participate in review of experimental and technical content

[
16
]
.

The purpose of internal review is to ensure scientific validity, technical
consistency, appropriate attribution, and responsible communication of
CHRONOS results.

2.11
Code of Conduct

CHRONOS considers a safe, respectful, fair, and professionally responsible
working environment to be an essential component of scientific collaboration

[
17
]
.

All members are expected to respect the dignity, expertise, experience,
opinions, and contributions of others and to treat participants fairly in
professional interactions and in access to research opportunities.

Members are expected to communicate constructively and courteously, including
when scientific or organizational disagreements arise, and to respect
differences in nationality, culture, language, institution, discipline,
career stage, and personal background

[
17
]
.

The Code of Conduct applies to all CHRONOS-related activities, including
in-person and online meetings, electronic communication, laboratories,
experimental facilities, conferences, workshops, schools, training
activities, outreach, travel, meals, social events, and activities undertaken
externally in the name of CHRONOS

[
17
]
.

2.12
Fair Treatment and Professional Conduct

All members must treat one another fairly and respectfully.

No participant may be unfairly disadvantaged in meeting participation,
access to information, assignment of responsibilities, professional
opportunities, or career development because of personal characteristics,
institutional affiliation, career stage, employment status, or differences in
available resources

[
17
]
.

CHRONOS does not tolerate harassment, sexual harassment, bullying,
intimidation, coercion, discrimination, or abuse of authority

[
17
]
.

Scientific and organizational disagreements are recognized as a normal part
of collaborative research. Criticism and disagreement should address
evidence, methods, assumptions, reasoning, technical issues, or
organizational decisions and should be expressed professionally and
constructively

[
17
]
.

Disagreement must not be used as a basis for personal attacks, humiliation,
intimidation, exclusion, harassment, discrimination, or retaliation.

2.13
Responsibilities of Persons in Positions of Authority

Persons exercising supervisory, managerial, evaluative, or organizational
authority within CHRONOS must recognize that their authority may affect the
research, education, employment, and career development of other members

[
17
]
.

They are expected to communicate roles, responsibilities, deadlines, and
expectations clearly, provide appropriate supervision and constructive
feedback, treat members fairly, and provide reasonable educational and
research opportunities to students and early-career researchers.

Authority must not be used to impose excessive or inappropriate workloads,
disadvantage a person for raising a concern or scientific disagreement, or
improperly influence employment, evaluation, recommendations, contract
renewal, or career progression

[
17
]
.

Relevant conflicts of interest must be disclosed, and appropriate action must
be taken when persons in positions of authority become aware of harassment,
bullying, discrimination, retaliation, or safety concerns.

2.14
Consultation, Reporting, and Protection against Retaliation

General concerns under the Code of Conduct and alleged violations are overseen
by the Membership Coordinator

[
17
]
.

Members may seek advice or report concerns to the Membership Coordinator or,
where appropriate, to the PI of their participating institution.

Confidential consultation may be sought before a formal complaint is made.
Where necessary, the nature, seriousness, urgency, safety risks, conflicts of
interest, and applicable CHRONOS rules are assessed before determining the
appropriate procedure

[
17
]
.

CHRONOS prohibits retaliation against any person who, in good faith, seeks
advice, raises a concern, makes a report, provides information or evidence,
participates in a review, supports another member, or contacts an institution,
funding agency, ethics body, or other competent authority

[
17
]
.

Information concerning consultations, reports, complaints, reviews,
protective measures, and corrective actions is handled as confidentially as
reasonably possible, subject to the requirements of safety, fair review,
fact-finding, prevention of retaliation, legal obligations, and external
reporting

[
17
]
.

2.15
Membership Coordination

The Membership Coordinator manages membership records and serves as the
primary contact for membership matters, concerns under the Code of Conduct,
and proposals for organizational improvement

[
15
]
.

The coordinator receives applications from new members, institutional
members, and Emeritus Members and maintains records of names, affiliations,
roles, contact information, and participation status.

New members are provided with the relevant organizational rules,
Collaboration Policy, publication and data requirements, and Code of Conduct.

The Membership Coordinator also receives consultations and reports concerning
the Code of Conduct and serves as an initial contact for concerns involving
harassment, research integrity, authorship, misuse of results, conflicts of
interest, or related matters

[
15
]
.

The Membership Coordinator does not independently conduct formal
investigations, determine facts, or impose sanctions. Serious matters are
referred, where appropriate, to institutions, funding bodies, or other bodies
with suitable investigative authority

[
15
]
.

2.16
Participating Institutions and Members

Each participating university or research institute appoints an institutional
PI who serves as the principal point of contact between the Executive
Committee and members of that institution

[
15
]
.

The institutional PI coordinates CHRONOS members within the institution,
supports and supervises students, postdoctoral researchers, technical staff,
and other members, and monitors the institution’s contributions to
experiments, instrument development, and scientific research.

Institutional PIs also coordinate local budgets, facilities, personnel, and
safety procedures, communicate decisions of the Executive Committee to local
members, collect proposals, requests, and concerns, and coordinate with the
relevant CHRONOS coordinators

[
15
]
.

Institutional procedures concerning research integrity, safety, intellectual
property, export control, and related obligations remain the responsibility of
the relevant participating institution.

At the same time, scientific participation is not restricted by institutional
boundaries. Members may participate in experiments, instrument development,
simulations, data analysis, scientific studies, publications, and working
groups across participating institutions

[
15
]
.

2.17
Emeritus Members

Researchers who have made sustained contributions to CHRONOS and have retired
from an active academic or research position may be appointed as Emeritus
Members

[
15
]
.

Emeritus Members may remain involved without a current institutional
affiliation and may participate in scientific and technical activities,
advise on research direction and technical development, mentor students and
early-career researchers, review papers and technical documents, and
contribute to the transfer of institutional knowledge and technical
experience.

Emeritus status alone does not automatically confer authorship, priority in
research activities, or unrestricted rights to use CHRONOS results. Such
rights remain determined by substantive contribution and the applicable
CHRONOS policies

[
15
]
.

2.18
Conflicts of Interest

Members must disclose conflicts of interest that could reasonably affect
decisions concerning authorship, internal review, access to information, data
use, publication, project allocation, resource allocation, complaints, or
corrective action

[
16
]
.

The PI, coordinators, and institutional PIs must not use their positions to
improperly control authorship, research outputs, access to data, research
opportunities, employment, recommendations, contract renewal, or career
progression.

Where an actual, potential, or perceived conflict of interest exists and
impartiality may reasonably be questioned, the person concerned must withdraw
from the relevant review, deliberation, or decision

[
16
]
.

2.19
Obligations after Leaving CHRONOS

Certain obligations arising from participation in CHRONOS continue after a
member leaves the collaboration

[
16
]
.

These include obligations concerning confidentiality, unpublished CHRONOS
information, data and software use, authorship and attribution, internal
review, and publication approval.

Leaving CHRONOS does not automatically create an independent right to use,
continue, present, or publish unpublished CHRONOS research outputs.

Published CHRONOS results may be cited and discussed according to normal
academic practice, but the contributions of CHRONOS or individual
collaborators must not be misrepresented as the independent or sole work of a
former participant

[
16
]
.

2.20
Relationship to Institutional and External Rules

The internal rules of CHRONOS do not replace the applicable policies,
procedures, and legal requirements of participating institutions, host
institutions, research facilities, funding agencies, publishers, professional
societies, or public authorities

[
16
,
17
]
.

Members remain subject to applicable requirements concerning research
integrity, research ethics, intellectual property, confidentiality,
collaboration agreements, employment, funding conditions, safety, and
applicable laws.

Where necessary, CHRONOS procedures and formal procedures conducted by
institutions, funding agencies, or other competent external authorities may
operate in parallel

[
16
]
.

2.21
Operating Principles

The governance of CHRONOS is based on the principles of transparency,
fairness, accountability, and coordination

[
15
]
.

Important decisions and their reasons should be documented and communicated
to relevant members. Opportunities, responsibilities, budgets, authorship,
and access to research results should be handled according to clear and
consistent criteria.

Each organizational role is accountable for explaining decisions and
reporting progress within its assigned area. Matters spanning multiple roles
should be handled jointly and, where necessary, brought to the Executive
Committee

[
15
]
.

Experimental safety, scientific integrity, respectful conduct, privacy, and
protection against retaliation take priority over schedule or operational
convenience

[
15
]
.

Through these principles and the complementary governance documents described
above, the CHRONOS Collaboration aims to provide a sustainable framework in
which researchers and participating institutions can contribute fairly,
responsibly, and effectively to a long-term international experimental
program.

Chapter 3  Sensitivity

The reference sensitivity in this White Paper uses the multi-input quantum
model of Ref.
[
14
]
. It includes the vacuum fields
entering the main input and the rear ports of the ring-cavity end and middle
test masses, together with measurement–back-action correlations. Classical
noise is added to form the full detector noise budget. Two operating points
are retained: one minimizes the lowest value of a power-law-integrated (PI)
curve, and the other minimizes its logarithmic mean over 0.1–10 Hz.
Unless an alternative is explicitly identified, the minimum-PI point is the
reference configuration.

Figure
3.1
replaces the earlier sensitivity estimate.
Quantum noise is the leading contribution over 0.1–10 Hz for these reference
points; coating and bar thermal noise contribute toward the upper end of the
band, particularly at the minimum-PI point. The relative importance of noise
sources must be read from this full budget rather than inferred from an ideal,
lossless speed-meter model. Table
3.1
lists the shared
parameters, and Table
3.3
gives the two phase
settings and circulating powers.

Figure 3.1:
Full strain noise budget reproduced from Fig. 7 of
Ref.
[
14
]
. (a) Minimum-PI point; (b) minimum-logarithmic-mean
point. Black: total noise; dashed purple: quantum noise including correlations.
Coating Brownian, bar thermal, seismic, Newtonian, and intensity noise are
included in the PSD sum, also where a component lies below the plotting floor.
The displayed band is 0.05–10 Hz; the optimization band is 0.1–10 Hz.
The ordinate is an amplitude spectral density (ASD), in
\mathrm{Hz}^{-1/2}
.

3.1
Reference parameters and strain calibration

Here
f
is frequency in hertz,
\Omega=2\pi f
is angular frequency in
radians per second, and
\hbar
is the reduced Planck constant. Fourier
amplitudes use the time dependence
e^{+i\Omega t}
.
All spectra are one-sided power spectral densities (PSDs):
S_{h}(f)
is the
equivalent strain-noise PSD and
S_{\theta}(f)
is the equivalent input-angle
noise PSD. Their square roots are amplitude spectral densities (ASDs).
Let
\theta_{X}
and
\theta_{Y}
denote the externally driven signal rotations
of the two orthogonal bars before optical feedback. Their differential angle is

\theta_{\rm sig}=\theta_{X}-\theta_{Y}=\eta h
, with
\eta=0.93656
for the
adopted mass geometry. Here
h
is the antenna-projected strain in the
free-rotation, long-wavelength limit. A specified source direction and
polarization require the corresponding antenna response. Thus

S_{h}=S_{\theta}/|\eta|^{2}
; the angular and strain ASDs must not be interchanged.
The differential coordinate has inertia
I_{d}=I/2
, where
I
is the inertia
of one bar. The standard quantum limit (SQL) is the uncorrelated
measurement-imprecision/back-action benchmark; its differential angular ASD is

\theta_{\rm SQL,d}=\sqrt{4\hbar/(I\Omega^{2})}
.

The mirror abbreviations are ITM (input test mass), ETM (end test mass),
MTM (middle test mass), PRM (power-recycling mirror), and SRM
(signal-recycling mirror). PR and SR denote power and signal recycling.

Table 3.1:
Shared parameters for both reference operating points, from
Ref.
[
14
]
. Transmissions and reflectivities are power
quantities. The quoted circulating power in Table
3.3

is for one propagation direction in a ring.

The reference calculation treats the nonreflected ETM and MTM light as
transmitted light and includes the associated independent rear-port vacuum
inputs. Absorption, suspension control, and departures from the specified
symmetric geometry require their own model. Residual laser-frequency and
actuator noise require separate engineering allocations; the six-component
reference budget is not an exhaustive list of technical noise. In particular, a large cancellation
of the driving torques does not alone establish a small calibrated readout
noise: mechanical feedback and the signal transfer function also enter.
