3.2
Sensing Function and final strain sensitivity

The
Sensing Function
describes how a small signal angle becomes a
measurable output. Let
\delta P_{\rm BHD}(f)
be the signal-induced change
in the balanced-homodyne-detector (BHD) power difference. The complex angular
sensing function
H_{\theta}(f)
maps
\theta_{\rm sig}
to this power
difference; its magnitude is the gain in
\mathrm{W/rad}
and its phase is
the output phase relative to the angular input. The corresponding
strain sensing function is
H_{h}=\eta H_{\theta}
, in watts per unit strain.
These optical functions precede the photodetector electronics and digitization.

Only the final readout expressions are retained below; their optical boundary
conditions and derivation are given in Ref.
[
14
]
.
The dimensionless complex denominator
M(f)
contains the optical return
paths and radiation-pressure feedback. The scalar
\mathcal{D}(f)
is the
signal numerator after projection onto the detected power difference,
using
\theta_{\rm sig}/\theta_{\rm SQL,d}
as signal normalization.
For independent vacuum input
q
,
\mathcal{C}_{q}(f)
is a row with two
entries, the coefficients of its amplitude and phase quadratures in the
same output. These quadratures have unit one-sided symmetrized vacuum PSD;

\dagger
denotes complex-conjugate transpose. Both signal and
local-oscillator (LO) paths are included coherently in each projected
coefficient. These frequency-dependent quantities are calculated from the
reference model and the specified operating point, not independent fit constants.

The phase
\beta_{\rm sag}
is the bare-Sagnac phase in that model.
Specifically, if
r_{\rm cav}
is the complex ring amplitude reflectivity,
define its unwrapped phase
\beta_{\rm ring}
by

r_{\rm cav}=-|r_{\rm cav}|e^{-2i\beta_{\rm ring}}
, with

\beta_{\rm ring}(0)=0
. For propagation time
\tau_{\rm link}
along the
Sagnac paths outside the rings,

\beta_{\rm sag}=2\beta_{\rm ring}+\Omega\tau_{\rm link}/2+\pi/2
.
With
S_{h}^{\rm quant}
denoting the quantum strain-noise PSD,
the sensing function and equivalent quantum strain ASD are then

\displaystyle H_{\theta}(f)

\displaystyle\equiv\frac{\delta P_{\rm BHD}(f)}{\theta_{\rm sig}(f)}=\frac{e^{-i\beta_{\rm sag}(f)}\mathcal{D}(f)}{M(f)\theta_{\rm SQL,d}(f)},\qquad H_{h}(f)=\eta H_{\theta}(f),

(3.1)

\displaystyle\sqrt{S_{h}^{\rm quant}(f)}

\displaystyle\simeq\frac{\theta_{\rm SQL,d}(f)}{|\eta|}\frac{\sqrt{\displaystyle\sum_{q}\mathcal{C}_{q}(f)\mathcal{C}_{q}^{\dagger}(f)}}{|\mathcal{D}(f)|}.

(3.2)

The ratio defining
H_{\theta}
is evaluated for a small deterministic signal
with the additive noise set to zero. The retained inputs comprise one main
input, eight ETM-rear inputs, and eight MTM-rear inputs. For each input,
its signal- and LO-path amplitudes are combined coherently before the PSDs
of independent inputs are summed. The approximation denotes the omitted
input ports; their contribution was below 1% in the configurations tested
in the reference, rather than identically zero.

The quantum components

S_{h}^{\rm shot}
,
S_{h}^{\rm ba}
, and
S_{h}^{\rm cross}
denote measurement
imprecision (shot noise), radiation-pressure back action, and their cross
term, respectively. Equivalently, the quantum PSD is

S_{h}^{\rm quant}=S_{h}^{\rm shot}+S_{h}^{\rm ba}+S_{h}^{\rm cross}
.
The cross term is signed and can be negative. Shot and radiation-pressure
ASDs therefore cannot in general be added in quadrature without this term.
All three contributions use the same full signal response, including
optomechanical feedback. For classical noise, the superscripts

\mathrm{coat}
,
\mathrm{bar}
,
\mathrm{seis}
,
\mathrm{NN}
, and

\mathrm{int}
denote coating Brownian, bar thermal, seismic, Newtonian,
and laser intensity noise. The superscript
\mathrm{tot}
denotes their
total with quantum noise. Assuming independent classical contributions,
the final strain sensitivity is

\sqrt{S_{h}^{\rm tot}}=\left[S_{h}^{\rm quant}+S_{h}^{\rm coat}+S_{h}^{\rm bar}+S_{h}^{\rm seis}+S_{h}^{\rm NN}+S_{h}^{\rm int}\right]^{1/2}.

(3.3)

Every term is a
strain PSD in
\mathrm{Hz}^{-1}
, evaluated at the same operating point.

Figure 3.2:
Sensing Function

H_{\theta}
: the complex response from
external differential signal angle
\theta_{\rm sig}
to balanced-detector
power difference
\delta P_{\rm BHD}
, together with quantum strain sensitivity,
reproduced from Fig. 5 of Ref.
[
14
]
. Left: minimum-PI
point; right: minimum-logarithmic-mean point. The first two rows show the
sensing-function magnitude [W/rad] and phase; TF means transfer function.
The remaining rows show quantum-noise ASDs and the signed cross PSD normalized by the sum
of shot and back-action PSDs. The gray dashed ASD omits only the cross term.
Lines are the analytical model and circles are selected FINESSE 3 samples;
shading marks 0.1–10 Hz. The black ASD in this figure is quantum noise,
whereas the black curves in Fig.
3.1
include
classical noise as well.

Figure
3.2
presents the Sensing Function’s magnitude and phase together
with the quantum-noise decomposition. At the minimum-PI point, the ETM and
MTM rear inputs each account for 44.84% of the quantum PSD at 0.1 Hz in the
reference calculation. Omitting these inputs would underestimate the
quantum ASD there by a factor of 3.11. This residual back action explains
why the ideal speed-meter argument alone is insufficient for the noise budget.
The agreement with FINESSE 3 checks the calculated response; it does not
replace a stability assessment for a proposed controlled operating point.

3.3
Optimization with the full noise budget

Let
\Omega_{\rm PI}(f)
denote the dimensionless PI threshold on the
gravitational-wave energy density per logarithmic frequency, expressed as a
fraction of the present critical energy density. This
\Omega_{\rm PI}
is
not the angular frequency
\Omega
. The PI construction is given in
Sec.
9.3
. The phase settings are selected using PI curves formed from
Eq. (
3.3
), following
Refs.
[
18
,
14
]
. The reference assumes
one detector with known noise, one year of effective observation,
signal-to-noise ratio (SNR) 1, and a dimensionless signal-power response
factor
\mathcal{R}=1
. These assumptions define a comparison metric; they
are not the sensitivity of an independently validated stochastic-background
cross-correlation measurement.

For the fixed band
f_{1}=0.1~\mathrm{Hz}
to
f_{2}=10~\mathrm{Hz}
, the two
objectives are

\displaystyle J_{\min}

\displaystyle=\min_{f_{1}\leq f\leq f_{2}}\Omega_{\rm PI}(f),

(3.4)

\displaystyle J_{\log}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\ln(f_{2}/f_{1})}\int_{f_{1}}^{f_{2}}\ln\Omega_{\rm PI}(f)\,d\ln f.

(3.5)

The first favors the lowest PI threshold, while the second weights logarithmic
frequency uniformly;
\exp J_{\log}
is the geometric mean of the PI curve.
The bandwidth itself is not optimized. The quoted operating points are
those calculated in Ref.
[
14
]
. The tunable angles

\delta_{\rm PR}
and
\delta_{\rm SR}
are the PRM and SRM microscopic
phase settings;
\zeta_{\rm LO}
is the LO optical phase setting.

P_{\rm ring}
denotes circulating carrier power in one ring propagation
direction. The table reports angles in degrees; phase relations use radians.

Table 3.3:
Operating points selected from the same full noise budget and
0.1–10 Hz band
[
14
]
.
J_{\min}
is the principal
reference;
J_{\log}
is the alternative favoring the band-wide geometric mean.

Quantity

Minimum
J_{\min}

Minimum
J_{\log}

PR setting
\delta_{\rm PR}
[deg]

66.282

25.301

SR setting
\delta_{\rm SR}
[deg]

3.166

0.705

LO setting
\zeta_{\rm LO}
[deg]

-39.042

-19.163

P_{\rm ring}
, one direction [W]

1609.75

320.09

\min\Omega_{\rm PI}
, one year

4.0142\times 10^{-4}

5.9611\times 10^{-4}

Frequency of PI minimum [Hz]

4.687

1.821

\exp J_{\log}
, one year

2.4034\times 10^{-3}

1.5067\times 10^{-3}

The corresponding optical phases
\Phi_{\rm PR}
,
\Phi_{\rm SR}
, and

\Phi_{h}
for the PR, SR, and homodyne paths, respectively, obey

\Phi_{\rm PR}=\pi/2-2\delta_{\rm PR}
,

\Phi_{\rm SR}=\pi/2-2\delta_{\rm SR}
, and

\Phi_{h}=\pi/2-\zeta_{\rm LO}
. The LO optical setting is independent of SR
setting and is distinct from the quadrature projection angle. In this
convention PR resonance occurs at
\delta_{\rm PR}=90^{\circ}
and SR resonance
at
\delta_{\rm SR}=0^{\circ}
, including the Sagnac return phase.

A consistent optimization varies PR, SR, and LO settings together while
holding input power, optical geometry, mirror properties, bar inertia, and
spot lever fixed. At every trial, the carrier field, circulating power,
complex signal TF, quantum correlations, and intensity noise must be
recalculated before evaluating the full-noise PI objective. PR tuning
changes both internal power and the carrier phase relative to the LO; it
cannot be represented solely by a quadrature rotation at fixed power.
A practical search first explores bounded phase ranges from multiple starting
points, then refines the best candidates with the full frequency grid.
Convergence should be checked by increasing the frequency resolution and
repeating the search; the complex response and noise decomposition should be
cross-checked with an independent optical solver. The reported points are
numerically selected candidates, with no proof of global optimality.
A candidate setting must also satisfy independent power, thermal-load,
and control-stability requirements before experimental adoption.

Figure 3.3:
PI comparison reproduced from Fig. 2 of
Ref.
[
14
]
. Blue minimizes
J_{\min}
; orange minimizes

J_{\log}
. Dots mark the minima. The curve is displayed from 0.05 Hz,
but integration and optimization use only 0.1–10 Hz. Both curves assume
one detector, known noise, one year, SNR 1, and
\mathcal{R}=1
.

The logarithmic-mean choice reduces the geometric mean by 37.3% relative
to the minimum-PI choice, while raising the minimum by 48.5%. It gives
lower full noise at low frequencies, whereas the minimum-PI choice performs
better toward the upper band edge. Selecting a setting for a particular
science target therefore requires its integrated signal statistic, not
only the lowest value of a strain ASD.

3.4
Classical noise and design implications

The classical spectra in Fig.
3.1
follow the
noise-budget and intensity-noise models used by the reference study

[
19
,
20
]
. Their physical and
environmental assumptions are common to the two phase settings; intensity
noise is recalculated with the circulating power and signal TF at each point.
The relevant mitigation measures are summarized here without repeating the
derivations.

3.4.1
Bar and coating thermal noise

Thermal noise follows mechanical dissipation and temperature. Bar and
suspension losses couple through the angular and elastic response;
coating loss produces surface displacement sampled by the optical beam

[
21
,
22
,
23
]
. Cryogenic operation, low-loss materials,
and suitable beam size are therefore required even where quantum noise is
larger. The test-mass and coating programs are described in
Chaps.
17
and
18
.
The reference curves do not establish that either thermal contribution
dominates the entire 1–10 Hz band.

3.4.2
Seismic and Newtonian noise

Ground motion couples to yaw through suspension asymmetries and other
translation-to-rotation paths. Isolation and control must be assessed using
the corresponding measured transfer functions. Newtonian noise instead
arises from fluctuating gravitational fields and cannot be removed by
mechanical isolation; site characterization and environmental subtraction
are separate requirements
[
5
]
. Their relative size is the one
shown in Fig.
3.1
, not a universal crossover
frequency valid for every site or operating point.

3.5
Use of the reference sensitivity in science forecasts

The reference ASD, an integrated SNR, a PI curve, and a systematic-error
floor describe different quantities. Forecasts must identify the operating
point, frequency band, exposure, response convention, and included noise.
The one-year PI values in Table
3.3
scale
as
T_{\rm obs}^{-1/2}
, where
T_{\rm obs}
is effective observing time,
only with the same known-noise and response assumptions. This time is
distinct from the test-mass temperature
T
in Table
3.1
.
At ten years the corresponding minima are
1.2694\times 10^{-4}
and

1.8851\times 10^{-4}
, at the same frequencies.

Compact-binary reach, ideal Yukawa integration times, and the explicitly
defined earthquake template benchmarks in this White Paper are recalculated with the archived full-noise spectra of the reference study.
Other source-specific forecasts require their own calibrated signal model;
replacing a detector noise curve alone does not validate an earlier SNR. The Yukawa systematic-floor projections in
Chap.
8
use the harmonic-subtracted torque model and its stated
metrology assumptions; their finite-time noise must be propagated through
the same calibrated harmonic channels. A gravitational-wave strain ASD
alone also does not define a dark-matter coupling limit.

3.6
CHRONOISE software

CHRONOISE (CHRONOS Noise Estimation Software) is being developed as a common
environment for detector modeling, noise-budget evaluation, and science
forecasts. Its purpose is to keep optical settings, mechanical parameters,
calibration conventions, and derived sensitivity in a reproducible calculation,
so that an instrument change can be traced through to its scientific impact

[
24
]
.

The current software combines FINESSE-based calculations with a staged
analytical quantum model, developed from mirror and ring-cavity responses
through the Sagnac interferometer, recycling cavities, coupled torsion bars,
and balanced readout. The analytical implementation retains all modeled vacuum
inputs; the compact expressions in this chapter use the reference paper’s
17-input approximation. Comparison tools examine complex transfer functions,
shot and back-action noise, their signed correlation, and total quantum noise.
Agreement tests cover selected operating points and parameter variations;
they do not imply validation of every possible configuration.

The graphical interface provides parameter editing, frequency scans, component
noise budgets, transfer-function magnitude and phase, and mechanical-response
views. Science tools calculate known-noise PI curves and, with LALSuite,
compact-binary waveforms and matched-filter reach. Parameter scans and bounded
optimization support comparisons between operating points. Saved configurations,
numerical arrays, and calculation records allow those comparisons to be
reproduced. Optimization uses local exploration, population-based search, and
refinement; a completed evaluation budget is not proof of a global optimum.
Publication reference settings are explicit cases, rather than assumed GUI
defaults. The baseline results here do not include optional squeezing.

Figure 3.4:
Existing CHRONOISE software logo. The software links detector
parameters, noise and response calculations, and conditional science forecasts.

Chapter 4  Science Roadmap

The CHRONOS project is designed as a staged experimental program in which the scientific reach of the observatory expands progressively as the detector matures
[
7
]
. At each stage, scientifically impactful objectives are defined, with the aim of producing a continuous stream of scientific results throughout the development of the experiment
[
25
]
.

The experimental team, coordinated by the Experimental Coordinator, carries out the planned experimental program, tests the relevant hypotheses, and publishes the resulting experimental findings. In parallel, the theory team, under the coordination of the Science Coordinator, explores the scientific potential of CHRONOS and evaluates the feasibility of the proposed measurements and science cases. These activities are coordinated so as to maximize the scientific opportunities and contributions of all collaborators and to translate promising ideas into concrete experimental programs. The priorities for instrument development are defined by the Instruments Coordinator in accordance with the scientific and experimental requirements of each stage.

Rather than defining a single final science target, CHRONOS follows a three-phase roadmap:

1.

principle demonstration
[
7
]
,

2.

gravity-gradient science
[
26
]
, and

3.

gravitational-wave science
[
25
,
7
]
.

The overall concept is summarized in Fig.
4.1
. Each phase has a distinct experimental objective, while the achievements of each stage provide the technological and scientific foundation for the subsequent phase.

4.1
Phase 1: Principle Demonstration

The first phase focuses on establishing the key measurement principles and enabling technologies required for CHRONOS. In particular, this phase aims to demonstrate the operation of a torsion-based interferometric sensor and the optical configurations required for quantum measurements at low frequencies.

One of the primary objectives of this phase is the experimental demonstration of the speed-meter concept and quantum non-demolition (QND) measurement techniques in a macroscopic torsional system. Through these studies, we aim to establish the optical response, quantum-noise behavior, and control techniques ultimately required for CHRONOS to approach its fundamental sensitivity limits.

Within this phase, three successive experimental milestones are defined according to the technological maturity of the instrument and our understanding of its noise performance.

The first milestone is the realization of a speed-meter interferometer
[
10
,
27
]
. A Sagnac interferometer will be constructed, and its characteristic speed-meter response will be experimentally measured. In particular, the objective is to demonstrate the characteristic transfer function proportional to frequency,
f
, over the relevant frequency range. To our knowledge, such a speed-meter response has not yet been experimentally demonstrated in combination with a macroscopic torsion-bar system. Therefore, demonstrating the underlying measurement principle itself has scientific significance, even before the instrument reaches its ultimate displacement or strain sensitivity. An equally important objective at this stage is to establish the calibration procedure and reconstruct the calibrated strain-equivalent time series
h(t)
. These fundamental characterization measurements are intended to be performed at a relatively early stage of the experiment.

The second milestone is the demonstration of quantum non-demolition measurement
[
28
]
. At this stage, the interferometer will be operated under active control and its noise spectrum will be measured quantitatively. Because the quantum-noise spectrum depends on both the optical power and the homodyne readout angle, these parameters will be systematically scanned to characterize the measurement imprecision, quantum back-action, and their correlations. From these measurements, we aim to experimentally demonstrate the QND property associated with the angular degree of freedom, corresponding to the condition

[H,L]=0,

where
H
is the Hamiltonian of the measurement system and
L
denotes the angular momentum of the torsional degree of freedom.

The third milestone is an experimental test of Ozawa’s error–disturbance relation
[
29
,
30
,
31
]
. For this purpose, two dimensionless evaluation parameters are introduced. The Heisenberg indicator is defined as

\mathcal{H}(P,\xi;T)=\frac{\epsilon_{\theta}(P,\xi;T)\,\eta_{L}(P,\xi;T)}{C_{\theta L,T}},

(4.1)

while the Ozawa indicator is defined as

\mathcal{O}(P,\xi;T)=\frac{\epsilon_{\theta}\eta_{L}+\epsilon_{\theta}\sigma_{L}+\sigma_{\theta}\eta_{L}}{C_{\theta L,T}}.

(4.2)

The objective is to determine these quantities experimentally and demonstrate statistically that

\mathcal{H}<1,\qquad\mathcal{O}>1.

The condition
\mathcal{H}<1
indicates a violation of the conventional Heisenberg error–disturbance product, whereas
\mathcal{O}>1
confirms that the generalized Ozawa error–disturbance relation remains satisfied.

In parallel with these quantum-measurement studies, the instrument can also be used to investigate new sensing concepts for dark-matter searches. These measurements exploit the distinctive response of the CHRONOS configuration to weak forces or field-induced differential motions. Some candidate searches may be sensitive to the material composition of the mirrors or test masses, while others motivate tests of the dark-matter response of a speed-meter
configuration. A nonzero calibrated response must be established first,
as described in Chap.
6
. Such science can therefore be pursued during the development of the detector, before CHRONOS reaches its ultimate gravitational sensitivity.

The scientific feasibility and appropriate timing of these measurements will be assessed and coordinated by the Science Coordinator. Based on these studies, the Instruments Coordinator will establish priorities for instrument development, taking into account both technological readiness and the availability of resources and funding. The Experimental Coordinator will then be responsible for organizing and executing the corresponding experimental program.

The primary role of Phase 1 is therefore to experimentally validate the measurement architecture on which the subsequent CHRONOS science program will be built.

4.2
Phase 2: Gravity-Gradient Science

Once stable operation in the low-frequency band has been established, CHRONOS will move into a regime in which environmental and terrestrial gravitational fields can be measured directly. An important prerequisite for entering this second phase is that the interferometer reaches the engineering-run stage and is capable of acquiring stable observational data over extended periods. This requires the deployment of environmental monitoring systems, calibration of the detector response, and establishment of a reliable data-reconstruction pipeline capable of continuously producing trustworthy
h(t)
data for at least approximately one hour.

Signals produced by gravity gradients are expected to be much larger than the targeted gravitational-wave signals and to appear prominently at low frequencies. By actively exploiting these signals, CHRONOS can generate unique scientific results well before reaching its ultimate gravitational-wave sensitivity. At the same time, reliable calibration in the low-frequency regime is essential for accurately reconstructing such signals. For this purpose, a gravity-field calibrator capable of generating a controlled gravitational-field modulation will become one of the key instruments in Phase 2.

In this phase, CHRONOS will observe gravitational signals associated with seismic motion and mass-density perturbations in the Earth and near the surface, while also carrying out precision gravity experiments using artificially generated gravitational fields. Phase 2 is organized around three major scientific objectives.

The first scientific objective is the observation of gravity perturbations induced by earthquakes
[
26
]
. Taiwan, where CHRONOS is planned to be installed, is a region of high seismic activity and therefore provides frequent opportunities to observe time-dependent gravitational fields associated with earthquakes. Signals that are normally treated as environmental disturbances in conventional gravitational-wave detectors can instead be exploited as scientific signals in CHRONOS. Directly targeting earthquake-induced gravitational-field variations in this way constitutes one of the distinctive science cases of CHRONOS.

The second scientific objective is the direct measurement and characterization of Newtonian noise
[
26
]
. Newtonian noise arises when environmental mass-density fluctuations, caused for example by seismic motion, atmospheric disturbances, groundwater motion, or other near-surface mass redistribution, exert direct gravitational forces on the test masses. Because this noise is gravitational in origin, it cannot be suppressed by conventional mechanical isolation. Its characterization and mitigation therefore remain active areas of research in current observatories such as LIGO and Virgo. Moreover, Newtonian noise is expected to become one of the dominant sensitivity limitations for future gravitational-wave detectors operating in the sub-Hz frequency band.

If CHRONOS can directly measure the spatial and temporal properties of Newtonian noise, these studies may contribute to the development of monitoring and mitigation techniques for future gravitational-wave observatories. For example, compact torsion-based gravity-gradient sensors could potentially be deployed as local sensors near the end stations of facilities such as LIGO or Virgo and used for environmental monitoring or feedforward Newtonian-noise subtraction. Related concepts have also been investigated in torsion-pendulum experiments such as TorPeDO. Evaluating, both theoretically and experimentally, whether CHRONOS-type sensors could serve as environmental gravity monitors for future gravitational-wave detectors therefore represents an important research direction within Phase 2.

The third scientific objective is the search for gravitational interactions beyond Newtonian gravity using dedicated gravity-field calibrators
[
28
,
32
]
. By appropriately designing the multipole components of a rotating source mass, a gravity-field calibrator can realize a null configuration in which the Newtonian gravitational contribution to a selected observable is strongly suppressed. Such a configuration enables searches for small deviations from the Newtonian prediction while reducing the dominant conventional gravitational signal, thereby providing sensitivity to possible new interactions, including Yukawa-type forces. The updated forecast combines the Newtonian
null with signed
4f
and
6f
subtraction, applied to both the background
and the Yukawa response (Chap.
8
).

Furthermore, by varying the source-mass distribution, material composition, source-detector separation, rotation conditions, and geometrical configuration, a wide range of experimental conditions may be explored for different models of new physics. It is therefore important not to restrict the program to a single theoretical scenario, but rather to evaluate systematically which classes of new gravitational physics could be tested experimentally with CHRONOS. Under the coordination of the Science Coordinator, candidate science cases will be assessed in terms of sensitivity, feasibility, and required experimental configuration, with the aim of translating the most promising scenarios into concrete measurement programs.

In this way, Phase 2 combines geophysical studies based on naturally occurring gravity gradients with precision gravitational experiments based on artificially controlled gravitational fields. Through these activities, CHRONOS will deepen its understanding of the detector response at low frequencies, calibration, environmental gravitational fields, and gravity-gradient noise.

Phase 2 therefore serves not only as an independent program of gravitational and geophysical science, but also as a crucial bridge connecting detector characterization and precision gravitational physics to the eventual realization of sub-Hz gravitational-wave observations.

4.3
Phase 3: Gravitational-Wave Science

Phase 3 represents the gravitational-wave observation phase of CHRONOS, to be carried out once the detector has reached its final design sensitivity. At this stage, CHRONOS will enter full scientific operation as a sub-Hz gravitational-wave observatory.

Through Phases 1 and 2, the suspension performance, optical sensitivity, low-frequency control, environmental-noise mitigation, calibration, and understanding of gravity-gradient noise will be progressively improved. Building on these developments, CHRONOS aims ultimately to achieve the sensitivity required to search for astrophysical and cosmological gravitational-wave signals. Unlike the earlier phases, which focus primarily on principle demonstrations and detector characterization, the principal objective of Phase 3 is long-duration scientific observation at the final detector sensitivity.

The first major scientific objective is the search for a stochastic gravitational-wave background (SGWB)
[
7
,
25
]
. The sub-Hz band targeted by CHRONOS lies between the frequency ranges explored by current ground-based interferometers and planned space-based gravitational-wave observatories, leaving a frequency region that remains comparatively unexplored. Phase 3 aims to achieve world-leading sensitivity in this observational gap and to open new parameter space for searches for stochastic gravitational-wave backgrounds. A longer-term upgrade objective is to test specified spectra below the
integrated Big Bang Nucleosynthesis (BBN) bound. The reference PI curve in
Chap.
3
does not establish that capability; an integral
cosmological bound is not a pointwise detector threshold.

A robust search for a stochastic gravitational-wave background will also require careful consideration of joint observations with other detectors. With a single detector, it is difficult to completely distinguish a stochastic gravitational-wave signal from local instrumental noise and correlated environmental disturbances. Therefore, in addition to improving the sensitivity of CHRONOS itself, joint observations with other gravitational-wave detectors or related instruments, cross-correlation analyses, and characterization of correlated environmental noise will be essential. Prior to Phase 3, theoretical and experimental studies will therefore be required to identify the most effective detector combinations and to establish the necessary observing duration, frequency coverage, timing synchronization, data-sharing framework, and analysis methodology. Assessing the feasibility of a future joint-observation network is itself an important research objective for CHRONOS.

The second major scientific objective is the search for intermediate-mass black-hole (IMBH) binaries and the observational investigation of the intermediate-mass black-hole population
[
7
,
25
]
. The existence, abundance, and formation mechanisms of black holes between the stellar-mass and supermassive regimes remain incompletely understood. CHRONOS will approach this problem from a frequency band different from that of existing ground-based gravitational-wave observatories. By directly searching for gravitational waves from IMBH binaries, CHRONOS aims to provide observational evidence for this population and to place new constraints on black-hole formation histories and hierarchical growth scenarios.

Multiband observations with other gravitational-wave detectors may also provide important opportunities for IMBH science. If CHRONOS can observe the inspiral of an IMBH binary in the sub-Hz band before the signal enters higher-frequency detectors, combining these observations could improve source-parameter estimation and enable consistency tests of gravitational-wave propagation across widely separated frequency bands. The conditions required for such joint observations must therefore also be investigated in the context of future gravitational-wave detector networks.

These two major science targets provide key benchmarks for defining the sensitivity, long-term stability, calibration accuracy, observational duty cycle, and coordination requirements of the final CHRONOS configuration. The detailed scientific reach of each target is discussed separately in the corresponding science chapters.

Phase 3 therefore represents the culmination of the CHRONOS roadmap, integrating the technologies and understanding established through the principle demonstrations of Phase 1 and the low-frequency gravity science of Phase 2. At its final sensitivity, CHRONOS aims to open a new observational window for cosmological and astrophysical gravitational-wave science in the comparatively unexplored sub-Hz frequency band.

4.4
Evolution of the CHRONOS Program

The three phases should not be regarded as independent experiments. Instead,
they form a continuous progression in which each stage both delivers scientific
results and validates the technologies required for the next stage.

The principle-demonstration phase establishes the quantum measurement,
interferometric sensing, and control techniques. The gravity-gradient science
phase extends the experiment to precision measurements of real gravitational
fields and provides direct characterization of one of the dominant
environmental backgrounds in the sub-Hz band. These developments ultimately
converge in the gravitational-wave science phase, in which the full detector
concept is exploited for astrophysical and cosmological observations.

This staged strategy allows CHRONOS to produce scientifically meaningful results
throughout its development while progressively reducing the technical risk
associated with the final observatory. The roadmap therefore connects detector
development and scientific exploitation from the earliest prototype
measurements to the long-term goal of sub-Hz gravitational-wave astronomy.

Figure 4.1:

Science roadmap of the CHRONOS project.
The program is organized into three successive phases.
Phase 1 establishes the key measurement principles, including
speed-meter operation and quantum non-demolition measurements,
while also exploring new sensing concepts for dark-matter searches.
Phase 2 exploits the instrument for gravity-gradient science, including
earthquake-induced gravity perturbations, Newtonian-noise studies,
and laboratory searches for deviations from Newtonian gravity.
Phase 3 operates CHRONOS at its final sensitivity for sub-Hz
gravitational-wave observations, targeting stochastic gravitational-wave
backgrounds and intermediate-mass black-hole binaries.
Detailed science cases are discussed separately in the corresponding
chapters.

Chapter 5  Quantum measurement

The quantitative detector baseline is the full multi-input model in
Chap.
3
. The ideal speed-meter response used below
organizes the staged measurement tests; it does not replace the calibrated
transfer function or imply complete cancellation of the rear-port vacuum
back action. All comparisons to detector sensitivity use the reference
operating points and angle-to-strain convention of that chapter.

CHRONOS provides a unique platform for investigating quantum
measurement with a macroscopic torsional mechanical system.
In addition to its primary scientific objectives as a low-frequency
precision interferometer, the combination of a torsion-bar suspension,
a Sagnac speed-meter topology, radiation-pressure actuation, and
homodyne readout enables a systematic study of the quantum measurement
process itself.

The quantum measurement program of CHRONOS is organized into three
successive scientific stages.
Each stage addresses a distinct question in quantum measurement and,
at the same time, establishes the experimental capability required for
the next stage.

The first stage is the experimental demonstration of the

speed-meter response
.
The central question is whether the CHRONOS interferometer measures
the velocity-like observable predicted for the Sagnac speed-meter
topology, rather than simply behaving as a conventional position
meter.
This can be tested through controlled excitation of the torsion bar
and measurement of the complex transfer function of the
interferometer.
Importantly, this first demonstration does not require operation at
the quantum-noise limit and can therefore be performed during the
early commissioning phase of CHRONOS.

The second stage is the demonstration of

quantum non-demolition (QND) measurement
.
Once the speed-meter response has been established, the experiment
moves from characterization of the classical optical response to
measurement of quantum noise.
By varying the optical power, the measurement strength can be changed,
allowing the competition between measurement imprecision and
radiation-pressure back-action to be investigated.
Balanced homodyne detection provides an additional degree of freedom:
the measured optical quadrature can be varied through the homodyne
angle, enabling the correlation between measurement imprecision and
quantum back-action to be controlled.
The objective of this stage is to demonstrate that the speed-meter
topology provides measurable quantum back-action suppression or
back-action evasion relative to a conventional position measurement.

The third stage extends these measurements to a fundamental test of
quantum measurement theory through

Ozawa’s error–disturbance relation
.
In this stage, measurement imprecision and radiation-pressure
back-action are no longer treated only as contributions to the total
noise spectrum.
Instead, they are independently calibrated and interpreted as the
measurement error
\epsilon_{\theta}
and the measurement disturbance

\eta_{L}
of the torsional degree of freedom.
Here,
\theta
denotes the angular coordinate of the torsion bar and

L
denotes its conjugate angular momentum.
Together with the initial-state fluctuations
\sigma_{\theta}
and

\sigma_{L}
, these quantities allow a direct experimental evaluation
of the generalized error–disturbance relation.

For the canonical pair
(\theta,L)
, we define

C_{\theta L}\equiv\frac{1}{2}\left|\left\langle[\theta,L]\right\rangle\right|.

(5.1)

For an ideal canonical angular coordinate and angular momentum,

[\theta,L]=i\hbar
, and therefore
C_{\theta L}=\hbar/2
.
Using this scale, the conventional Heisenberg-type error–disturbance
relation can be written as

\epsilon_{\theta}\eta_{L}\geq C_{\theta L}.

(5.2)

We introduce the dimensionless Heisenberg indicator

\mathcal{H}\equiv\frac{\epsilon_{\theta}\eta_{L}}{C_{\theta L}}.

(5.3)

The conventional Heisenberg-type error–disturbance relation therefore
corresponds to

\mathcal{H}\geq 1.

(5.4)

Ozawa’s generalized error–disturbance relation is given by

\epsilon_{\theta}\eta_{L}+\epsilon_{\theta}\sigma_{L}+\sigma_{\theta}\eta_{L}\geq C_{\theta L}.

(5.5)

Correspondingly, we define the dimensionless Ozawa indicator as

\mathcal{O}\equiv\frac{\epsilon_{\theta}\eta_{L}+\epsilon_{\theta}\sigma_{L}+\sigma_{\theta}\eta_{L}}{C_{\theta L}}.

(5.6)

Ozawa’s generalized relation is therefore expressed simply as

\mathcal{O}\geq 1.

(5.7)

These two dimensionless quantities provide a convenient experimental
representation of the error–disturbance measurement.
In particular, the regime

\mathcal{H}<1,\qquad\mathcal{O}\geq 1

(5.8)

is of particular interest.
Observation of this regime would demonstrate that the simple
Heisenberg-type error–disturbance product is not universally valid,
while the generalized Ozawa relation remains satisfied.

By varying the optical power and homodyne angle, CHRONOS can explore a
controlled family of quantum measurements.
The optical power controls the measurement strength and hence the
balance between measurement imprecision and radiation-pressure
back-action, whereas the homodyne angle controls the measured optical
quadrature and the correlation between these two noise contributions.
As a result, the quantities
\epsilon_{\theta}
,
\eta_{L}
,

\sigma_{\theta}
, and
\sigma_{L}
, and consequently the indicators

\mathcal{H}
and
\mathcal{O}
, can be mapped over a controlled region
of the error–disturbance parameter space.

The three stages therefore form a progressive scientific roadmap,

\text{Speed-meter response}\,\longrightarrow\,\text{QND measurement}\,\longrightarrow\,\text{Ozawa's error--disturbance relation}.

(5.9)

This staged approach is important experimentally.
The first stage establishes the relevant mechanical and optical
transfer functions.
The second stage establishes control and measurement of quantum
back-action and its correlations with measurement imprecision.
The third stage builds on both capabilities to reconstruct the
measurement error and disturbance in absolute physical units and to
evaluate the dimensionless indicators
\mathcal{H}
and
\mathcal{O}
.
Thus, each stage provides not only an independent scientific result
but also the calibration and experimental foundation for the
subsequent stage.

The remainder of this section is organized as follows.
Section
5.1
describes the demonstration of the
speed-meter response.
Section
5.2
presents the strategy for demonstrating
QND measurement and quantum back-action suppression.
Section
5.3
describes the proposed test of Ozawa’s
error–disturbance relation and the experimental reconstruction of

\mathcal{H}
and
\mathcal{O}
.
Finally, Sec.
5.4
summarizes the key
technologies required to realize the complete quantum measurement
program.

5.1
Stage 1: Demonstration of the Speed-Meter Response

The first stage of the CHRONOS quantum measurement program is the
experimental demonstration of the speed-meter response.
The purpose of this stage is to establish that the interferometer
responds to the velocity-like observable expected from the Sagnac
speed-meter topology, rather than simply behaving as a conventional
position meter.

Importantly, Stage 1 is a classical transfer-function measurement.
Operation at the quantum-noise limit is not required.
The experiment can therefore be performed during the early
commissioning phase using the torsion-bar suspension, the coil-magnet
actuator, and a mid-fringe interferometer lock.

For the torsional degree of freedom, let
\theta(\Omega)
denote the
Fourier amplitude of the calibrated angular coordinate in radians,
and let
\Omega=2\pi f
for frequency
f
in hertz. For the main science
channel this coordinate is the differential bar angle; an individual-bar
measurement must be identified separately.
The interferometer readout is represented by the error signal

d_{\rm err}(\Omega)
, which is the experimentally recorded sensing
channel in digital counts. This chapter’s
Sensing Function

H_{\theta\rightarrow d_{\rm err}}
is the angle-to-error-signal response,
in counts/rad, evaluated for a deterministic small signal. If feedback
is active, its effect must be removed or included explicitly in the model.
We define this complex transfer function as

H_{\theta\rightarrow d_{\rm err}}(\Omega)\equiv\frac{d_{\rm err}(\Omega)}{\theta(\Omega)}.

(5.10)

The superscripts PM and SM denote position-meter and speed-meter responses,
respectively. The PM optical/readout gain
G_{\rm opt}^{\rm PM}
maps
angle to error counts. For a conventional position meter,

H_{\theta\rightarrow d_{\rm err}}^{\rm PM}(\Omega)=G_{\rm opt}^{\rm PM}(\Omega),

(5.11)

where
G_{\rm opt}^{\rm PM}(\Omega)
represents the optical sensing
gain from angular displacement to the measured error signal.
In the frequency region well below the characteristic optical
frequencies, the position-meter response is approximately constant,

H_{\theta\rightarrow d_{\rm err}}^{\rm PM}(\Omega)\simeq{\rm const.}

(5.12)

In a speed meter, the same mechanical degree of freedom is sampled at
two different times separated by an effective optical delay
\tau
.
The measured signal is therefore proportional to the difference
between two successive measurements of the angular displacement,

\theta(t+\tau)-\theta(t).

(5.13)

Here,
\tau
should be understood as the effective delay associated
with the Sagnac optical response, rather than simply as a single-pass
light-travel time.

We define the speed-meter response function as

H^{\rm SM}(\Omega)\equiv e^{-i\Omega\tau}-1.

(5.14)

Let
G_{\rm opt}
denote the remaining optical and readout-chain response
after factoring out the dimensionless delay response
H^{\rm SM}
.
The angle-to-error-signal transfer function can then be written as

H_{\theta\rightarrow d_{\rm err}}(\Omega)=G_{\rm opt}(\Omega)H^{\rm SM}(\Omega),

(5.15)

where
G_{\rm opt}(\Omega)
contains the remaining optical sensing
response of the interferometer, including any frequency dependence
associated with the optical cavities, recycling configuration, and
readout chain.

Therefore, the measured error signal is

d_{\rm err}(\Omega)=G_{\rm opt}(\Omega)H^{\rm SM}(\Omega)\theta(\Omega).

(5.16)

In the low-frequency limit,

\Omega\tau\ll 1,

(5.17)

the speed-meter response becomes

H^{\rm SM}(\Omega)=e^{-i\Omega\tau}-1\simeq-i\Omega\tau.

(5.18)

Consequently,

H_{\theta\rightarrow d_{\rm err}}(\Omega)\simeq-i\Omega\tau G_{\rm opt}(\Omega).

(5.19)

If the optical response
G_{\rm opt}(\Omega)
varies slowly over the
measurement band, the characteristic low-frequency behavior is
therefore

\left|H_{\theta\rightarrow d_{\rm err}}(\Omega)\right|\propto\Omega,

(5.20)

with a phase shift corresponding to the time derivative of the angular
displacement.

This relation provides the direct connection between the theoretical
speed-meter response
H^{\rm SM}(\Omega)
and the experimentally
measured transfer function

H_{\theta\rightarrow d_{\rm err}}(\Omega)
.
In practice, the identification of the speed-meter response should be
based on a comparison of the full complex transfer function, including
both its amplitude and phase, with the complete optical model rather
than solely on the asymptotic

|H_{\theta\rightarrow d_{\rm err}}|\propto\Omega
behavior.

5.1.1
Transfer-Function Measurement with the Coil Actuator

The speed-meter response is measured by applying a controlled
sinusoidal excitation to the torsion bar using the coil-magnet
actuator.

We denote the digital control signal sent to the coil actuator by

d_{\rm ctrl}(\Omega)
.
The actuator transfer function
H_{\rm coil}(\Omega)
converts this
digital control signal into an applied torque,

\tau_{\rm coil}(\Omega)=H_{\rm coil}(\Omega)d_{\rm ctrl}(\Omega).

(5.21)

The torsion-bar motion is

\theta(\Omega)=\chi_{\theta}(\Omega)\tau_{\rm coil}(\Omega),

(5.22)

where
\chi_{\theta}(\Omega)
is the mechanical angular susceptibility
of the torsional degree of freedom.

The measured interferometer error signal is therefore

\displaystyle d_{\rm err}(\Omega)

\displaystyle=H_{\theta\rightarrow d_{\rm err}}(\Omega)\theta(\Omega)

\displaystyle=G_{\rm opt}(\Omega)H^{\rm SM}(\Omega)\chi_{\theta}(\Omega)H_{\rm coil}(\Omega)d_{\rm ctrl}(\Omega).

(5.23)

Thus, the directly measured control-to-error transfer function is

H_{d_{\rm ctrl}\rightarrow d_{\rm err}}(\Omega)\equiv\frac{d_{\rm err}(\Omega)}{d_{\rm ctrl}(\Omega)}=G_{\rm opt}(\Omega)H^{\rm SM}(\Omega)\chi_{\theta}(\Omega)H_{\rm coil}(\Omega).

(5.24)

After removing the independently characterized actuator response and
the mechanical susceptibility,

H_{\theta\rightarrow d_{\rm err}}(\Omega)=\frac{H_{d_{\rm ctrl}\rightarrow d_{\rm err}}(\Omega)}{\chi_{\theta}(\Omega)H_{\rm coil}(\Omega)},

(5.25)

the experimentally reconstructed optical response can be directly
compared with the model

H_{\theta\rightarrow d_{\rm err}}^{\rm model}(\Omega)=G_{\rm opt}(\Omega)H^{\rm SM}(\Omega).

(5.26)

The essential test of Stage 1 is therefore whether the reconstructed

H_{\theta\rightarrow d_{\rm err}}(\Omega)
contains the
characteristic speed-meter factor

H^{\rm SM}(\Omega)=e^{-i\Omega\tau}-1,

(5.27)

in both amplitude and phase.

At low frequencies, the speed-meter response behaves as

H_{\theta\rightarrow d_{\rm err}}^{\rm SM}(\Omega)\propto-i\Omega,

(5.28)

whereas a conventional position meter approaches

H_{\theta\rightarrow d_{\rm err}}^{\rm PM}(\Omega)\propto{\rm const.}

(5.29)

The distinct frequency dependence and phase evolution of these two
responses provide the principal experimental signature of the
speed-meter behavior.
A successful Stage 1 demonstration will therefore require agreement
between the measured and modeled complex transfer functions over a
finite frequency band, rather than identification of a single
frequency point or amplitude scaling alone.

5.2
Stage 2: Demonstration of QND Measurement

Stage 2 aims to demonstrate quantum non-demolition (QND) measurement using the CHRONOS Sagnac speed-meter topology. Following the classical characterization of the speed-meter response in Stage 1, Stage 2 focuses on the quantum measurement process, in particular the suppression of radiation-pressure back-action relative to a conventional position measurement.

5.2.1
Quantum-Noise Measurement

The interferometer is operated near the dark fringe, with balanced homodyne detection (BHD) providing the primary science readout. The calibrated torsional observable is written as

\theta_{\rm meas}=\theta_{\rm sig}+\theta_{\rm imp}+\chi_{\theta}\tau_{\rm BA},

(5.30)

where
\theta_{\rm imp}
is the measurement-imprecision noise,
\tau_{\rm BA}
is the radiation-pressure back-action torque, and
\chi_{\theta}
is the mechanical susceptibility of the torsional degree of freedom.

The corresponding quantum-noise spectrum is

S_{\theta}^{\rm quant}=S_{\theta}^{\rm imp}+|\chi_{\theta}|^{2}S_{\tau}^{\rm BA}+2\,{\rm Re}\left[\chi_{\theta}S_{\theta\tau}\right],

(5.31)

where
S_{\theta\tau}
is the imprecision–back-action cross-spectral density. In conventional interferometer terminology, these three terms correspond to shot noise, radiation-pressure noise, and their correlation, respectively.

For an uncorrelated position measurement,
S_{\theta\tau}=0
. In a homodyne measurement, however, the correlation can be controlled through the detected optical quadrature and therefore constitutes an essential element of the QND measurement.

5.2.2
Power and Homodyne-Angle Scans

Stage 2 uses the optical power
P
and homodyne angle
\zeta
as two independent experimental control parameters. The optical quadrature detected by the BHD is

\hat{b}_{\zeta}=\hat{b}_{1}\cos\zeta+\hat{b}_{2}\sin\zeta.

(5.32)

Varying
\zeta
changes the detected quadrature and hence the correlation between measurement imprecision and radiation-pressure back-action.

The optical power controls the measurement strength. In the quantum-noise-dominated regime,

\sqrt{S_{\theta}^{\rm imp}}\propto P^{-1/2},\qquad\sqrt{S_{\tau}^{\rm BA}}\propto P^{1/2},

(5.33)

and therefore

\sqrt{S_{\theta}^{\rm RP}}=|\chi_{\theta}|\sqrt{S_{\tau}^{\rm BA}}\propto|\chi_{\theta}|P^{1/2}.

(5.34)

The measurement is performed over a set of optical powers and homodyne angles,

P=P_{1},\ldots,P_{N},\qquad\zeta=\zeta_{1},\ldots,\zeta_{M},

(5.35)

yielding the two-dimensional data set

S_{\theta}(\Omega;P,\zeta).

(5.36)

The power scan separates contributions according to their measurement-strength dependence: the imprecision contribution decreases with increasing power, whereas radiation-pressure back-action increases. The homodyne-angle scan provides an independent handle on the imprecision–back-action correlation through the measured optical quadrature. Technical noise that is approximately independent of
P
and
\zeta
can thereby be distinguished from these quantum-noise contributions.

The full data set is simultaneously compared with

\displaystyle S_{\theta}^{\rm model}(\Omega;P,\zeta)

\displaystyle=S_{\theta}^{\rm imp}(\Omega;P,\zeta)+|\chi_{\theta}(\Omega)|^{2}S_{\tau}^{\rm BA}(\Omega;P)

\displaystyle\quad+2\,{\rm Re}\left[\chi_{\theta}(\Omega)S_{\theta\tau}(\Omega;P,\zeta)\right].

(5.37)

A global fit over the
(P,\zeta)
parameter space therefore constrains the measurement imprecision, radiation-pressure back-action, and their correlation within a single quantum-measurement model.

5.2.3
QND Signature and Success Criterion

The principal QND signature in Stage 2 is the suppression of radiation-pressure back-action by the Sagnac speed-meter topology. For a conventional position meter,

S_{\theta}^{\rm shot}\propto P^{-1},\qquad S_{\theta}^{\rm RP}\propto P,

(5.38)

which gives rise to the conventional quantum measurement trade-off. In a speed meter, sequential sampling of the mechanical degree of freedom suppresses the low-frequency coupling of radiation-pressure back-action to the measured observable.

The most direct test would be to operate the same apparatus in both speed-meter and position-meter configurations. However, implementing an equivalent position-meter configuration in CHRONOS would require substantial modifications to the optical layout and control system. The baseline Stage 2 test therefore compares the experimentally inferred speed-meter back-action,

S_{\theta}^{\rm RP,SM}(\Omega),

(5.39)

with the prediction of a calibrated conventional position-meter model,

S_{\theta}^{\rm RP,PM,model}(\Omega),

(5.40)

whose optical and mechanical parameters are constrained by independent measurements.

Stage 2 is considered successful when the measured spectra across the
(P,\zeta)
parameter space are consistently described by the quantum-noise model and the inferred radiation-pressure back-action is suppressed or coherently evaded relative to the calibrated position-meter prediction.

Observation of

S_{\theta}^{\rm SM}(\Omega)<S_{\theta}^{\rm SQL}(\Omega)

(5.41)

over a finite frequency band would provide a stronger demonstration of QND performance by surpassing the conventional position-meter standard quantum limit. However, sub-SQL operation is not adopted as a necessary condition for the Stage 2 milestone.

The measurement imprecision, back-action, and their correlation calibrated through the
(P,\zeta)
scans provide the experimental basis for Stage 3, where they are quantitatively interpreted as the measurement error
\epsilon_{\theta}
and disturbance
\eta_{L}
and used to evaluate the Heisenberg and Ozawa indicators
\mathcal{H}
and
\mathcal{O}
.

5.3
Stage 3: Test of Ozawa’s Error–Disturbance Relation

The third stage of the CHRONOS quantum-measurement program aims at a
quantitative test of Ozawa’s generalized error–disturbance relation
using the torsional degree of freedom as a macroscopic continuous
quantum-measurement system.

For the angular coordinate
\theta
and its canonically conjugate
angular momentum
L
,

[\theta,L]=i\hbar.

(5.42)

For an experimentally defined measurement mode, we introduce

C_{\theta L}\equiv\frac{1}{2}\left|\left\langle[\Theta_{w},L_{w}]\right\rangle\right|,

(5.43)

which reduces to

C_{\theta L}=\frac{\hbar}{2}

(5.44)

for a canonically normalized mode.

The conventional Heisenberg-type error–disturbance product is
characterized by

\mathcal{H}\equiv\frac{\epsilon_{\theta}\eta_{L}}{C_{\theta L}},

(5.45)

where
\epsilon_{\theta}
is the measurement error of the angular
coordinate and
\eta_{L}
is the measurement-induced disturbance of the
angular momentum.

Ozawa’s generalized relation additionally depends on the intrinsic
fluctuations of the initial mechanical state,

\sigma_{\theta}
and
\sigma_{L}
,
and can be written as

\mathcal{O}\equiv\frac{\epsilon_{\theta}\eta_{L}+\epsilon_{\theta}\sigma_{L}+\sigma_{\theta}\eta_{L}}{C_{\theta L}}.

(5.46)

The conventional Heisenberg-type relation corresponds to

\mathcal{H}\geq 1,

(5.47)

whereas Ozawa’s relation requires

\mathcal{O}\geq 1.

(5.48)

The scientifically distinctive target is therefore the regime

\mathcal{H}<1,\qquad\mathcal{O}\geq 1.

(5.49)

Stage 2 provides the experimental basis for this program by measuring
and characterizing measurement imprecision, radiation-pressure
back-action, and their correlation.
Stage 3 extends this framework by additionally requiring a calibrated
determination of the intrinsic fluctuations of the mechanical state.

A complete experimental implementation therefore requires reliable
estimates of

\epsilon_{\theta},\qquad\eta_{L},\qquad\sigma_{\theta},\qquad\sigma_{L},

(5.50)

together with their statistical and calibration uncertainties.

The detailed methodology for reconstructing these quantities,
particularly the initial-state variances

\sigma_{\theta}
and
\sigma_{L}
in the presence of active feedback and
quantum back-action, is currently under investigation.
Possible approaches include calibrated time-domain reconstruction,
spectral decomposition using controlled changes of the measurement
strength and readout quadrature, and independent reconstruction of the
mechanical state from multiple sensing and control channels.

At the present stage, CHRONOS therefore defines the Stage 3 goal at the
level of the experimentally testable error–disturbance relation,
while leaving the detailed state-reconstruction and estimation
procedure for future dedicated studies.

The development of this methodology will form part of the transition
from the Stage 2 QND experiment to a quantitative test of generalized
quantum measurement theory.

5.4
Required Technologies and Minimum Implementation Path

The CHRONOS quantum-measurement program is designed as a staged
experimental development.
Each stage adds only the technologies required for the corresponding
scientific milestone.

The minimum progression is

\displaystyle\text{Stage~1: Speed-meter response}

\displaystyle\longrightarrow\text{Stage~2: QND measurement}

(5.51)

\displaystyle\longrightarrow\text{Stage~3: Error--disturbance test}.

The detailed measurement procedures are described in the preceding
sections.
Here we summarize only the additional experimental capabilities
required at each stage.

5.4.1
Stage 1 Requirements

Stage 1 requires the minimum system needed to measure the classical
speed-meter response.

The essential technologies are

1.

a torsion-bar suspension with a reproducible angular
response;

2.

a Sagnac or equivalent two-time optical interaction with the
same torsional degree of freedom;

3.

a coil-magnet actuator for controlled excitation;

4.

a linear interferometric readout, such as a mid-fringe
sensing configuration;

5.

digital excitation and acquisition of

d_{\rm ctrl}
and
d_{\rm err}
for complex transfer-function
measurements.

Quantum-noise-limited operation, balanced homodyne detection, absolute
torque calibration, and state reconstruction are not required at this
stage.

5.4.2
Stage 2 Requirements

Stage 2 adds the technologies required to measure and separate quantum
noise and to demonstrate suppression of radiation-pressure
back-action.

The additional capabilities are

1.

stable dark-fringe operation;

2.

balanced homodyne detection of the science output;

3.

calibrated control of the optical power
P
;

4.

calibrated control of the homodyne angle
\zeta
;

5.

sufficient optomechanical coupling to resolve
radiation-pressure back-action;

6.

calibrated angular reconstruction of the BHD science
channel;

7.

acquisition and analysis of auto- and cross-spectral
densities over multiple
(P,\zeta)
configurations.

Resonant optical enhancement and power recycling are introduced when
required to achieve sufficient circulating power and quantum
sensitivity.
Signal recycling remains optional and is introduced only if
frequency-dependent shaping of the optical response is needed.

A direct conversion of the CHRONOS interferometer to a conventional
position-meter configuration is not required.
The baseline comparison is made with a calibrated position-meter model
constrained by independently measured CHRONOS parameters.

5.4.3
Stage 3 Requirements

Stage 3 adds the capabilities required to convert the Stage 2
quantum-noise measurements into the absolute error–disturbance
quantities

\epsilon_{\theta}
,
\eta_{L}
,
\sigma_{\theta}
, and
\sigma_{L}
.

The additional technologies are

1.

absolute calibration of the BHD angular response;

2.

PCAL calibration of the radiation-pressure torque;

3.

GCAL cross-calibration of the low-frequency torque scale;

4.

calibrated reconstruction of the torsion-bar angular
displacement
\theta(t)
and angular velocity
\dot{\theta}(t)
;

5.

two independent reconstruction pipelines for the mechanical
state;

6.

cross-spectral and coherence validation of the two
reconstructed signals;

7.

propagation of calibration, statistical, and model
uncertainties into
\mathcal{H}
and
\mathcal{O}
.

The detailed reconstruction of

\epsilon_{\theta}
,
\eta_{L}
,
\sigma_{\theta}
, and
\sigma_{L}

is described in Sec.
5.3
.
The essential additional capability of Stage 3 is therefore absolute
calibration and independently validated reconstruction of the
torsional mechanical state.

5.4.4
Technology Progression

The staged implementation can be summarized as

\displaystyle\text{Stage~1:}

\displaystyle\text{torsion bar}+\text{two-time optical interaction}+\text{coil actuation}+d_{\rm ctrl}/d_{\rm err}

(5.52)

\displaystyle\longrightarrow\quad\text{Stage~2:}

\displaystyle\text{dark fringe}+\text{BHD}+P\text{ scan}+\zeta\text{ scan}+\text{quantum-noise decomposition}

\displaystyle\longrightarrow\quad\text{Stage~3:}

\displaystyle\text{PCAL/GCAL}+\text{two reconstruction pipelines}+\text{absolute calibration}

\displaystyle\longrightarrow\mathcal{H},\mathcal{O}.

In this staged strategy, Stage 1 establishes the speed-meter response,
