Chapter 6  Dark-Matter Searches

6.1
Scope and present objective

The technologies developed for gravitational-wave observatories provide a broad
platform for dark-matter searches. Suspended test masses, long-baseline laser
interferometry, low-noise angular readout, stabilized lasers, optical cavities,
polarization optics, and continuously recorded auxiliary channels can respond
not only to gravitational waves, but also to weak forces and changes in the
properties of matter or light. Existing interferometers have already been used
to search directly for scalar, vector, and tensor ultralight dark matter

[
33
,
34
,
35
]
, demonstrating
that the use of gravitational-wave instrumentation as a dark-matter detector is
experimentally well motivated.

This chapter does not present a projected sensitivity, an exclusion curve, or
a discovery reach for a particular dark-matter model. It summarizes the
general detection principles used in conventional precision laser
interferometers: which physical quantity is perturbed, how that perturbation is
converted into an interferometric observable, what temporal signature is
expected, and which detector symmetries can suppress the response. How these
principles might be implemented in CHRONOS is not yet fixed and is therefore
outside the present scope. No particular CHRONOS degree of freedom, optical
port, readout scheme, or temporary optical configuration is assumed.

This distinction is important. A gravitational-wave detector does not respond
to a particle label by itself. It responds to displacement, velocity, torque,
optical phase, polarization, refractive index, laser frequency, or a transient
gravitational field. The first task is therefore to map a dark-matter
interaction into generalized mechanical or optical coordinates of a
conventional interferometric detector.

6.2
Generic signal description

Ultralight bosonic dark matter with a large occupation number may be treated as
a classical field. Let
X_{0}
be the amplitude of a local field component

X
,
m_{X}
its particle mass,
\omega_{X}
its angular frequency,
\bm{k}_{X}

its wave vector, and
\varphi_{X}
its phase. Here
t
is time,
\bm{x}

position,
c
the speed of light, and
\hbar
the reduced Planck constant.
A local component can be represented schematically as

X(t,\bm{x})=X_{0}\cos\!\left(\omega_{X}t-\bm{k}_{X}\!\cdot\!\bm{x}+\varphi_{X}\right),\qquad\omega_{X}\simeq\frac{m_{X}c^{2}}{\hbar}.

(6.1)

The Galactic velocity distribution gives the signal a finite linewidth and a
finite coherence time. The laboratory motion through the Galactic field can
also produce daily and annual changes in the amplitude, phase, or apparent
direction. The corresponding experimental target is therefore generally a
narrow spectral feature with a predictable coherence structure rather than an
exactly monochromatic line.
Writing
f=\omega_{X}/(2\pi)
and
\Delta f
for the signal linewidth,
for a virialized population with velocity dispersion
v\sim 10^{-3}c
,

\Delta f/f\sim 10^{-6}
and the coherence time is of order
1/\Delta f

(up to convention-dependent factors of
2\pi
)
[
36
]
.
The conversion
m_{X}c^{2}=4.1357\times 10^{-15}~\mathrm{eV}\,(f/\mathrm{Hz})

maps 0.1–10 Hz to
4.14\times 10^{-16}
–
4.14\times 10^{-14}
eV.
This identifies an oscillation-frequency interval, not a coupling sensitivity.
The field amplitude also depends on the fraction of the local dark-matter
density carried by the candidate; a halo model and density assumption must
therefore accompany any eventual limit.

For a mechanical detector, the response may be written in the frequency domain
as

\bm{q}(f)=\bm{H}_{\mathrm{mech}}(f)\,\bm{F}_{\mathrm{DM}}(f),

(6.2)

where
\bm{q}
contains the translational and rotational coordinates,

\bm{H}_{\mathrm{mech}}
is the calibrated mechanical response matrix, and

\bm{F}_{\mathrm{DM}}
denotes generalized dark-matter forces and torques. The
response in an arbitrary detector coordinate can then be expressed as

d_{I}(f)=\bm{R}_{I}^{\mathrm{mech}}(f)\bm{q}(f)+d_{I}^{\mathrm{mat}}(f)+d_{I}^{\mathrm{pol}}(f)+n_{I}(f),

(6.3)

where
I
labels any accessible detector coordinate,

\bm{R}_{I}^{\mathrm{mech}}
maps motion into that coordinate,

d_{I}^{\mathrm{mat}}
describes dimensional or refractive-index changes,

d_{I}^{\mathrm{pol}}
describes polarization-dependent propagation, and

n_{I}
is instrumental noise. Equations
(
6.2
) and (
6.3
), rather
than a sensitivity curve, define the present feasibility problem.

Macroscopic dark-matter structures instead produce transient signals. A domain
wall may cause a temporary variation of fundamental constants, while a compact
clump passing near the instrument may accelerate the suspended masses through
gravity or an additional long-range interaction. The event duration is set by
the size and velocity of the structure, and its trajectory determines the
relative arrival times and amplitudes in spatially separated sensors.

6.3
Resonant and narrowband searches

A complementary strategy is to match the dark-matter oscillation frequency to
a mechanical, acoustic, optical, or polarization resonance. Since the field
frequency
f_{\rm DM}
is related to its particle mass
m_{\rm DM}
by
using the Planck constant
h_{\rm P}=2\pi\hbar
(distinct from GW strain),

f_{\mathrm{DM}}=\frac{m_{\mathrm{DM}}c^{2}}{h_{\rm P}},

(6.4)

each resonance defines a narrow dark-matter mass window. Resonant searches are
therefore naturally suited to coherent ultralight fields, while broadband
transient searches remain more appropriate for domain walls and compact
clumps.

For a torsional mode with moment of inertia
I
, resonant angular frequency

\omega_{0}
, and mechanical quality factor
Q_{\mathrm{m}}
, the response to
a dark-matter torque
\tau_{\rm DM}
is described by the angular
susceptibility
\chi_{\tau}
(angle per unit torque). For Fourier angular
frequency
\omega=2\pi f
, the response is

\theta(\omega)=\chi_{\tau}(\omega)\tau_{\mathrm{DM}}(\omega),\qquad\chi_{\tau}(\omega)=\frac{1}{I\left(\omega_{0}^{2}-\omega^{2}+i\omega\omega_{0}/Q_{\mathrm{m}}\right)}.

(6.5)

At resonance, the angular response to a coherent torque is enhanced by the
mechanical quality factor,

\left|\chi_{\tau}(\omega_{0})\right|=\frac{Q_{\mathrm{m}}}{I\omega_{0}^{2}}.

(6.6)

The corresponding translational response is obtained by replacing
I
with
the effective mass of the mode. Thus any translational, rotational, or
internal elastic mode with nonzero overlap with the dark-matter generalized
force is a potential narrowband sensor. The relevant condition is mode
overlap, rather than the operational name assigned to an interferometer degree
of freedom.

The full steady-state gain requires coherent driving for a time long compared
with the amplitude ring-up time
2Q_{\rm m}/\omega_{0}
. If the field coherence
time or the dwell time at a tuning is shorter, the finite-duration response
must be calculated instead. A virialized field has

Q_{\rm DM}\equiv f_{\rm DM}/\Delta f\sim(c/v)^{2}\sim 10^{6}
; a substantially narrower mechanical
resonance samples only part of its spectrum
[
36
]
.
The resonance frequency and quality factor must remain stable over the search.

Resonant enhancement does not automatically multiply the final force
sensitivity by
Q_{\mathrm{m}}
. The signal displacement is enhanced near
resonance, which is valuable when optical readout noise is important, but the
thermal displacement noise is filtered by the same mechanical susceptibility.
In the thermal-force-limited regime, the advantage comes from low mechanical
dissipation, cryogenic operation, and long coherent integration rather than
from displacement amplification alone. Resonant searches must therefore be
formulated in calibrated force or torque units as well as in displacement or
angle.

Several established response concepts illustrate this principle.
The torsional resonance of a composition-asymmetric bar can amplify the
oscillating torque from vector dark matter. A differential optomechanical
resonator made from materials with different effective charges can similarly
search for vector forces
[
36
]
. Longitudinal or
breathing modes of a test mass, resonant bar, suspension element, or reference
cavity can respond to scalar-induced strain; the AURIGA resonant-mass detector
has already been used for such a scalar search
[
37
]
. These
internal modes extend the science case beyond the low-frequency rigid-body
modes normally used for gravitational-wave observation.

Optical resonance provides a separate form of enhancement. A resonant optical
system can amplify a field-induced phase or polarization perturbation when its
eigenfrequency structure is matched to the signal. For axion-like dark matter,
the frequency splitting between two polarization eigenmodes can be tuned so
that axion-induced conversion from the pumped polarization to the orthogonal
polarization is resonant
[
38
]
. This principle
depends on the polarization eigenmode splitting and is distinct from ordinary
carrier resonance; its practical excitation and readout are left unspecified.

A resonant search need not be restricted to one fixed natural frequency. In
principle the eigenfrequency may be varied through mechanical or dynamical
stiffness, or several mechanical, acoustic, optical, or polarization modes may
be combined. Such tuning changes the response function but does not create
fundamental force sensitivity; additional dissipation and fluctuations must be
included in a concrete realization.

If a resonant coordinate is dynamically modified or actively controlled, its
physical susceptibility must be replaced by the corresponding effective
closed-loop susceptibility. Information about an external generalized force
is then distributed between physical motion and applied control action. The
invariant object is the external force or torque inferred from a complete
dynamical model, independently of a particular sensing or actuation scheme.

6.4
Laser-interferometer detection principles

6.4.1
Scalar and dilaton dark matter

A coherently oscillating scalar field may appear macroscopically as a temporal
variation of material dimensions, refractive index, elastic properties, or a
material-referenced optical frequency. An optical system converts these
changes into phase only to the
extent that distinct propagation segments or materials have unequal response
coefficients. Finite optical thickness and path asymmetry are examples of the
general mechanism by which common material expansion becomes differential
phase
[
39
,
33
]
; comparisons among dissimilar
material or geometric responses provide the same principle

[
40
]
.

The scalar response must be evaluated for the complete optical system. An
identical fractional change of every relevant length and optical property may
be rejected as common mode, while unequal coefficients, finite propagation
times, and repeated sampling can create differential or frequency-dependent
responses. The fundamental calculation is therefore to propagate the
scalar-induced perturbation of every transmissive and reflective component
through the optical transfer function. This statement does not privilege a
particular output: any accessible coordinate with nonzero overlap may carry the
information.

6.4.2
Vector and dark-photon dark matter

Ultralight vector fields are candidates for ultralight dark matter.
They can be regarded as gauge bosons of a
U(1)_{D}
gauge symmetry, where
D
denotes a charge such as
B
(baryon number) or
B-L
(baryon minus lepton number).
We consider vector dark matter coupled to the matter current through the interaction
\epsilon_{D}eJ^{\mu}_{D}A_{\mu}
, where
\epsilon_{D}
is the dimensionless coupling relative to the electromagnetic coupling
e
,
J_{D}^{\mu}
is the matter four-current carrying charge
D
, and
A_{\mu}
is the vector field. Greek indices label spacetime components and repeated indices are contracted. In this interaction expression we use natural units,
\hbar=c=1
. For vector-particle mass
m_{A}
, the field oscillates at
f=m_{A}c^{2}/h_{\rm P}
when SI units are restored, and induces a fifth force on test masses, resulting in a tiny oscillatory displacement of the mirrors in an optical cavity
[
41
,
42
]
.
For electrically neutral atoms, the
B-L
charge is the neutron number;
the relevant contrast is charge per unit mass. These gauged-matter-current
models are distinct from a dark photon coupled only through electromagnetic
kinetic mixing. In particular,
U(1)_{B}
and
U(1)_{B-L}
gauge bosons have been searched for using observational data from aLIGO, Virgo, and KAGRA
[
43
,
44
]
.

For body element
a
, let
\bm{r}_{a}
be its position relative to the bar
center of mass and
\bm{F}_{a}^{\rm DM}
the force exerted by dark matter.
The corresponding generalized torque
\bm{\tau}_{\rm DM}
is

\bm{\tau}_{\mathrm{DM}}(t)=\sum_{a}\bm{r}_{a}\times\bm{F}_{a}^{\mathrm{DM}}(t).

(6.7)

Equation (
6.7
) also exposes a central feasibility condition. A
spatially uniform force proportional to mass has zero torque about the center
of mass, including for a geometrically asymmetric freely suspended bar.
A torque requires a dipole of effective charge relative to that center,
a field gradient, or a nontrivial suspension response. Composition contrast
can produce such a charge dipole. Mass or geometric asymmetry alone does not
evade the cancellation for a uniform mass-proportional force. Translational
and rotational coordinates must therefore be modeled separately.

6.4.3
ALP dark matter

Axion-like particles (ALPs) arise in many extensions of the Standard Model,
including string-motivated scenarios
[
45
,
46
]
.
Let
a
be the ALP field,
m_{a}
its particle mass,
g_{a\gamma}
the
ALP–photon coupling,
F_{\mu\nu}
the electromagnetic field-strength
tensor, and
\widetilde{F}^{\mu\nu}
its dual. A possible interaction
Lagrangian density is

\mathcal{L}_{a\gamma}=g_{a\gamma}aF_{\mu\nu}\widetilde{F}^{\mu\nu}/4

in natural units. Its strength is model dependent. An oscillating ALP field
can produce opposite phase shifts for the two circular polarizations and
therefore an oscillating rotation of linear polarization, with SI frequency

f=m_{a}c^{2}/h_{\rm P}

[
47
,
48
,
49
]
.
Figure
6.1
illustrates a cavity polarimetry concept.
The signal depends on the complete polarization transfer function, including
mirror birefringence, propagation delays, eigenmode splitting, and losses.

Ring and linear cavities provide different polarization responses; neither
geometry is uniformly superior at every axion mass. The DANCE experiment
demonstrated a cavity search without an external magnetic field and reported
no significant signal in its first 24-hour data set
[
50
]
.
LIDA and ADBC provide further experimental examples

[
51
,
52
]
. A 2025 KAGRA report describes installation
of polarization optics for an axion search
[
53
]
.
These results motivate a CHRONOS feasibility study; a projected CHRONOS
axion limit still requires a specific optical response and a measured
polarization-noise budget.

Figure 6.1:

Schematic diagram of a dark matter experiment. FI: Faraday isolator; M1(2): mirror with reflectivity
r_{1(2)}
and transmittivity
t_{1(2)}
;
\bm{E}_{\rm cav}
: electric field inside the cavity at the reflection mirror M1; HWP: half-wave plate; PBS: polarizing beam splitter; BD: beam dump; PD: photodetector. The polarization plane of the incident laser rotates inside the resonant cavity due to the interaction between dark matter and photons. The laser polarization is separated by a polarizing beam splitter, and the new polarization state induced by dark matter is measured at the detection port near the cavity reflection mirror (a) or transmission mirror (b). This figure is adapted from
[
54
]
.

6.4.4
Ultralight tensor fields

A coherent massive tensor field can produce an effective oscillating strain or
a differential force with a polarization pattern distinct from ordinary scalar
or vector interactions. Detector coordinates with different spatial
orientations provide complementary projections of the tensor structure.
Recent multi-model interferometer analyses demonstrate that scalar, vector,
and tensor hypotheses can be tested with separate response models

[
35
]
.

The experimental challenge is model discrimination. A tensor dark-matter line
can resemble a persistent gravitational-wave signal in a single channel. Its
finite linewidth, spatial coherence, polarization response, daily modulation,
and consistency among differently oriented detectors must therefore be included
in the analysis. A network can strengthen signal discrimination and test spatial coherence;
a single-detector search is also possible with an explicit response and
background model.

6.4.5
Topological defects and transient variations of constants

Scalar fields may form domain walls, strings, or other macroscopic structures.
Their passage through the apparatus can temporarily change material dimensions,
refractive indices, clock frequencies, or forces on test masses

[
39
]
. Such a signal would appear as a transient with a
duration related to the defect thickness and Galactic transit speed. The same
event could be observed sequentially by interferometers, optical cavities,
atomic clocks, or other precision sensors.

The response principle is coherent comparison of spatially separated detector
coordinates. A distributed network can test whether relative arrival times
and amplitudes are compatible with passage of a common structure. Because
local disturbances can also produce short transients, consistency with a
physically allowed propagation pattern and with independent environmental
information is essential to the dark-matter hypothesis test.

6.4.6
Compact clumps and macroscopic dark matter

A compact dark-matter clump or primordial compact object passing near a detector
can accelerate its test masses. The leading observable is generally the
differential Newtonian acceleration; the gravitational time delay of the light
path may provide an additional contribution
[
55
,
56
]
. The same trajectory may also produce a
body-dependent force in models with an additional long-range interaction.

Any coordinate sensitive to relative translation, rotation, or a local
gravitational reference may respond. A spatially uniform acceleration is
rejected by a differential detector, whereas the field gradient
across the baseline or torsion bar remains observable. The signal model must
therefore include the clump trajectory, the three-dimensional detector geometry,
and the suspension response. Candidate events should be tested against
earthquakes, anthropogenic impulses, magnetic disturbances, and ordinary
interferometer glitches.

6.4.7
Coherent scattering and dark-matter wind

An exploratory possibility is the coherent transfer of momentum from a flux of
light dark-matter particles to macroscopic test bodies. If two bodies have
different coherent-scattering form factors, their accelerations can differ and
produce a measurable torsional signal. Proposed torsion-balance concepts use
equal-mass test bodies with deliberately different geometries to create this
difference
[
57
]
. Similarly, different compositions
can probe interactions whose effective charge-to-mass ratios are not universal.

The response is nonzero only when the bodies differ in the relevant form factor
or effective charge-to-mass ratio. Reversal of the asymmetry changes the sign
of the hypothesized differential response while leaving many backgrounds
unchanged, providing a general symmetry test independent of how the resulting
motion is observed.

6.5
Candidate-to-observable map

Table
6.1
connects each dark-matter candidate to
the physical perturbation it may produce, the detector response coordinate,
and the minimum condition for a non-vanishing and distinguishable response.
It is a candidate-to-response map, not a prescription for a particular
measurement implementation. Resonance is included only where it belongs to
the response principle; transient candidates instead require a response over
the inverse event duration.

Table 6.1:
Dark-matter candidates and the corresponding physical response
principles. The table is independent of a particular readout implementation
and does not represent a sensitivity forecast.

Dark-matter candidate

Physical effect in the apparatus

Response coordinate

Condition for nonzero response

Scalar or dilaton field

Oscillation of material dimensions, refractive index, or a
material-referenced optical frequency

Differential phase or excitation of a mechanical mode

Unequal material, geometric, or temporal response; absence of exact
common-mode cancellation

Vector field or dark photon

Oscillating, possibly composition-dependent force on suspended masses

Relative translation or rotation

Nonuniform force, charge dipole relative to the center of mass, or differential
suspension response

Axion or axion-like field

Opposite phase shifts of left- and right-circularly polarized light

Polarization rotation or differential polarization phase

Nonzero projection onto a polarization-sensitive eigenmode

Ultralight tensor field

Coherent oscillating effective strain with a tensor antenna pattern

Oriented strain or mechanical coordinates

Nonzero contraction between the field tensor and detector response tensor

Topological defect

Transient variation of constants, optical properties, or forces during
passage

Time-dependent material, optical, or mechanical coordinates

Event duration within the response band and a consistent propagation
pattern

Compact dark-matter clump

Trajectory-dependent acceleration or field gradient

Differential displacement or torque

A resolvable gradient or body-dependent acceleration across the apparatus

Coherent particle scattering

Geometry- or composition-dependent momentum transfer from a dark-matter
wind

Differential force or torque

Different coherent form factors or effective charge-to-mass ratios

The table also clarifies why no single detector coordinate covers the full
program. Scalar fields are converted by material and optical asymmetries,
vector fields by differential force or torque, axion-like fields by
polarization propagation, and macroscopic objects by transient gravitational
gradients. A candidate interaction must produce a mutually consistent pattern
among phase, translation, rotation, polarization, frequency, and force
coordinates, regardless of the channels used to reconstruct them.

6.6
Indirect dark-matter science with gravitational waves

Gravitational-wave observations can also probe dark matter without a direct
interaction between the Galactic field and the detector. These searches are
conceptually distinct from the instrumental measurements described above. They
include searches for sub-solar-mass compact binaries as candidates for
primordial black holes, continuous gravitational waves from boson clouds around
rotating black holes, modifications of compact-binary evolution by a dense
dark-matter environment, lensing or propagation effects, and gravitational-wave
backgrounds associated with primordial-black-hole formation or dark-sector
cosmological processes

[
58
,
59
,
60
]
.

These topics define a broader dark-matter science portfolio for gravitational-
wave astronomy. They require separate studies of the source population,
waveform, detector response, and analysis band. The direct instrumental
program and the astrophysical program should therefore remain distinct.

6.7
Future work: applicability to CHRONOS

Any quantitative CHRONOS forecast should start from the full noise and
complex response in Chap.
3
, then calculate the
candidate-specific force or optical coupling. A GW strain ASD cannot be
relabeled as a dark-matter coupling limit. Coherence time, scan dwell time,
field-density fraction, detector orientation, and the statistical confidence
construction must be specified. For a polarization search, the relevant
readout noise must be established independently of the GW strain budget.

The preceding sections summarize detection principles established for generic
precision interferometers. Their applicability to CHRONOS has not yet been
demonstrated. The next stage is therefore not to select a measurement channel,
but to determine, for each candidate class, whether the corresponding physical
response is observable at all in the CHRONOS instrument. The required action
items are as follows.

1.

Scalar and dilaton fields:
calculate the response to changes of
material dimensions, refractive index, elastic properties, and reference
frequency, including all common-mode and finite-propagation-time
cancellations.

2.

Vector fields and dark photons:
calculate the generalized
forces and torques on the suspended bodies and determine whether the detector
symmetries make every accessible differential response vanish.

3.

Axion and axion-like fields:
propagate the predicted
polarization-dependent phase through the optical system and determine whether
any accessible detector coordinate retains a nonzero response.

4.

Ultralight tensor fields:
derive the detector response tensor
and determine whether the available spatial orientations provide a
distinguishable projection of the predicted field.

5.

Topological defects:
calculate the response to a propagating
transient variation of material, optical, and mechanical quantities and
determine the range of event durations that is not suppressed by the detector
dynamics.

6.

Compact clumps and macroscopic dark matter:
calculate the
trajectory-dependent differential acceleration, torque, and optical
propagation effects, and determine whether a resolvable spatial gradient
remains after common acceleration is rejected.

7.

Coherent particle scattering:
determine whether the relevant
test bodies have sufficiently different form factors to produce a nonzero
differential force or torque.

8.

Cross-cutting assessment:
for every candidate above, identify
the observable detector coordinates without assuming that the final science
channel is used; derive the complete response function including mechanical,
optical, and control dynamics; identify exact symmetry cancellations; and
compare the surviving response with the relevant physical backgrounds.

The result of this program should be an explicit classification of each
candidate as observable, conditionally observable, or unobservable with the
available CHRONOS configuration. Only after that classification is complete
should a specific measurement implementation, calibration procedure, or
sensitivity projection be developed.

Chapter 7  Earthquake Gravity Signals

7.1
Introduction

The sub-Hz frequency band targeted by CHRONOS provides access not only to
astrophysical gravitational-wave sources, but also to terrestrial
gravitational perturbations generated by seismic and atmospheric processes.
In this frequency range, fluctuations of the local gravitational field,
commonly referred to as gravity-gradient noise or Newtonian noise, are
expected to become an important environmental background for ground-based
gravitational-wave detectors
[
61
,
62
]
.

Unlike ordinary seismic disturbances, Newtonian noise couples directly to
the test masses through gravity. It therefore cannot be suppressed by
conventional mechanical isolation systems. This characteristic makes
gravity-gradient fluctuations one of the fundamental challenges for
extending ground-based gravitational-wave observations into the sub-Hz
regime.

For CHRONOS, however, terrestrial gravity perturbations are not only a
source of noise. The same sensitivity required for sub-Hz
gravitational-wave observation provides an opportunity to directly observe
time-dependent gravitational fields generated by geophysical processes.

A particularly interesting target is the prompt gravitational perturbation
generated by an earthquake. During an earthquake, rapid redistribution of
underground mass modifies the surrounding gravitational field. The
associated gravitational perturbation propagates essentially at the speed
of light and can therefore reach a detector before elastic seismic waves,
including the P wave
[
63
]
.

This opens the possibility of observing an earthquake through gravity
before conventional seismic signals arrive. Such a measurement would
provide an observational channel fundamentally different from ordinary
seismology and could potentially complement existing earthquake
early-warning networks
[
64
,
65
]
.

More importantly, direct observation of the prompt gravity perturbation
would provide information about the earliest stages of earthquake rupture
and the evolution of the seismic moment itself. CHRONOS may therefore
operate not only as a sub-Hz gravitational-wave detector, but also as a
high-sensitivity terrestrial gravity-gradient observatory.

7.2
Prompt Gravity Signal from an Earthquake

An earthquake produces rapid deformation and displacement of material
inside the Earth. This redistribution of mass modifies the local Newtonian
gravitational field and produces a time-dependent gravitational
perturbation.

At the same time, the earthquake generates conventional elastic waves,
including P waves, S waves, and surface waves. These waves propagate
through the Earth with velocities of several kilometers per second
[
66
]
.
The gravitational perturbation, in contrast, propagates effectively at the
speed of light.

As a result, a sufficiently sensitive gravity-gradient detector can respond
to the changing mass distribution before the seismic elastic wavefront itself
reaches the detector.

Figure
7.1
illustrates this basic concept.
The earthquake rupture generates both a prompt gravity perturbation and
conventional seismic waves. The prompt gravity signal reaches CHRONOS first,
followed by the P wave after a propagation delay determined primarily by the
source–detector distance and the seismic-wave velocity.

Figure 7.1:

Concept of prompt gravity detection from an earthquake.
Rapid redistribution of underground mass generates a time-dependent
gravitational perturbation as well as conventional seismic waves.
The gravity perturbation reaches CHRONOS before the seismic P wave.

Within the seismic-source description adopted here, the density perturbation
associated with the earthquake can be expressed in terms of the seismic
potential
\phi_{s}
as
[
63
]

\delta\rho_{\rm EQ}=-\rho_{0}\nabla^{2}\phi_{s},

(7.1)

where
\rho_{0}
is the unperturbed density of the surrounding medium.

Let
t
be time since rupture onset,
r
the source–detector distance,

G
Newton’s gravitational constant, and
M_{0}(t)
the seismic moment in

\mathrm{N\,m}
. For a point double-couple source in a homogeneous,
unbounded medium, the root-mean-square (RMS) rotational gravity strain,
averaged over source and detector orientations, is
[
63
]

h_{g,\mathrm{rms}}(r,t)=6\sqrt{\frac{14}{5}}\,\frac{G}{r^{5}}I_{4}[M_{0}](t),\qquad 0<t<t_{P},\quad t_{P}=\frac{r}{v_{P}}.

(7.2)

Here
v_{P}
is the P-wave speed,
t_{P}
its arrival time, and
I_{4}
denotes
four integrations with zero initial conditions:

I_{4}[M_{0}](t)=\frac{1}{6}\int_{0}^{t}(t-t^{\prime})^{3}M_{0}(t^{\prime})\,dt^{\prime}.

(7.3)

The gravity-strain convention of Ref.
[
63
]
uses half
the relative rotation of two ideal perpendicular bars. With the geometric
factor
\eta
of Chap.
3
, the free differential
rotation is
2\eta h_{g}
. The equivalent GW-strain input is therefore

h_{\rm EQ}=2h_{g}
, so that
\theta_{\rm sig}=\eta h_{\rm EQ}
. This factor
of two is applied before comparison with the Chapter 3 noise. Gravity strain
here describes a Newtonian tidal response, not an emitted propagating GW.

For reproducible source waveforms, let
M_{\infty}
denote the final moment,

M_{w}
the moment magnitude, and
T_{s}
half the source duration. We adopt the
moment-rate shape and duration scaling of
Ref.
[
64
]
:

\displaystyle M_{\infty}

\displaystyle=10^{1.5M_{w}+9.1}~\mathrm{N\,m},

\displaystyle 2T_{s}

\displaystyle=\left(\frac{M_{\infty}}{10^{16}~\mathrm{N\,m}}\right)^{1/3}\mathrm{s},

(7.4)

\displaystyle\dot{M}_{0}(t)

\displaystyle=\frac{A_{s}M_{\infty}}{T_{s}}g(t/T_{s}),

\displaystyle A_{s}^{-1}

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{2}g(u)\,du,

\displaystyle g(u)

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}u^{2},&0\leq u<1,\\
[1-(u-1)^{2}]^{6},&1\leq u\leq 2,\\
0,&\text{otherwise}.\end{cases}

(7.5)

The dimensionless constant
A_{s}
normalizes the integrated moment to

M_{\infty}
;
u=t/T_{s}
is dimensionless time. The duration depends on
magnitude, independently of source distance.

7.3
Coupling to the CHRONOS Torsion Bar

Terrestrial gravity perturbations couple directly to the torsional degree
of freedom of CHRONOS
[
8
,
67
]
.

For a single-bar rotation
\theta(t)
, the rotational motion can be described by

I\ddot{\theta}+\frac{I\omega_{\rm tb}}{Q}\dot{\theta}+I\omega_{\rm tb}^{2}\theta=\tau_{\rm ext},

(7.6)

where
I
is the moment of inertia of the torsion bar,

\omega_{\rm tb}=2\pi f_{\rm tb}
is the torsional resonant angular
frequency (
f_{\rm tb}
is the resonance in hertz),
Q
is the mechanical
quality factor, and
\tau_{\rm ext}
is
the external gravitational torque.

Let
f
denote Fourier frequency,
\Omega=2\pi f
angular frequency,
and
\chi_{\theta}
the torque-to-angle susceptibility in
\mathrm{rad/(N\,m)}
.
In the frequency domain,

\theta(f)=\chi_{\theta}(f)\tau_{\rm ext}(f),

(7.7)

where the torsional susceptibility is

\chi_{\theta}(f)=\frac{1}{I\left(\omega_{\rm tb}^{2}-\Omega^{2}+i\Omega\omega_{\rm tb}/Q\right)},\qquad\Omega=2\pi f.

(7.8)

Let
\Gamma_{ij}=\partial g_{i}/\partial x_{j}
be the gravity-gradient tensor,
where
g_{i}
is gravitational acceleration, and define the bar’s mass second
moments
J_{ij}=\int\rho_{\rm bar}x_{i}x_{j}\,d^{3}x
. Here
x,y
are horizontal
principal axes,
z
is vertical, and
\rho_{\rm bar}
is the bar density.
For one bar, the leading torque about
z
is

\tau_{z}=\left(J_{xx}-J_{yy}\right)\Gamma_{xy}.

(7.9)

The resulting angular response is therefore

\theta(f)=\chi_{\theta}(f)\left(J_{xx}-J_{yy}\right)\Gamma_{xy}(f).

(7.10)

The very low torsional resonance frequency of CHRONOS makes this rotational
degree of freedom particularly sensitive to slowly varying gravity-gradient
fields in the sub-Hz band.
This provides the fundamental detector response used to search for
earthquake-induced gravity perturbations.

7.4
Detectability with CHRONOS

The numerical forecasts use the same two full strain-noise spectra as
Chap.
3
: the reference-paper quantum model combined
with CHRONOISE coating, bar-thermal, seismic, Newtonian, and intensity noise

[
14
,
24
]
. These archived operating
points are used consistently across the science chapters. Their ASDs at
0.5 Hz are
1.237\times 10^{-17}
and
6.154\times 10^{-18}~\mathrm{Hz}^{-1/2}

for minimum PI and minimum logarithmic mean, respectively.

We define a single-channel, pre-P template benchmark with

v_{P}=8.0~\mathrm{km\,s^{-1}}
. Let
w(t)=\sin^{2}(\pi t/t_{P})
on

0\leq t\leq t_{P}
and zero elsewhere. Its Fourier amplitude is

\widetilde{h}_{w}(f)=\int_{0}^{t_{P}}w(t)h_{{\rm EQ},\mathrm{rms}}(t)e^{-2\pi ift}\,dt.

(7.11)

The Hann taper suppresses endpoint discontinuities and is not amplitude
renormalized. Thus
\widetilde{h}_{w}
has units
\mathrm{Hz}^{-1}
; it is
not a signal ASD. With one-sided strain-noise PSD
S_{n}(f)
, the template
norm, quoted below as the tapered pre-P SNR
\rho_{w}
, is

\rho_{w}^{2}=4\int_{f_{\min}}^{f_{\max}}\frac{|\widetilde{h}_{w}(f)|^{2}}{S_{n}(f)}\,df,\qquad f_{\min}=\max(0.1~\mathrm{Hz},1/t_{P}),\quad f_{\max}=10~\mathrm{Hz}.

(7.12)

The lower cutoff excludes periods longer than the available window and
stays inside the common science band. No multiple-channel gain is assumed.
Figure
7.2
shows the waveform and the
characteristic strains
h_{c}=2f|\widetilde{h}_{w}|
and
h_{n}=\sqrt{fS_{n}}
.
Table
7.1
gives representative results.

Figure 7.2:
Recalculated earthquake benchmark for
M_{w}=5.2
at 40 km.
Left: RMS equivalent GW-strain input and its Hann-tapered pre-P template;

t_{P}=5
s. Right: characteristic template strain and the two Chapter 3
noise spectra, including the CHRONOISE classical noise budget. The integral
uses 0.2–10 Hz and gives
\rho_{w}=48.0
(minimum PI) or 91.1
(minimum logarithmic mean), under the stated ideal source assumptions.

Table 7.1:
Tapered pre-P template SNRs with the reference quantum plus CHRONOISE classical noise.
These use the one-channel RMS source benchmark and window specified in the text.

Distance [km]

t_{P}
[s]

Logarithmic mean

8.0

37.5

309

652

These numbers are the noise-weighted norms of the specified tapered
signals. They do not establish real-time alert significance: a causal
search needs finite-window noise covariance, local transient backgrounds,
and a false-alarm calibration. The point-source calculation also omits
free-surface structure, finite faults, and the particular site’s source
orientations. The approximation is especially uncertain when the rupture
extent is comparable to the source distance. The waveform, SNR grids,
source normalization, noise-file hashes, and integration checks are
included with the source data. Halving the time step and doubling Fourier
padding changes the four tabulated SNR pairs by less than
8\times 10^{-5}
.

7.5
Lead Time before Seismic-Wave Arrival

The most distinctive feature of prompt gravity detection is that the
gravitational perturbation is observed before the arrival of the seismic
P wave.

If the earthquake occurs at source distance
r
, the approximate lead time
relative to the P-wave arrival is

\Delta t\simeq\frac{r}{v_{P}},

(7.13)

where the propagation time of the gravitational perturbation itself is
negligible on this scale and
v_{P}
is the effective P-wave velocity
[
66
]
.

Assuming

v_{P}=8.0~{\rm km\,s^{-1}},

(7.14)

a source at
40~{\rm km}
corresponds to a lead time of approximately

\Delta t\simeq 5~{\rm s}.

(7.15)

Considering representative P-wave velocities between

5.8
and
13.7~{\rm km\,s^{-1}}
, the corresponding lead time for the
same source distance lies approximately between

2.9~{\rm s}<\Delta t<6.9~{\rm s}.

(7.16)

The interval
r/v_{P}
is an upper bound set by propagation. An operational
warning time must subtract signal accumulation, processing, and communication
latencies. Detectability before P-wave arrival must be evaluated with the
truncated waveform, not with a full-event SNR.

This produces an important trade-off.
Nearby earthquakes generate stronger gravitational signals but provide
shorter warning times, whereas more distant earthquakes provide longer lead
times at the cost of significantly reduced signal amplitude.

The significance of the prompt gravity measurement should therefore not be
viewed solely in terms of the number of seconds of early warning.
The gravity signal provides information about the source through a channel
that is independent of seismic-wave propagation.
Its combination with conventional seismic data could therefore improve our
understanding of the earliest earthquake rupture process.

7.6
Magnitude and Distance Dependence

Figure
7.3
recalculates the magnitude–distance
map with the CHRONOISE-based noise used in Chap.
3
.
Both panels use exactly the same source, strain conversion, pre-P window,
and frequency prescription; only the optical operating point changes.
The contours describe the tapered-template benchmark, rather than an
operational detection boundary.

Figure 7.3:
Tapered pre-P SNR from the common CHRONOISE-based noise budget:
minimum PI (left) and minimum logarithmic mean (right). Colour gives

\log_{10}\rho_{w}
; white curves label constant
\rho_{w}
. Each point uses

t_{P}=r/(8~\mathrm{km\,s^{-1}})
, the RMS point-source model, and
Eq. (
7.12
). These idealized maps do not include finite-fault
or site-specific orientation effects and are not alert-probability maps.

Magnitude changes both the final moment and the rupture duration. In the
early part of the adopted self-similar source,
M_{0}(t)\propto t^{3}
and

M_{\infty}/T_{s}^{3}
is constant; the pre-P signal can therefore approach a
magnitude-independent limit at fixed distance. Distance also changes the
available observation time, so the SNR cannot be obtained from an
r^{-5}

amplitude rescaling alone. A physical detection rate requires finite-fault
waveforms and measured local backgrounds in addition to this comparison.

7.7
Prospects for Earthquake Observation in Taiwan

The practical significance of the projected CHRONOS sensitivity can be
motivated by comparing candidate source parameters with historical
earthquake activity around Taiwan
[
68
]
.

Using earthquakes with

M_{w}\geq 5

(7.17)

and source–detector distances below approximately
100~{\rm km}
between
January 1995 and May 2026, the historical earthquake catalog
[
68
]
gives an
average occurrence rate of approximately

6.3~{\rm events~yr^{-1}}.

(7.18)

Most of these earthquakes fall within the range

5<M_{w}<6.

(7.19)

The catalog motivates studies of moderate regional events as well as
rare large earthquakes; it does not by itself determine a detectable rate.

Figure
7.4
illustrates the distribution of
historical earthquakes around the proposed detector region together with
their magnitude distribution.

Figure 7.4:
Historical earthquake population around Taiwan relevant to the
candidate CHRONOS source region, together with the corresponding moment
magnitude distribution.

The catalog demonstrates that the region contains potential sources for
a prompt-gravity program. A rate forecast requires a verified
magnitude–distance selection function with the updated noise, local
backgrounds, detector uptime, and source orientations. Historical occurrence
alone does not establish repeated detectable events.

7.8
Gravity-Gradient Background

The observation of prompt earthquake gravity signals must be considered
together with the ambient terrestrial gravity-gradient environment.

Density fluctuations produced by seismic surface waves and atmospheric
pressure variations generate continuous fluctuations of the local
gravitational field.
These stochastic backgrounds constitute Newtonian noise for
gravitational-wave observations
[
61
,
69
]
.

Figure
7.5
uses the Newtonian component
from the same CHRONOISE noise data as the SNR calculation. It is the
Rayleigh-wave contribution for the reference environmental settings,
shared by both operating points. The ground-motion input is the archived
KAGRA spectrum multiplied by the reference amplitude factor
0.01
,
with an assumed detector depth of 200 m. This assumed spectrum is not a
measurement of the proposed Taiwan site. Atmospheric and local transient
gravity backgrounds require separate site-specific estimates.

Figure 7.5:
CHRONOISE Rayleigh-wave Newtonian strain ASD (dashed green) and
the two full-noise spectra used throughout this White Paper. All curves
use the same differential-angle strain convention and the reference
0.1–10 Hz band. The Newtonian contribution is already included in both
totals; it must not be added a second time to the SNR noise.

The Rayleigh-wave gravity-gradient background depends on both the detector
depth and the seismic quietness of the site.
An underground location suppresses the gravitational coupling from surface
Rayleigh waves, particularly toward higher frequencies
[
61
]
.
However, the overall seismic activity of the site has a stronger effect on
the predicted background amplitude.

The gravity-gradient contribution depends on the measured seismic spectrum,
depth, density structure, and spatial correlations. Its comparison with
instrumental noise must use the updated budget in
Fig.
3.1
. A fixed crossover interval from an
earlier noise curve is not retained as a property of the revised baseline.

These results indicate that site characterization is an essential component
of the CHRONOS program.
Measurements of the local seismic field, subsurface structure, and spatial
correlations will be required both to predict the Newtonian-noise floor and
to distinguish transient earthquake signals from the stationary
gravity-gradient background.

The same measurements will also provide valuable information for developing
future Newtonian-noise subtraction techniques using environmental sensor
arrays
[
70
,
71
]
.

7.9
Scientific Perspective

The earthquake program illustrates a distinctive feature of sub-Hz
gravitational measurements with CHRONOS.

Terrestrial gravity fluctuations have traditionally been regarded primarily
as a limiting noise source for low-frequency gravitational-wave detectors.
For CHRONOS, however, these fluctuations can themselves become measurable
signals carrying information about geophysical processes.

The science opportunity is conditional on reconstructing a prompt-gravity
waveform above the full noise before seismic arrival. The updated sensitivity
and source-response calculation must be evaluated together; the geometric
lead-time estimates alone do not establish this capability.

The scientific importance of such a measurement extends beyond earthquake
early warning.
Because the gravitational perturbation directly traces changing mass
distribution rather than the subsequent propagation of elastic waves, it
provides an independent probe of the earthquake source.

Direct observation of prompt gravity signals could therefore establish a
new observational channel for studying the onset of earthquake rupture and
the evolution of the seismic moment.

At the same time, continuous measurements of the ambient gravity-gradient
field would provide direct information about Rayleigh-wave Newtonian noise,
the secondary microseism environment, and the site dependence of terrestrial
gravity fluctuations.

CHRONOS can therefore serve simultaneously as a sub-Hz
gravitational-wave detector and as a precision terrestrial
gravity-gradient observatory.
Earthquake-induced prompt gravity signals represent one of the clearest
examples of the interdisciplinary science enabled by extending precision
gravitational measurements into the sub-Hz regime.

Chapter 8  Search for Yukawa-Type Gravity

Let
r
be the separation between a source mass
m
and a test-body mass
M
,
and
G
the Newtonian gravitational constant. The potential energy
V(r)

includes a Yukawa correction of dimensionless relative strength
\alpha_{Y}

and interaction range
\lambda
. CHRONOS can search for this correction
using controlled gravitational torques,

V(r)=-\frac{GmM}{r}\left(1+\alpha_{Y}e^{-r/\lambda}\right),

(8.1)

This chapter adopts the revised differential-GCal analysis of
Ref.
[
72
]
. Let
f_{\rm rot}
denote the source-rotor rotation frequency. The key
improvement is to combine the
2f_{\rm rot}
signal with calibrated
4f_{\rm rot}
and
6f_{\rm rot}

amplitudes, suppressing the leading geometrical uncertainties. The same
subtraction must be applied to the predicted Yukawa signal. The resulting
limits are conditional systematic-floor projections, not measured exclusions.

8.1
Differential source and reference geometry

Two counter-rotating gravitational calibrators are placed at different
vertical distances below the detector. Their radii and separations are chosen
to cancel the nominal Newtonian
2f_{\rm rot}
torque. The closer and farther
sources are denoted
S
and
L
. For source index
i\in\{S,L\}
,
m_{i}
is
the mass of each rotor endpoint,
b_{i}
the rotor radius,
h_{i}
the vertical
separation, and
\rho_{i}
the transverse offset. The detector parameter
a

is its quadrupole effective distance from the rotation axis. Define the
effective source–test-body separation
R_{i}
and dimensionless expansion
parameter
\xi_{i}
below. The function
{}_{2}F_{1}
is the Gauss hypergeometric
function. The GCal torque and finite-size factor

\eta_{\rm GCAL}
are introduced in Chap.
21
; the latter is
included once in each torque amplitude. With

R_{i}^{2}=a^{2}+b_{i}^{2}+h_{i}^{2}+\rho_{i}^{2}
and
\xi_{i}=2ab_{i}/R_{i}^{2}
, the exact null is

\frac{m_{L}\xi_{L}^{2}}{R_{L}}\,{}_{2}F_{1}\!\left(\frac{5}{4},\frac{7}{4};3;\xi_{L}^{2}\right)=\frac{m_{S}\xi_{S}^{2}}{R_{S}}\,{}_{2}F_{1}\!\left(\frac{5}{4},\frac{7}{4};3;\xi_{S}^{2}\right).

(8.2)

The distance ratio
\gamma\equiv h_{L}/h_{S}
is fixed by this null at the nominal geometry. Uncertainty propagation
holds that operating position fixed; the null is not re-tuned separately
for each perturbed realization.

Figure 8.1:
Differential GCal concept, reproduced from Fig. 1 of
Ref.
[
72
]
. The farther source position is adjusted
to null the Newtonian
2f_{\rm rot}
torque. Auxiliary harmonics provide
additional information on departures from this nominal geometry.
The displayed series use positive integer index
n
, dimensionless Newtonian
coefficients
A_{n}
, and dimensionless Yukawa coefficients

B_{n}(\beta_{i}^{(Y)})
, with
\beta_{i}^{(Y)}=R_{i}/\lambda
for
i=S,L
.
The other geometric symbols are defined in the text.

The fiducial rotor frequency is
f_{\rm rot}=0.25~\mathrm{Hz}
, giving
measurement frequencies 0.5, 1.0, and 1.5 Hz. Table
8.1

records the geometry and uncertainty assumptions used for the quoted limits.
Here
M=85.66~\mathrm{kg}
is the mass parameter of one effective end body,
not the full
171.32~\mathrm{kg}
bar mass;
a=0.30213~\mathrm{m}
is the
quadrupole effective distance, not the main-laser spot lever
\ell
.

8.2
Harmonic subtraction and signal response

Let
\bm{p}
contain the source and detector parameters in
Table
8.1
, with nominal values
\bm{p}_{0}
.
For harmonic index
q=1,2,3
, let
d_{q}
be the measured signed, phase-referenced amplitude
at
2qf_{\rm rot}
, expressed in single-bar torque units. Write
N_{q}(\bm{p})

for the Newtonian prediction and
Y_{q}(\lambda)
for the unit-strength
Yukawa torque, evaluated at
\alpha_{Y}=1
and the nominal geometry.
Both predictions use the finite-size gravitational model of
Ref.
[
72
]
. The nominal Newtonian amplitudes are

N_{q,0}=N_{q}(\bm{p}_{0})
; only
N_{1,0}
is nulled.

Let
k_{4}
and
k_{6}
be dimensionless subtraction coefficients for the

4f_{\rm rot}
and
6f_{\rm rot}
amplitudes. The residual torque
r
and
its unit-strength Yukawa response
Y_{\rm eff}
are

\displaystyle r

\displaystyle=(d_{1}-N_{1,0})-k_{4}(d_{2}-N_{2,0})-k_{6}(d_{3}-N_{3,0}),

(8.3)

\displaystyle Y_{\rm eff}(\lambda)

\displaystyle=Y_{1}(\lambda)-k_{4}Y_{2}(\lambda)-k_{6}Y_{3}(\lambda).

(8.4)

Amplitudes are combined with their signs; subtracting the absolute values
would not implement this estimator. Let
J
be the Newtonian torque
Jacobian, with entries
J_{qi}=\partial N_{q}/\partial p_{i}|_{\bm{p}_{0}}
for
parameter
p_{i}
. A parameter name in the second subscript selects that
column of
J
. Then the
4f
correction chooses

k_{4}=J_{1,h_{L}}/J_{2,h_{L}}
and
k_{6}=0
. When
6f
is added, both coefficients
are recalculated from

J_{1,p}-k_{4}J_{2,p}-k_{6}J_{3,p}=0,\qquad p\in\{h_{L},b_{S}\}.

(8.5)

These conditions remove the selected first-order parameter errors; they do
not remove all geometrical errors or nonlinear residuals.

Each harmonic must be calibrated using its own complex mechanical and
optical response before forming Eq. (
8.3
). In an
ideal orthogonal two-bar difference, the
4f
torque cancels. Its auxiliary
measurement therefore requires an individual-bar or sum channel; it cannot
be recovered by simply reusing the differential
2f
strain channel.

8.3
Sensitivity after subtraction

Let
\bm{w}=(1,-k_{4},-k_{6})^{T}
be the subtraction-weight vector, where the
superscript
T
denotes transpose, and
C_{p}
the covariance matrix of
parameter errors. The Newtonian torque covariance is
C_{N}=JC_{p}J^{T}
at
first order. The corresponding variance of the residual systematic torque
is
\sigma_{\rm sys,lin}^{2}=\bm{w}^{T}C_{N}\bm{w}
. The quoted forecasts use the
nonlinear total checked with 50 000 independent Gaussian Monte Carlo
realizations in Ref.
[
72
]
;
\sigma_{\rm sys}

denotes the resulting systematic torque uncertainty. Let
T
be coherent
integration time in seconds and
S_{\tau}
the one-sided, phase-referenced
harmonic torque-noise PSD matrix, in
(\mathrm{N\,m})^{2}/\mathrm{Hz}
.
The coupling thresholds
\alpha_{Y,\rm floor}
and
\alpha_{Y,1\sigma}

are the systematic floor and combined one-standard-deviation threshold.
Define
T_{\rm eq}
as the time when statistical and systematic torque
variances are equal. The final expressions are

\displaystyle\alpha_{Y,\rm floor}(\lambda)

\displaystyle=\frac{\sigma_{\rm sys}}{|Y_{\rm eff}(\lambda)|},

(8.6)

\displaystyle\alpha_{Y,1\sigma}(\lambda,T)

\displaystyle=\frac{\sqrt{\sigma_{\rm sys}^{2}+\bm{w}^{T}S_{\tau}\bm{w}/T}}{|Y_{\rm eff}(\lambda)|},

(8.7)

\displaystyle T_{\rm eq}

\displaystyle=\frac{\bm{w}^{T}S_{\tau}\bm{w}}{\sigma_{\rm sys}^{2}}.

(8.8)

Let
S_{\tau,2}
,
S_{\tau,4}
, and
S_{\tau,6}
be the diagonal noise
PSDs of the
2f_{\rm rot}
,
4f_{\rm rot}
, and
6f_{\rm rot}
channels.
For independent readout noise,

\bm{w}^{T}S_{\tau}\bm{w}=S_{\tau,2}+k_{4}^{2}S_{\tau,4}+k_{6}^{2}S_{\tau,6}
.
Thus suppressing systematic error can amplify auxiliary measurement noise.
At
T=T_{\rm eq}
the total threshold is
\sqrt{2}
times the systematic
floor, and reaching within 10% of that floor requires

T_{10\%}=T_{\rm eq}/0.21
. The floor is not a finite-time confidence limit.

, from

Without harmonic subtraction, the largest error contributions are
h_{L}

and

. After canceling both at first order, source-mass uncertainties
and the effective distance
a
become important. Better source metrology,

8.4
Noise normalization and integration time

The torque formulation above avoids mixing strain conventions between
studies. Here
\chi
is the single-bar angular susceptibility in rad/(N m),

\tau
is the single-bar torque amplitude,
\eta_{Y}
is the reference
geometry factor, and
F_{\rm eff}
is its effective antenna factor.
In the free-rotor limit,
\chi=-1/[I(2\pi f)^{2}]
, with single-bar inertia
I

and signal frequency
f
. The Yukawa reference defines the apparent strain of a single-bar

2f
torque
\tau
as

h_{\rm ref}=4|\chi\tau|/\eta_{Y}
, with
\eta_{Y}=0.936
and

|F_{\rm eff}|=1
. Chapter
3
instead uses

\theta_{X}-\theta_{Y}=\eta h
with
\eta=0.93656
, giving

h=2|\chi\tau|/\eta
for opposite torques on the two bars.
Consequently
h=(\eta_{Y}/2\eta)h_{\rm ref}
; both noise and signal must be
converted by this same factor. It cancels from
\alpha_{Y,\rm floor}
but
must be retained when computing integration times with a new noise curve.

The published ideal times assume

\sqrt{S_{h,\rm ref}(0.5~\mathrm{Hz})}=10^{-17}~\mathrm{Hz}^{-1/2}
,

I=19.86~\mathrm{kg\,m^{2}}
, and negligible
4f/6f
readout noise. They are
listed as reference assumptions in Table
8.3
; they
are not the noise level of the updated detector baseline. The updated
minimum-PI and logarithmic-mean noise values and the resulting times are
also provided there, with the same torque normalization and metrology
floor. These remain ideal projections until the auxiliary channels and
their correlated noise have been characterized.

T_{\rm eq}
in days for the three estimators.

with the original noise and strain convention.
The other rows use the full strain noise of Chap.

:

1.237\times 10^{-17}
and

at 0.5 Hz.
The Monte Carlo floors are converted to the same convention. In all rows,

T_{10\%}=T_{\rm eq}/0.21
and auxiliary readout noise is neglected.

Integration-time forecasts recalculated with the two full-noise
operating points in Chap.
3
. Each estimator is evaluated
at the range of its own minimum in Table
8.2
.
Solid curves combine statistical and systematic errors; dotted lines show

[

]
.
The strain conversion is applied to the floors before adding the detector
noise. Auxiliary
4f/6f
measurement noise is neglected, so these are ideal
times rather than established experimental integration requirements.

8.5
Long-range interpretation and priorities

At the exact Newtonian
2f
null,
Y_{1}\propto\lambda^{-2}
for large

\lambda
, so the uncorrected coupling threshold grows as
\lambda^{2}
.
For the auxiliary channels
Y_{2,3}\to N_{2,3,0}
, and the subtracted
response tends to
-k_{4}N_{2,0}-k_{6}N_{3,0}
. This gives the finite plateaus
in Table
8.2
. Those plateaus depend on external
priors on
G
, source masses, and geometry: without those priors, an
infinite-range Yukawa term is degenerate with the gravitational normalization.
Zeros of
Y_{\rm eff}
instead produce loss of sensitivity and poles in
the threshold curve.

The experimental program is therefore to validate the Newtonian null,
measure all participating complex harmonic responses, demonstrate the
predicted rejection of geometrical errors, and determine the statistical
and systematic covariance of the combined estimator. The projected gain
from subtraction is realized only if these measurements support the
assumptions used in Eqs. (
8.6
)–(
8.8
).

Chapter 9  Gravitational-Wave Science

CHRONOS targets the 0.1–10 Hz interval between the principal bands of
space-based and conventional terrestrial laser interferometers. Its science
case includes binaries containing intermediate-mass black holes (IMBHs)
and stochastic gravitational-wave backgrounds (SGWBs). The forecasts here
use the full strain noise of Chap.
3
for both optimized
operating points. They are conditional instrument projections, not measured
performance or guaranteed detection rates.

9.1
Accessing the gravitational-wave frequency gap

Pulsar timing arrays probe nanohertz frequencies, LISA is designed for the
millihertz band, and conventional terrestrial interferometers principally
observe above roughly 10 Hz. These windows are not sharp boundaries.
Ground-based access to lower frequencies is limited by seismic coupling,
gravity-gradient noise, and mechanical dissipation. CHRONOS combines a
low-frequency torsional response with cryogenic operation and a Sagnac
speed-meter readout. Environmental gravity fluctuations still require site
characterization and mitigation.

The relevant sensitivity measure depends on the source. A compact-binary
search coherently accumulates a waveform; an SGWB search accumulates excess
or correlated power. Neither reach is determined by the lowest strain ASD
alone. The two PI-optimized settings need not be optimal for every binary
mass or every stochastic spectrum.

9.2
Intermediate-mass black-hole binaries

IMBHs are commonly associated with masses of roughly
10^{2}
–
10^{5}M_{\odot}
.
GW190521 provided evidence for an IMBH merger remnant

[
73
]
; the abundance and formation history of this
population remain uncertain. Observations could test hierarchical mergers,
stellar-cluster formation channels, and the growth of massive black-hole
seeds, provided the detector selection function is established.

9.2.1
Mass, frequency, and calibrated signal overlap

Increasing binary mass shifts coalescence toward lower frequency. A useful
scale is the Schwarzschild test-particle innermost-stable-circular-orbit
(ISCO) frequency. Let
G
be the gravitational constant,
c
the speed of
light,
M_{\odot}
the solar mass,
z
the cosmological redshift,

M_{\rm tot}
the source-frame total mass, and
M_{z}=(1+z)M_{\rm tot}

the detector-frame total mass. The observed frequency is

f_{\rm ISCO,obs}\simeq\frac{c^{3}}{6^{3/2}\pi GM_{z}}\simeq 4.4~\mathrm{Hz}\left(\frac{10^{3}M_{\odot}}{M_{z}}\right),\quad M_{z}=(1+z)M_{\rm tot}.

(9.1)

This scale is not a precise merger or ringdown frequency for
comparable masses. Lower-mass binaries traverse the CHRONOS band in inspiral;
higher-mass systems can coalesce in the band or below it.

For the signal comparison,
\widetilde{h}(f)
is the Fourier amplitude of
the projected strain and
S_{n}(f)
is the full one-sided strain-noise PSD.
Define the dimensionless characteristic signal
h_{c}=2f|\widetilde{h}|
and
noise
h_{n}=\sqrt{fS_{n}}
. The spin labels denote the mass-weighted projections

\chi_{\rm eff}
along the orbital angular momentum, defined precisely
below in Eq. (
9.3
).

Figure 9.1:
Consistently normalized signal and noise comparison using the
updated detector spectra. Noise is
h_{n}=\sqrt{fS_{n}}
; binary signals are

h_{c}=2f|\widetilde{h}|
. All signals are at luminosity distance 50 Mpc and
use IMRPhenomD, equal masses, face-on inclination, and antenna factor 1.
The three panels give detector-frame total masses. Green curves compare
equal component spins
\chi_{1z}=\chi_{2z}=\chi_{\rm eff}=-0.8,0,+0.8
;
blue and orange curves show the two full-noise spectra.
All quantities on the ordinate are dimensionless.

9.2.2
Spin dependence and matched-filter SNR at 50 Mpc

For a Fourier-domain projected strain
\widetilde{h}(f)
and a one-sided
noise PSD
S_{n}(f)
, the matched-filter SNR
\rho
is

\rho^{2}=4\int_{0.1\,\mathrm{Hz}}^{10\,\mathrm{Hz}}\frac{|\widetilde{h}(f)|^{2}}{S_{n}(f)}\,df=\int\left(\frac{h_{c}}{h_{n}}\right)^{2}d\ln f.

(9.2)

The noise is squared ASD, and the signal and noise use the same strain
calibration. Antenna or orientation factors must not be applied twice.

Figures
9.1
and
9.2
use LALSuite
IMRPhenomD
[
74
]
, a dominant-quadrupole model for
quasi-circular binaries with spins parallel or antiparallel to the orbital
angular momentum. We compare equal-mass binaries at
D_{L}=50
Mpc with
face-on inclination and effective antenna response 1. For binary component

i=1,2
, let
m_{i}
be its mass,
\bm{S}_{i}
its spin angular momentum,
and
\widehat{\bm{L}}
the unit vector along the orbital angular
momentum. The dimensionless aligned-spin component
\chi_{iz}
and
mass-weighted effective spin
\chi_{\rm eff}
are

\chi_{iz}=\frac{c\,\bm{S}_{i}\cdot\widehat{\bm{L}}}{Gm_{i}^{2}},\qquad\chi_{\rm eff}=\frac{m_{1}\chi_{1z}+m_{2}\chi_{2z}}{m_{1}+m_{2}}.

(9.3)

We set
\chi_{1z}=\chi_{2z}=-0.8,0,+0.8
, so that these values also label

\chi_{\rm eff}
. Positive spin denotes alignment with the orbit. These
examples lie within the equal-mass calibration range of IMRPhenomD

[
74
]
; they do not cover spin precession, unequal masses,
eccentricity, or higher multipoles. The full passage
through 0.1–10 Hz is included, without duty-cycle or finite observing-window
corrections. The mass is detector-frame mass and the distance is luminosity
distance; no source-frame conversion or population model is imposed.
The archived input spectra and derived values accompany this White Paper.

with

. The final column rescales the same
waveform to the illustrative
\rho=8
horizon.

CBC matched-filter SNR at 50 Mpc as a function of detector-frame
total mass, for the two operating points and the three equal-component
spin choices in Table
9.1
. The horizontal line marks the
illustrative
\rho=8
threshold. All curves use the full one-sided noise
PSD and integration over 0.1–10 Hz.

Aligned spins shift the merger and ringdown to higher frequencies at fixed
total mass, changing their overlap with the detector band. In the examples
computed here, the maximum SNR at 50 Mpc rises from 1.55 to 3.36 for the
minimum-PI point, and from 1.61 to 3.48 for the logarithmic-mean point, as

\chi_{\rm eff}
changes from
-0.8
to
+0.8
. The mass of maximum SNR
also increases (Table
9.1
). This is a result of the full
waveform integral, not a spin correction to the Schwarzschild ISCO estimate.

9.2.3
Mass–distance SNR contours

Figure
9.3
extends the calculation to luminosity
distance
D_{L}
. Define the reference distance
D_{0}=50
Mpc and

\rho_{0}(M_{z},\chi_{\rm eff})
as the SNR at that distance, using the
equal-mass, equal-spin waveforms and noise spectra specified above.
At fixed detector-frame total mass
M_{z}
and spin, the waveform amplitude
scales as
D_{L}^{-1}
. For a target SNR
\rho_{\star}
, the distance contour

D_{L}^{(\rho_{\star})}
therefore follows

\rho(M_{z},D_{L},\chi_{\rm eff})=\rho_{0}(M_{z},\chi_{\rm eff})\frac{D_{0}}{D_{L}},\qquad D_{L}^{(\rho_{\star})}(M_{z},\chi_{\rm eff})=D_{0}\frac{\rho_{0}(M_{z},\chi_{\rm eff})}{\rho_{\star}}.

(9.4)

The columns compare the two optimized operating points and the rows the
three spin choices. The contours show
\rho_{\star}=1,3,5,8
; the 50 Mpc
line is the slice plotted in Fig.
9.2
. Distance is varied
without changing detector-frame mass, so no source-frame mass or redshift
conversion is implicit in this map.

Figure 9.3:
Mass–distance contours of CBC matched-filter SNR. Columns show
the minimum-PI and minimum-logarithmic-mean operating points; rows show

\chi_{1z}=\chi_{2z}=\chi_{\rm eff}=-0.8,0,+0.8
. The color scale gives

\rho
and white contours mark
\rho=1,3,5,8
, with the
\rho=8
contour
emphasized. Dashed lines mark 50 Mpc. All panels use equal-mass, face-on
IMRPhenomD waveforms, antenna response 1, the full noise spectra of
Chap.
3
, and integration over 0.1–10 Hz.
The horizontal axis is detector-frame total mass.

The
nonspinning
\rho=8
horizons remain 12.9 and 13.4 Mpc; the aligned

\chi_{\rm eff}=+0.8
examples reach 21.0 and 21.7 Mpc, respectively.
Even these examples stay below
\rho=8
at 50 Mpc. A real search
threshold depends on data quality, the template bank, and the measured
background. Neither this threshold nor
\rho=1
establishes a detection
probability or an event rate. The projected distances concern nearby
systems; they do not imply direct observations of seed formation at high
redshift. A population interpretation requires inclination and sky averaging,
a source-frame mass distribution, and an observing-time selection function.

Multiband observations require adequate SNR in each participating instrument.
Low-mass binaries may evolve into the band of conventional terrestrial
detectors; more massive systems may connect a space-based inspiral to a
low-frequency coalescence. Residence times and early-warning intervals
depend on mass and entry frequency, and are not automatically months long
in the 0.1–10 Hz band.

9.3
Stochastic gravitational-wave backgrounds

Unresolved compact binaries and early-Universe processes can produce SGWBs.
Their detectability requires a specified spectrum, amplitude, and response.
Let
\rho_{\rm GW}
be the present GW energy density,
\rho_{c}
the
critical energy density, and
H_{0}
the present Hubble expansion rate. The
dimensionless energy-density spectrum
\Omega_{\rm GW}
is defined by

\Omega_{\rm GW}(f)=\frac{1}{\rho_{c}}\frac{d\rho_{\rm GW}}{d\ln f},\qquad\rho_{c}=\frac{3H_{0}^{2}c^{2}}{8\pi G},

(9.5)

where both densities are energy densities. The one-sided tensor-background
strain convention used here is

S_{h}^{\rm GW}(f)=\frac{3H_{0}^{2}}{2\pi^{2}}\frac{\Omega_{\rm GW}(f)}{f^{3}}.

(9.6)

9.3.1
The common PI comparison metric

Chapter
3
uses a single-detector, weak-signal,
perfectly known-noise metric with response
\mathcal{R}=1
, SNR 1,

H_{0}=67.4~\mathrm{km\,s^{-1}\,Mpc^{-1}}
, and integration over 0.1–10 Hz.
Let
T
denote effective observing time in seconds,
\rho_{\star}
the
target SNR,
f_{\rm ref}
a reference frequency, and
\beta
a power-law
slope. For a spectrum
\Omega_{\beta}(f/f_{\rm ref})^{\beta}
,
\Omega_{\beta}

is its amplitude at
f_{\rm ref}
. Define
\Omega_{n}
as the equivalent
noise energy-density spectrum,
\Omega_{\beta}^{\rm thr}
as the threshold
amplitude, and
\Omega_{\rm PI}
as the power-law-integrated envelope:

\displaystyle\Omega_{n}(f)

\displaystyle=\frac{2\pi^{2}}{3H_{0}^{2}}\frac{f^{3}S_{n}(f)}{\mathcal{R}},

(9.7)

\displaystyle\Omega_{\beta}^{\rm thr}

\displaystyle=\frac{\rho_{\star}}{\sqrt{T}}\left[\int_{0.1\,\mathrm{Hz}}^{10\,\mathrm{Hz}}\frac{(f/f_{\rm ref})^{2\beta}}{\Omega_{n}^{2}(f)}\,df\right]^{-1/2},

(9.8)

\displaystyle\Omega_{\rm PI}(f)

\displaystyle=\max_{\beta}\left[\Omega_{\beta}^{\rm thr}(f/f_{\rm ref})^{\beta}\right].

(9.9)

The envelope uses
-8\leq\beta\leq 8
and
f_{\rm ref}=1
Hz

[
18
,
14
]
. It is not a per-bin limit.
Peaked or broken spectra must be integrated with their actual shape.

The common one-year PI curves are shown once in
Fig.
3.3
. For ten years of effective observing time,
the same curves scale downward by
1/\sqrt{10}
under the known-noise
assumptions above. The response
\mathcal{R}=1
is a comparison convention,
not an independently calculated isotropic CHRONOS response.

The minimum-PI reference has
\min\Omega_{\rm PI}=4.0142\times 10^{-4}

for one year and
1.2694\times 10^{-4}
for ten years. Its reported pivot is
4.687 Hz. The logarithmic-mean setting gives
5.9611\times 10^{-4}
and

1.8851\times 10^{-4}
, respectively, with pivot 1.821 Hz. These values
replace the earlier ten-year estimate used in this chapter.

9.3.2
From a comparison metric to an SGWB measurement

A robust stochastic measurement requires control of noise bias. For two
channels with independent instrumental noise, let
C_{12}^{\rm GW}
be
their GW cross-PSD and
\Gamma_{12}
their dimensionless overlap response,
so that
C_{12}^{\rm GW}=\Gamma_{12}S_{h}^{\rm GW}
. Let
P_{1},P_{2}
be the
channel noise PSDs and
\rho_{12}
the cross-correlation SNR. The weak-signal statistic is

\rho_{12}^{2}=2T\int df\,\frac{|\Gamma_{12}(f)|^{2}[S_{h}^{\rm GW}(f)]^{2}}{P_{1}(f)P_{2}(f)}.

(9.10)

Here
P_{1},P_{2}
are one-sided noise PSDs and
\Gamma_{12}
includes geometry,
angular averaging, and separation
[
75
]
. Separate optical
readouts of shared mechanics need not have independent noise. The
single-detector PI benchmark must not be presented as a demonstrated
cross-correlation sensitivity without this calculation and a correlated-noise
assessment.

CMB constraints on the tensor-to-scalar ratio and direct interferometer
constraints apply at very different scales. For example, the BK18 bound

r_{0.05}<0.036
at 95% confidence is specified at a pivot wavenumber

0.05~\mathrm{Mpc}^{-1}

[
76
]
; extrapolation requires a primordial
spectrum and transfer history. NANOGrav’s 15-year evidence does not establish
a unique cosmological origin or a hertz-band extrapolation
[
77
]
.
The baseline here does not establish detectability of nearly scale-invariant
inflationary vacuum fluctuations. Phase transitions, strings, or enhanced
primordial spectra require model-specific comparisons with the integrated
statistic and with existing bounds, as discussed in Chap.
10
.

Chapter 10  Cosmology

Primordial gravitational waves are a unique observational
window into the physics of the early Universe. Unlike electromagnetic radiation, gravitational waves propagate
essentially freely once produced and therefore preserve direct
information about the physical processes occurring at extremely
high energy scales.
The detection of primordial gravitational waves would therefore
provide powerful insight into the origin of cosmic structure
and the fundamental physics governing the earliest moments
of the Universe.

One of the most compelling targets of modern cosmology is the
gravitational-wave background generated during the inflationary
epoch.
Quantum fluctuations of the spacetime metric during inflation
produce tensor perturbations that subsequently propagate as
relic gravitational waves.
The amplitude of these perturbations is commonly parameterized
by the tensor-to-scalar ratio
r
, which quantifies the
relative strength of tensor
compared to scalar fluctuations.

The most sensitive probe of primordial tensor perturbations
is the observation of the cosmic microwave
background (CMB) polarization. Primordial gravitational waves generate a characteristic
B-mode polarization pattern in the CMB, providing a direct
observable signature of tensor perturbations.
Experiments such as
Planck
and BICEP/Keck have
therefore placed increasingly stringent limits on the
tensor amplitude at cosmological scales.

While CMB observations probe gravitational waves at extremely
large cosmological scales, corresponding to frequencies near

f\sim 10^{-17}\,\mathrm{Hz}
,
all the other gravitational-wave
detectors probe dramatically higher frequencies.
Ground-based interferometers such as LIGO and Virgo are
sensitive to stochastic gravitational-wave backgrounds in
the frequency band around tens to hundreds of hertz, while
future space-based detectors will explore the millihertz
band.

One of the key quantities describing the primordial tensor spectrum
is the tensor spectral index
n_{t}
, which characterizes
the scale dependence of the gravitational-wave amplitude.
While many inflationary models predict a slightly
red-tilted spectrum, a variety of early-Universe scenarios
allow a positive tensor spectral index.
Such a blue-tilted spectrum corresponds to a gravitational-wave
energy density that increases toward higher frequencies.

Blue-tilted spectra are particularly
interesting: the enhanced power of gravitational waves at high frequencies can bring primordial signals within the
sensitivity range of
other detectors such as CHRONOS or interferometers even when the tensor amplitude at the CMB scales is small.
This makes gravitational-wave observations across multiple
frequency bands a powerful probe of early-Universe physics.

In this context, gravitational-wave detectors operating
in the sub-Hz frequency band can play a critical role.
This frequency range bridges the gap between space-based
and ground-based interferometers and provides a key window
for probing blue-tilted primordial spectra.
The CHRONOS experiment is designed to explore this
frequency band with high sensitivity, enabling
cosmological measurements that are complementary to
both CMB observations and higher-frequency
gravitational-wave detectors.

In this chapter, we describe the cosmological framework
used to connect primordial tensor perturbations with
present-day gravitational-wave observations and
investigate how multi-band measurements can constrain
blue-tilted spectra.

10.1
Basic principle of the analysis

The stochastic gravitational-wave background provides a statistical probe of primordial gravitational
waves generated in the early Universe.
Unlike transient signals produced by individual
astrophysical events, the primordial background is
expected to consist of the superposition of a large
number of independent sources and is therefore
characterized statistically through its spectral
energy density.

Let
k
be comoving wavenumber,
f
observed frequency,
c
the speed
of light,
a_{0}
the present scale factor, and
H_{0}
the present Hubble rate.
The dimensionless primordial tensor power spectrum
P_{t}(k)
has amplitude

A_{t}
at pivot wavenumber
k_{*}
and spectral index
n_{t}
:

P_{t}(k)=A_{t}\left(\frac{k}{k_{*}}\right)^{n_{t}},

(10.1)

where
A_{t}
is the tensor amplitude evaluated at the
pivot scale
k_{*}
and
n_{t}
denotes the tensor
spectral index.
The pivot scale is typically chosen as

k_{*}\simeq 0.05\,{\rm Mpc}^{-1},

which is commonly adopted in CMB analyses.

After their generation, primordial tensor perturbations
propagate through the expanding Universe.
Their evolution is described by the gravitational-wave
transfer function
T_{h}(k)
, which accounts for the
horizon re-entry of each mode and the expansion history
of the Universe.

The present-day gravitational-wave energy density
spectrum is related to the primordial tensor spectrum
through

\Omega_{\rm GW}(f)=\frac{1}{12}\left(\frac{ck}{a_{0}H_{0}}\right)^{2}P_{t}(k)T_{h}^{2}(k),

(10.2)

where
\Omega_{\rm GW}
is the GW energy density per logarithmic
frequency divided by the critical energy density, as in
Sec.
9.3
. The amplitude transfer
T_{h}
is normalized to
unit primordial tensor amplitude.

The comoving wavenumber
k
is related to the observed
gravitational-wave frequency through

k=2\pi fa_{0}/c.

(10.3)

This relation provides the link between the primordial
tensor spectrum defined at cosmological scales and the
gravitational-wave energy density measured by
interferometric detectors.

Measurements of
\Omega_{\rm GW}(f)
at interferometer
frequencies therefore provide complementary information
to CMB observations and enable constraints to be placed
on the scale dependence of primordial tensor
perturbations.

10.1.1
Transfer function in radiation- and matter-dominated eras

The amplitude transfer function
T_{h}(k)
describes the evolution of
primordial tensor perturbations after horizon re-entry and
encodes the effect of the cosmological expansion history.
Modes entering the horizon during the radiation-dominated
epoch evolve differently from those entering during the
matter-dominated epoch.
This produces a scale-dependent modification of the
primordial tensor spectrum that must be included when
computing the present-day gravitational-wave energy
density.

For a compact energy-density approximation, define a different,
dimensionless transfer factor
\mathcal{T}_{\Omega}(k)
. It incorporates
both
T_{h}
and the frequency prefactor above. Let
A_{s}
denote the primordial
scalar curvature-power amplitude at
k_{*}
, and
\Omega_{r}
the present
radiation density parameter. Then

\Omega_{\rm GW}(k)=\Omega_{\rm ref}\left(\frac{k}{k_{*}}\right)^{n_{t}}\mathcal{T}_{\Omega}^{2}(k),

(10.4)

where the normalization set by CMB parameters is

\Omega_{\rm ref}=\frac{3}{128}\,rA_{s}\,\Omega_{r}.

(10.5)

Here
\Omega_{r}
denotes the present radiation density
parameter and
k_{*}
is the pivot scale used in CMB
analyses.

The labels RD and MD denote radiation and matter domination. Define

k_{\rm eq}
as the comoving horizon wavenumber at matter–radiation equality.
The corresponding contributions to
\mathcal{T}_{\Omega}
are approximated by

\mathcal{T}_{\Omega}^{2}(k)=\mathcal{T}_{\rm MD}^{2}(k)+\mathcal{T}_{\rm RD}^{2}.

(10.6)

For modes that re-enter the horizon during the
matter-dominated era, the transfer function scales as

\mathcal{T}_{\rm MD}^{2}(k)=\frac{1}{2}\left(\frac{k_{\rm eq}}{k}\right)^{2},

(10.7)

where
k_{\rm eq}
is the comoving horizon scale at
the matter-radiation equality.

For modes entering during the radiation-dominated era,
the transfer function approaches a constant value,

\mathcal{T}_{\rm RD}^{2}=\frac{16}{9}.

(10.8)

Combining these contributions yields the approximate
expression

\Omega_{\rm GW}(k)=\Omega_{\rm ref}\left(\frac{k}{k_{*}}\right)^{n_{t}}\left[\frac{1}{2}\left(\frac{k_{\rm eq}}{k}\right)^{2}+\frac{16}{9}\right].

(10.9)

This analytic expression provides a convenient model
for the primordial gravitational-wave spectrum over a
wide range of frequencies and is widely used in studies
of multi-band gravitational-wave cosmology.

10.2
Cosmological model and assumptions

The cosmological analysis presented in this work is
performed within the framework of the standard

\Lambda
CDM cosmology extended to include primordial
tensor perturbations.

The parameter set used in the analysis is

\Theta=\{\Omega_{b},\Omega_{c},h,\tau,n_{s},A_{s},r,n_{t}\},

(10.10)

where
\Omega_{b}
and
\Omega_{c}
denote the present
baryon and cold dark-matter density parameters,

h
is the dimensionless Hubble parameter defined by

H_{0}=100\,h\,{\rm km\,s^{-1}\,Mpc^{-1}},

and
\tau
is the optical depth to reionization.

The scalar perturbations are characterized by the
spectral index
n_{s}
and the amplitude
A_{s}
of the
primordial curvature power spectrum.

The tensor sector is described by the tensor-to-scalar
ratio
r
and the tensor spectral index
n_{t}
.
The amplitude of the primordial tensor spectrum is
related to the scalar amplitude through

A_{t}=rA_{s}.

(10.11)

In the simplest slow-roll inflation models, the tensor
spectral index satisfies the consistency relation

n_{t}=-r/8
, i.e., the spectrum is slightly red-tilted (
n_{t}<0
). In this work, however, we do not impose this relation
and instead treat
n_{t}
as an independent free parameter.
This allows us to explore a wider class of early-Universe
scenarios and to test both red-tilted (
n_{t}<0
) and
blue-tilted (
n_{t}>0
) tensor spectra.

10.2.1
Blue-tilted tensor spectrum

While many slow-roll inflation models predict a
slightly red-tilted tensor spectrum, a variety of
early-Universe scenarios allow a positive tensor
spectral index (
n_{t}>0
).
Examples include inflationary models with
nonstandard dynamics, non-Bunch-Davies initial states,
particle production during inflation,
and alternative early-Universe scenarios such as
ekpyrotic cosmology
(see, e.g., Refs.
[
78
,
79
,
80
]
).
Generalized G-inflation provides the most general framework
of inflation models with second-order field equations, which
can accommodate the blue spectrum.
[
81
]
.

A positive tensor spectral index implies that the
gravitational-wave energy density increases toward
higher frequencies.
