\hat{a}
and
\hat{b}
denote the signal and LO field operators, respectively. For an ideal 50/50 beamsplitter, the output fields may be written as

\hat{c}=\frac{\hat{a}+\hat{b}}{\sqrt{2}},\qquad\hat{d}=\frac{\hat{b}-\hat{a}}{\sqrt{2}}.

(14.15)

The difference in detected photon number is

\hat{n}_{-}=\hat{c}^{\dagger}\hat{c}-\hat{d}^{\dagger}\hat{d}=\hat{a}^{\dagger}\hat{b}+\hat{b}^{\dagger}\hat{a}.

(14.16)

For a strong coherent LO,
\hat{b}\simeq\beta=|\beta|e^{i\zeta}
, the differential signal becomes

\hat{n}_{-}\simeq 2|\beta|\hat{X}_{\zeta},

(14.17)

where

\hat{X}_{\zeta}=\frac{\hat{a}e^{-i\zeta}+\hat{a}^{\dagger}e^{i\zeta}}{2}

(14.18)

is the quadrature selected by the LO phase
\zeta
.

For classical optical powers, the ideal differential photocurrent is

i_{-}=2\mathcal{R}_{\mathrm{pd}}\sqrt{P_{\mathrm{LO}}P_{s}}\cos\zeta,

(14.19)

where
\mathcal{R}_{\mathrm{pd}}
is the photodiode responsivity, and
P_{\mathrm{LO}}
and
P_{s}
are the LO and signal powers. This expression assumes ideal interference visibility and matched photodiode responses.

The one-sided shot-noise current power spectral density is

S_{i}^{\mathrm{shot}}=2e(I_{1}+I_{2})\simeq 2e\mathcal{R}_{\mathrm{pd}}P_{\mathrm{LO}},

(14.20)

when the LO dominates the detected power. A sufficiently strong LO allows the optical signal and shot-noise level to rise above the electronic-noise floor, but does not itself remove quantum shot noise.

14.3.2
Readout Angle

The principal advantage of BHD is the ability to select the measured quadrature through the relative phase between the LO and signal fields. Using amplitude and phase quadratures
b_{1}
and
b_{2}
, the measured quadrature is

b_{\zeta}=b_{1}\cos\zeta+b_{2}\sin\zeta.

(14.21)

The optimal readout angle depends on the interferometer response and the frequency-dependent quantum-noise budget. In particular, the selected quadrature determines how the signal and quantum-noise contributions are projected onto the detected output
[
106
]
.

Here
\zeta
is the local quadrature angle measured from the
b_{1}
axis.
It is not the mirror/LO setting
\zeta_{\rm LO}
in
Table
3.3
. Conversion to that setting
must include the carrier propagation phase; a numerical angle cannot be
copied between the two conventions without that conversion.

Fluctuations in the relative LO - signal phase cause mixing between the measured and orthogonal quadratures. For a small phase fluctuation
\delta\zeta
,

\delta b_{\zeta}\simeq b_{\zeta+\pi/2}\delta\zeta.

(14.22)

Careful control of the homodyne angle is therefore required to maintain optimal sensitivity across the measurement band. Stable control of this phase is essential, as fluctuations in the readout angle can lead to mixing of unwanted noise quadratures into the detected signal.

14.3.3
Optical Design

The optical layout of the BHD system is shown in Fig.
14.2
. The local oscillator is derived from a clean reference beam picked-off before the main interferometer, and routed to the homodyne beamsplitter with carefully controlled path length. The local oscillator and signal beam from the OMC is then mixed at the homodyne beamsplitter, and the two output ports are measured using photodiodes. The photocurrents are then subtracted to obtain the differential signal.

Particular attention is given to balancing optical power and path length on the two photodiodes, as any imbalance reduces the effectiveness of common-mode noise cancellation.

Figure 14.2:
Schematic diagram of balanced homodyne detection(BHD) readout scheme in the scheme of other CHRONOS subsystems to show the overall setup.

14.3.4
Future Upgrade

The use of squeezed states of light at the output port offers a method to reduce quantum noise in the CHRONOS readout. In particular, frequency-dependent squeezing could be employed to simultaneously suppress shot noise at higher frequencies and radiation-pressure noise at lower frequencies
[
106
]
. Implementing such a scheme would require an additional filter cavity to rotate the squeezing angle as a function of frequency before injection into the homodyne detector.

Although not part of the baseline design, the output optics layout may be designed with sufficient flexibility to accommodate future integration of squeezed light sources and associated filter cavities. This forward-compatible approach ensures that upgrades aimed at quantum noise reduction can be incorporated without major redesign of the detection setup.

Chapter 15  Cavity Control

Before discussing the cavity-control architecture of the interferometer, it is useful to clarify the design philosophy that guided the selection of the optical topology, cavity lengths, recycling configuration, and control sidebands adopted in this study. The ultimate goal of CHRONOS is the realization of a large-scale sub-hertz gravitational-wave detector. The interferometer described here is therefore designed to validate the key optical and control concepts required for such a detector while remaining experimentally feasible in a laboratory environment
[
7
]
.

Stable operation requires simultaneous control of multiple coupled optical cavities, precise mode matching, and robust control of the interferometer operating point. The primary objective of the cavity-control system is consequently to maintain the resonance conditions of the Sagnac cavity and the recycling cavities while providing stable references for balanced-homodyne readout and quantum-noise measurements.

15.1
Operation method

CHRONOS employs a dual-recycled triangular-ring Sagnac topology
[
10
]
. The system is a laboratory-scale platform for validating optical stability, mode matching, cavity-length control, recycling-cavity phase control, and quantum-noise readout
[
107
,
97
]
. Its differential Sagnac observable is velocity-like, or equivalently angular-momentum-like, rather than a direct position-displacement observable. This speed-meter response suppresses radiation-pressure noise at low frequencies and is central to the intended sub-hertz application
[
10
]
.

The frequency architecture is described first, followed by the sensing ports, degree-of-freedom definitions, and lock-acquisition sequence.

15.1.1
Sideband frequencies and frequency architecture

The RF-sideband frequencies are selected to provide the resonance and anti-resonance conditions needed to sense the power-recycling cavity (PRC), signal-recycling cavity (SRC), and triangular Sagnac cavity independently. The starting point is the round-trip length of the Sagnac ring cavity,

L_{\mathrm{ring}}=5.08~\mathrm{m},

(15.1)

which gives

\mathrm{FSR}_{\mathrm{ring}}=\frac{c}{L_{\mathrm{ring}}}\simeq 59.0~\mathrm{MHz}.

(15.2)

This frequency is the fundamental reference for the sideband scheme.

Signal-recycling cavity

The effective SRC length is chosen as

L_{\mathrm{src}}\simeq 5.08~\mathrm{m},\qquad\mathrm{FSR}_{\mathrm{src}}\simeq\frac{c}{2L_{\mathrm{src}}}=29.5~\mathrm{MHz}.

(15.3)

Thus, the SRC free spectral range is one half of the ring-cavity free spectral range.

Power-recycling cavity

The PRC length is set to

L_{\mathrm{prc}}\simeq 7.62~\mathrm{m},\qquad\mathrm{FSR}_{\mathrm{prc}}\simeq\frac{c}{2L_{\mathrm{prc}}}=19.7~\mathrm{MHz}.

(15.4)

These cavity lengths reproduce the resonance hierarchy used in large-scale gravitational-wave detectors. Although the present interferometer is approximately 2.5 m in scale, the relevant optical phase relationships are equivalent to those of kilometre-scale instruments, permitting a realistic validation of the cavity-control architecture
[
12
,
13
]
.

15.1.2
RF sidebands and degree-of-freedom separation

Two RF sidebands are used for main-interferometer control:

f_{1}=29.5~\mathrm{MHz},\qquad f_{2}=59.0~\mathrm{MHz}.

(15.5)

The frequency
f_{1}
equals the SRC free spectral range, whereas
f_{2}
equals the ring-cavity free spectral range. In the coupled-cavity system,
f_{2}
is resonant in the PRC and anti-resonant in the SRC. The resulting distinct optical responses provide discrimination among the recycling-cavity and Sagnac degrees of freedom with only two principal modulation frequencies. The sensing assignment is not inferred from the nominal FSR alone; it is fixed by the complete coupled-cavity response and the detection port, as summarized in Table
15.1
.

15.1.3
Input mode cleaner compatibility

The input mode cleaner (IMC) is a four-mirror suspended bow-tie cavity that acts as both a spatial-mode filter and a laser-frequency reference
[
108
]
. Its round-trip length and free spectral range are

L_{\mathrm{IMC}}=10.2~\mathrm{m},\qquad\mathrm{FSR}_{\mathrm{IMC}}\simeq 14.8~\mathrm{MHz}.

(15.6)

A dedicated phase-modulation frequency,

f_{\mathrm{IMC}}=44.2~\mathrm{MHz},

(15.7)

is used to derive the IMC Pound–Drever–Hall (PDH) error signal
[
107
,
97
]
. The frequency plan permits the main control sidebands at
f_{1}
and
f_{2}
to pass through the IMC with minimal attenuation while preserving laser-frequency stabilization.

15.1.4
Design philosophy summary

The cavity-control design is based on the ring FSR, distinct resonance conditions in the PRC and SRC, port-dependent sensing with two phase-coherent RF sidebands, and compatibility with the IMC. Together, these elements provide a laboratory-scale implementation of the optical and control relationships required by the full-scale CHRONOS concept.

15.1.5
Control ports and control scheme

The longitudinal control degrees of freedom are the input-mode-cleaner length (IMCL), power-recycling-cavity length (PRCL), signal-recycling-cavity length (SRCL), common Sagnac mode (CSAG), and differential Sagnac mode (DSAG). Dedicated sensing ports distributed through the interferometer provide the error signals needed for cavity locking, alignment control, and balanced-homodyne operation. Their physical locations are shown in Fig.
11.1
.

Initial alignment and control

Lock acquisition begins by establishing a stable spatial and frequency reference. Mirror positions and orientations for each cavity, together with the input steering mirrors, are adjusted to optimize beam pointing and mode matching. The IMC is the first cavity to be locked. Its reflected field is demodulated at 44.2 MHz to obtain the PDH length signal. The suspended IMC mirrors, denoted
IMC1
–
IMC4
in Fig.
11.1
, are actuated with coil–magnet actuators, providing low-frequency control authority while retaining mechanical isolation.

The transmitted IMC beam provides the downstream spatial-mode and frequency reference. Its mode quality and intensity are monitored continuously, and its low-frequency component can be used for offset compensation in the laser-frequency stabilization loop
[
108
]
. The IMC RF reference is phase-locked to the sources at
f_{1}=29.5~\mathrm{MHz}
and
f_{2}=59.0~\mathrm{MHz}
so that the REFL and POP demodulation channels share a coherent phase reference. Completion of this stage prepares the system for green pre-lock and subsequent acquisition of the coupled PRC, SRC, and Sagnac cavities.

Control ports and optical readout scheme

Reflection port (REFL)

The
REFL_{j}
port, where
j\in\{X,Y\}
, detects light reflected from the input mirror of the PRC. Demodulation at
f_{1}=29.5~\mathrm{MHz}
supplies the primary PRCL error signal by the PDH technique. The same port also monitors CSAG, the average optical path length of the clockwise (CW) and counter-clockwise (CCW) fields, and contributes to overall resonance and laser-frequency stabilization.

Pick-off port (POP)

The
POP_{j}
port samples the intracavity PRC field
[
109
]
. Two photodiodes at distinct Gouy phases separate the relevant spatial and longitudinal responses:

•

POP_{j}
-A (near field):
sensitive to CSAG and to PRCL as an auxiliary channel for common-path and laser-frequency stabilization;

•

POP_{j}
-B (far field):
displaced by a Gouy phase of approximately
45^{\circ}
–
90^{\circ}
relative to
POP_{j}
-A and primarily sensitive to DSAG.

Both channels are demodulated at
f_{2}=59.0~\mathrm{MHz}
. Their Gouy-phase separation helps discriminate CSAG, PRCL, and DSAG.

Antisymmetric port (AS)

The
AS_{j}
port is the nominally dark output port of the Sagnac interferometer. Demodulation at
f_{2}=59.0~\mathrm{MHz}
provides the primary SRCL error signal
[
110
]
. The
AS_{X}
and
AS_{Y}
fields are combined at the output beam splitter and decomposed into common and differential components. The differential component carries the science observable, is passed through the output mode cleaner, and is read out as DSAG by balanced-homodyne detection at the AS-BHD port
[
111
,
112
]
.

Comparison with a Fabry–Perot Michelson interferometer

CSAG is analogous to the common-arm-length degree of freedom (CARM) of a Michelson interferometer and represents the common optical path coupled to laser frequency and PRC resonance. DSAG is analogous to the differential-arm-length degree of freedom (DARM), but it does not represent a direct position displacement. Instead, it is an angular-momentum- or velocity-like observable arising from the difference between the CW and CCW propagation responses
[
10
]
.

Lock acquisition strategy

Lock acquisition proceeds in the following stages:

1.

lock the IMC and complete the initial alignment, establishing the spatial-mode, phase, and laser-frequency reference;

2.

apply the green pre-lock to establish the initial Sagnac-cavity resonance condition;

3.

acquire the coupled cavities, including PRCL and SRCL, using the REFL, POP, and AS error signals while controlling CSAG and DSAG;

4.

bring the interferometer to the dark-fringe operating point and transition the DSAG science channel to DC balanced-homodyne readout.

Loop gains, bandwidths, and demodulation phases are adjusted during the sequence to limit cross-coupling among the strongly coupled degrees of freedom. The final operating state maintains the Sagnac and recycling-cavity resonances while providing the stable local-oscillator phase and dark-port conditions required for quantum-noise and speed-meter measurements.

Chapter 16  Vibration Isolation System

16.1
Design objectives

The CHRONOS detector measures the differential yaw motion of two orthogonal
torsion-bar end test masses (ETMs) in the sub-Hz band. Its suspension system must
therefore satisfy two requirements that are unusually difficult to combine.
First, it must provide sufficient low-frequency isolation to prevent ground
translation and rotation from contaminating the differential yaw signal.
Second, it must retain the actuation range required for alignment, damping,
interferometer lock acquisition, and recovery from large disturbances. The
baseline design separates these functions among a room-temperature seismic
isolation system, a common multistage geometric anti-spring (GAS) chain, and
two low-temperature suspension payloads.

The present chapter defines a preliminary design suitable for a conceptual or
preliminary design review. Numerical values labeled as requirements are
initial allocations rather than final component specifications. They will be
updated after the full six-degree-of-freedom mechanical model and the measured
site ground-motion spectrum become available.

The reference science band and minimum-PI strain ASD from
Chap.
3
are

0.1~\mathrm{Hz}\leq f\leq 10~\mathrm{Hz},\qquad S_{h}^{1/2}(2~\mathrm{Hz})=8.31\times 10^{-19}/\sqrt{\mathrm{Hz}},

(16.1)

with the most demanding isolation performance expected between approximately
0.1 and 3 Hz. Below 0.1 Hz, displacement range, alignment control, and robust
recovery are given priority over aggressive inertial isolation.

16.2
Baseline vibration-isolation architecture

Figure
16.1
defines the physical hierarchy and
nomenclature used throughout this chapter. Ground motion first enters the
four-stage internal seismic isolation (ISI) assembly. Stage-0 is the
ground-side, large-range platform and provides coarse position and attitude
control. It also forms the mechanical suspension head for GAS-4. Stages 1
and 2 provide successive isolation between Stage-0 and the precision optical
platform, Stage-3. The yaw-sensitive Sagnac optical system is mounted on
Stage-3 so that its optical reference is stabilized by the most strongly
isolated ISI stage. Independently of this optical branch, Stage-0 supports
four common geometric anti-spring filters, ordered from top to bottom as
GAS-4, GAS-3, GAS-2, and GAS-1. GAS-1 supports the common cryogenic platform
mass (PFM). At the PFM, the load path divides into nominally identical
x

and
y
cryogenic payloads.

Figure 16.1:
Conceptual architecture and component nomenclature of the CHRONOS
vibration-isolation and cryogenic suspension system. The room-temperature
ISI assembly comprises the coarse-control Stage-0 and the successively
isolated Stages 1–3. The yaw Sagnac is mounted on the precision optical
platform, Stage-3, while Stage-0 supports GAS-4.
The common GAS-4–GAS-1 chain provides multistage passive isolation and
supports the cryogenic PFM. Below the PFM, the suspension divides into

x
and
y
main chains (MNIMx/MNIMy, IMMx/IMMy, and ETMx/ETMy) and corresponding
recoil chains (MNRMx/MNRMy and IMRMx/IMRMy).

The baseline architecture is therefore

\begin{gathered}\text{ground}\rightarrow\text{Stage-0 coarse platform}\rightarrow\begin{cases}\text{Stage-1}\rightarrow\text{Stage-2}\rightarrow\text{Stage-3 + Sagnac},\\
\text{GAS-4}\rightarrow\text{GAS-3}\rightarrow\text{GAS-2}\rightarrow\text{GAS-1}\\
\rightarrow\text{common cryogenic PFM}\\
\rightarrow\left\{\begin{aligned} \mathrm{MNIM}_{x}\rightarrow\mathrm{IMM}_{x}\rightarrow\mathrm{ETM}_{x},\\
\mathrm{MNRM}_{x}\rightarrow\mathrm{IMRM}_{x},\\
\mathrm{MNIM}_{y}\rightarrow\mathrm{IMM}_{y}\rightarrow\mathrm{ETM}_{y},\\
\mathrm{MNRM}_{y}\rightarrow\mathrm{IMRM}_{y}.\end{aligned}\right.\end{cases}\end{gathered}

(16.2)

Here MNIM and IMM denote the marionette intermediate mass and intermediate
main mass; MNRM and IMRM denote the marionette recoil mass and intermediate
recoil mass; and ETM denotes the end test mass, which is the suspended
torsion bar. Lower-case subscripts
x
and
y
identify the two orthogonal
payloads and correspond to the labels MNIMx/MNIMy, IMMx/IMMy, ETMx/ETMy,
MNRMx/MNRMy, and IMRMx/IMRMy in Fig.
16.1
. The
main and recoil chains are mechanically independent below the PFM but share
the four-stage ISI, GAS chain, and PFM above their branch point. The PFM is
the sole common mechanical support and the unique
x/y
branching point. Below
it, the
x
and
y
systems are two separate suspensions: there is no mechanical
connection, common suspension element, cross-brace, or lateral link between any
corresponding
x
- and
y
-side component, including MNRMx–MNRMy,
MNIMx–MNIMy, IMRMx–IMRMy, IMMx–IMMy, and ETMx–ETMy.

Two independent GAS towers would provide a direct mechanical separation of
the
x
and
y
payloads. However, such a design would require two heavily loaded
filter chains with closely matched resonances, substantially increase the
available-space and installation requirements, and complicate load tuning.
CHRONOS therefore adopts one common GAS chain and places the X–Y branching
point at the cryogenic PFM. A common platform motion ideally cancels in the
differential readout,

(\theta_{x}-\theta_{\rm PFM})-(\theta_{y}-\theta_{\rm PFM})=\theta_{x}-\theta_{y}.

(16.3)

In practice, imperfect symmetry causes residual common-to-differential
coupling. The
x
and
y
suspension parameters and attachment geometry must
therefore be made as symmetric as practical. The PFM motion and both
PFM-to-ETM transfer functions will be measured, and the remaining common
component will be removed through sensing and actuation diagonalization.

16.3
Four-stage internal seismic isolation system

The ISI is the room-temperature active-isolation subsystem above GAS-4. Its
sensor–actuator topology follows principles demonstrated by the Advanced LIGO
BSC isolation system

[
113
]
, but the four-stage
stack and its numbering are CHRONOS-specific. Each controlled platform is
treated as a rigid body with coordinates
x
,
y
,
z
, pitch, roll, and yaw.
Relative sensors retain position and alignment at low frequency, inertial
sensors suppress motion in and above the blend region, and actuators apply the
forces and torques required for damping and isolation.

The four ISI components are defined as follows:

Stage-0

The ground-side, large-range ISI platform and suspension head.
It provides coarse translation and attitude control, supports GAS-4,
and carries the combined static load of the GAS chain, PFM, both
cryogenic payloads, recoil chains, cabling, and thermal hardware.

Stage-1

The first precision-isolation platform above Stage-0. It
reduces residual Stage-0 translation and rotation and provides the
controlled mechanical reference for Stage-2.

Stage-2

The second precision-isolation platform. It further reduces
motion transmitted from Stage-1 and provides the controlled reference
for the optical platform.

Stage-3

The final precision optical platform. It supports the
yaw-sensitive Sagnac optical system and is controlled to minimize the
translation and, especially, yaw motion of that optical reference.

Adjacent platforms are connected by compliant, frictionless suspension
elements and instrumented with collocated relative sensors and actuators where
practical. Their rigid-body resonances are actively damped. These ISI elastic
elements are functionally distinct from the softer GAS filters supported by
Stage-0: the ISI controls six-degree-of-freedom platform motion, whereas the GAS chain
provides the main cascaded passive vertical attenuation.

GAS-4 is suspended from the center region of Stage-0. The suspension head
must distribute this concentrated load into the Stage-0 structure without
excessive static distortion or excitation of internal modes. The load path
and actuator layout must remain sufficiently symmetric that they do not create
a preferred pitch, roll, or yaw direction. Mechanical stops at every moving
ISI interface protect the long suspension during installation, earthquakes,
and loss of control.

The CHRONOS-specific ISI provisions are therefore a large-range Stage-0 for
coarse control and suspension support; a Stage-1–Stage-3 precision branch for
the optical system; a yaw Sagnac mounted directly on Stage-3; adequate yaw
inertia and actuator lever arms to limit response to force imbalance; and
loaded structural modes well above the isolation-control band.

16.3.1
Stage-3 yaw Sagnac

The Sagnac interferometer is the dedicated low-frequency yaw sensor for the
precision optical system. Its optical components and beam geometry are fixed
to Stage-3, so the measured rotation is assigned to the Stage-3 optical
reference rather than to Stage-0, the GAS chain, or the PFM. The Sagnac signal
is used in the Stage-3 yaw loop, with the lower ISI stages providing the
successive mechanical pre-isolation needed to retain Stage-3 actuator range.
Stage-0 yaw control remains deliberately coarse: it keeps the GAS suspension
head within its alignment and travel envelope but is not the science-quality
optical yaw reference.

16.4
Common GAS filter chain

The passive vertical-isolation chain shown in
Fig.
16.1
consists of four room-temperature
stages named GAS-4, GAS-3, GAS-2, and GAS-1 from top to bottom. This ordering
is used for all drawings, models, channels, and requirement records. The exact
distribution along the approximately 15-m suspension length will be determined
by the vacuum-vessel and cryostat layout. The four filters share a single
central load path and support the full mass below the ISI. Multiple symmetric
blade packs may be used at each stage to obtain the required load capacity
without constructing two separately tuned towers.

Each GAS stage is tuned to a nominal vertical resonance of
0.15–0.25 Hz. Local vertical displacement sensors and voice-coil actuators
damp the high-
Q
filter resonances and provide slow height control. The local
control gain is concentrated near the filter modes and reduced rapidly above
them, limiting injection of displacement-sensor noise into the science band.
Temperature sensors on the blade assemblies monitor slow changes in stiffness
and equilibrium height.

GAS-1 interfaces the room-temperature isolation chain to the cryogenic
payload. Heat links, radiation shields, and thermal intercepts near 50 K and
4–10 K are introduced between GAS-1 and the PFM without forming a mechanically
stiff bypass around the suspension. The PFM and the payloads below it operate
at a target temperature of approximately 10 K. Thermal-link stiffness and
vibration injection must be included explicitly in the final coupled model.

16.5
Cryogenic payload and recoil chains

Below the common PFM, the suspension separates into two nominally identical
payloads for the orthogonal
x
and
y
torsion bars. The two payloads are
mechanically independent after their separate attachment points on the PFM.
Each main chain contains a marionette intermediate mass, an intermediate main
mass, and an end test mass. The two distinct main chains are

\mathrm{PFM}\rightarrow\mathrm{MNIM}_{x}\rightarrow\mathrm{IMM}_{x}\rightarrow\mathrm{ETM}_{x},\qquad\mathrm{PFM}\rightarrow\mathrm{MNIM}_{y}\rightarrow\mathrm{IMM}_{y}\rightarrow\mathrm{ETM}_{y}.

(16.4)

Their corresponding recoil chains are separately suspended from the PFM,

\mathrm{PFM}\rightarrow\mathrm{MNRM}_{x}\rightarrow\mathrm{IMRM}_{x},\qquad\mathrm{PFM}\rightarrow\mathrm{MNRM}_{y}\rightarrow\mathrm{IMRM}_{y}.

(16.5)

This organization follows the demonstrated cryogenic-payload principle of
suspending the main and recoil chains separately from a common platform and
placing relative sensors and actuators on the recoil masses

[
114
]
. CHRONOS retains that principle but uses
two orthogonal main chains below one PFM and, in the baseline design, omits a
recoil mass at each ETM. The components in
Fig.
16.1
are defined as follows:

PFM

The platform mass at the cryogenic boundary and the common
suspension point for both payloads. It receives the GAS-1 load,
distributes it symmetrically to the
x
and
y
branches, supports
cryogenic interfaces, and provides a monitored six-degree-of-freedom
reference. It is not treated as an inertial ground.

MNIMx and MNIMy

The marionette intermediate masses of the
x
and
y

main chains. Each
carries the stages below it and provides large-range alignment, modal
damping, low-frequency control, and DC offloading without direct
actuation of the associated ETM.

MNRMx and MNRMy

The marionette recoil masses. Each is suspended from
the PFM independently of its MNIM and carries the reaction-side sensors
and actuators for the corresponding MNIM. It may also receive compliant
thermal links, provided that their stiffness and injected vibration are
included in the coupled model.

IMMx and IMMy

The intermediate main masses in the two main chains. Each
supports its ETM, provides an additional pendulum-isolation stage, and
supplies fine alignment and damping authority for lower suspension
modes.

IMRMx and IMRMy

The intermediate recoil masses. They are the local
reaction bodies for IMM sensing and actuation and are suspended through
the MNRM branch rather than through the corresponding main chain.

ETMx and ETMy

The orthogonal torsion-bar end test masses and the
science inertial elements. Their differential yaw coordinate

\Delta\theta=\theta_{x}-\theta_{y}
is the primary readout. No ETM recoil
masses are included in the baseline architecture.

Relative sensors and actuators are placed between MNIMx/MNIMy and MNRMx/MNRMy and
between IMMx/IMMy and IMRMx/IMRMy. The reaction force is thereby closed mainly
inside the suspended payload rather than directly against the PFM or the
ground. The MNIM stage
provides large-range alignment, low-frequency control, damping, and DC
offloading. The IMM stage provides finer alignment and damping of the lower
suspension modes.

No recoil mass is placed around either ETM. This choice preserves the open optical
and gravitational environment of the torsion bar and avoids an additional
nearby suspended structure that could introduce scattering, electrostatic
coupling, or Newtonian gravitational asymmetry. The absence of an ETM recoil
mass requires separate actuators for lock acquisition and low-noise science
operation, as described in Sec.
16.6
.

Cryogenic design must treat cooling hardware as part of the mechanical plant.
The KAGRA payload demonstrates why high-conductivity heat links must also be
mechanically compliant, why thermal contraction requires adequate sensor and
alignment range, and why actuator coefficients must be verified at operating
temperature
[
114
]
. For CHRONOS, heat-link
routing is therefore preferentially terminated on recoil structures, and the
stiffness, geometry, and vibration of every thermal link are included in the
PFM-to-ETM transfer functions rather than regarded as ideal thermal-only
connections.

16.6
End-test-mass actuation and operating states

The ETM actuation system is divided according to function:

•

MNIM–MNRM actuation provides alignment, damping, and DC offloading;

•

IMM–IMRM actuation damps the lower suspension modes and provides fine
alignment;

•

a floor-referenced electrostatic actuator (ESA) supplies the large
torque required during lock acquisition and recovery; and

•

a photon-pressure actuator (PPA) supplies low-noise torque during
science operation.

For a general electrode geometry the electrostatic force is

F_{\rm ESA}=\frac{1}{2}V^{2}\frac{\mathrm{d}C}{\mathrm{d}x},

(16.6)

and a parallel-plate estimate gives

F_{\rm ESA}\simeq\frac{\epsilon_{0}AV^{2}}{2d^{2}},\qquad\tau_{\rm ESA}=r(F_{\rm L}-F_{\rm R}),

(16.7)

where
A
is the effective electrode area,
d
the gap,
r
the torque arm,
and
F_{\rm L,R}
the forces applied on the two sides. Voltages of several
hundred volts to approximately 1 kV and millimeter-scale gaps can provide a
substantially larger acquisition torque than the PPA.

The optical coating does not prevent electrostatic actuation. Even when the
ETM substrate and multilayer coating are insulating, the electric field
polarizes the dielectric and changes the capacitance in
Eq. (
16.6
). Electrostatic drives have therefore been used on
dielectric gravitational-wave test masses, and side-mounted concepts have
also been developed for precision interferometers

[
115
,
116
]
. No conductive pad on the
optical surface is required. The preferred CHRONOS geometry places electrodes
near a side or rear surface, avoids the high-reflectivity optical area, and
uses a differential electrode pattern to generate yaw torque. The actuation
coefficient must be measured at the operating temperature because the
permittivity, surface resistance, charge relaxation, and patch-potential
behavior may change during cooldown.

Because the ESA is referenced to the ground, a nonzero bias produces an
electrostatic stiffness of approximate magnitude

k_{\rm ESA}\sim\frac{\epsilon_{0}AV^{2}}{d^{3}},

(16.8)

which can couple electrode or ground motion to the ETM. Surface charging can
also change the actuation coefficient and introduce excess force noise.
Consequently, the ESA is not used continuously in the nominal science state.
The operational handover is

1.

acquire the interferometer using the ESA together with MNIM/IMM control;

2.

close the PPA loop after the optical readout is linear and stable;

3.

cross-fade the commanded torque continuously from the ESA to the PPA;

4.

ramp the ESA voltage to zero;

5.

connect the electrodes to a defined low-noise ground; and

6.

remove the ESA command path from the science controller.

The ESA remains available for automatic lock recovery, while the PPA provides
the ETM torque in science mode. This division preserves acquisition authority
without retaining a floor-referenced electric spring during observation.

16.7
Sensors and control topology

The ISI uses relative sensors to control position at very low frequencies and
inertial sensors to suppress absolute motion through the observation band.
Following the active-isolation method reviewed by Matichard et al., calibrated
relative and inertial channels are combined with complementary filters; ground
instruments may additionally supply sensor correction and coherent
feedforward
[
113
]
. CHRONOS applies this
method recursively from Stage-0 through Stage-3 and adds a dedicated
low-frequency Sagnac yaw measurement on Stage-3.

Table 16.1:
Baseline ISI sensor suite. “Six-axis” denotes six appropriately
oriented single-axis channels used to reconstruct the six rigid-body degrees
of freedom.

Location

Sensor

Baseline quantity

Stage-3

1 optical system

All stages

at least 6 points per ISI

At each controlled interface, the relative and inertial signals are blended
according to frequency. Stage-0 uses facility-relative sensors for coarse
position, attitude, and drift control. Stages 1–3 progressively suppress the
motion of the precision optical reference. Ground sensors provide sensor
correction and feedforward, as well as earthquake monitoring. The Stage-3
Sagnac covers approximately
0.03–0.3 Hz, where yaw reconstructed from translational inertial sensors is
limited by tilt coupling and sensor self-noise. Above this range, yaw is
reconstructed from the tangential response of the Stage-3 inertial-sensor
array.

Three horizontal and three vertical sensing directions are distributed at
120-degree intervals with a target radius of at least 1 m. If three
tangential sensors have identical displacement noise
S_{x,n}^{1/2}
, the
equivalent yaw noise is approximately

S_{\theta,n}^{1/2}\simeq\frac{S_{x,n}^{1/2}}{R\sqrt{3}}.

(16.9)

Sensors and actuators are collocated and coaligned wherever practical to
reduce non-minimum-phase couplings. The geometry matrix is measured after the
full payload has been installed. Frequency-dependent diagonalization is used
if a constant rigid-body matrix is insufficient near structural modes.

Each GAS stage has three vertical displacement sensors arranged at 120-degree
intervals, allowing common vertical motion to be separated from tilt. GAS-1
also carries horizontal relative sensors, and the PFM is monitored in all six
rigid-body degrees of freedom. All local GAS loops are shaped so that their
sensor noise contribution decreases above the filter resonances.

16.8
Preliminary performance requirements

16.8.1
Top-level angular-noise allocation

For the differential CHRONOS coordinate, the reference response is

\Delta\theta_{\rm sig}\simeq\eta h,\qquad\eta=0.93656.

(16.10)

Allocating at most 10% of the target angular amplitude noise to the combined
ISI and GAS system gives

S_{\Delta\theta,{\rm ISI+GAS}}^{1/2}(f)\leq 0.1\eta S_{h}^{1/2}(f).

(16.11)

At 2 Hz this corresponds to

S_{\Delta\theta,{\rm ISI+GAS}}^{1/2}(2~\mathrm{Hz})\leq 7.8\times 10^{-20}~\mathrm{rad}/\sqrt{\mathrm{Hz}}.

(16.12)

Equation (
16.12
) is a requirement on the residual differential
ETM yaw after the complete suspension, not on Stage-0 yaw itself. Its
feasibility depends critically on passive attenuation and on the equality of
the two PFM-to-ETM transfer functions.

For a Stage-0 output degree of freedom

q\in\{x,y,z,R_{x},R_{y},R_{z}\}
, the correct displacement or angular requirement is

S_{q,{\rm S0}}^{1/2}(f)\leq\frac{0.1\eta S_{h}^{1/2}(f)}{\left|T_{q\rightarrow\theta_{x}}(f)-T_{q\rightarrow\theta_{y}}(f)\right|}.

(16.13)

The denominator is the measured or modeled common-to-differential coupling.
This equation, rather than a generic isolation number, will be used to freeze
the final Stage-0 output specification.

16.10
Verification and requirement maturation

The numerical allocations above define a starting point, but the Stage-0
motion table cannot be regarded as final until common-to-differential coupling
is quantified. Requirement maturation will proceed in the following order:

1.

establish the mass, center-of-mass, and inertia budget for every ISI,
GAS, PFM, main-chain, recoil-chain, cryogenic, and cabling
component;

2.

construct a coupled six-degree-of-freedom model of ISI Stage-3 through
Stage-0, the GAS-4–GAS-1 chain, PFM, and both
x/y
payloads;

3.

include thermal-link stiffness, cable stiffness, sensor geometry,
actuator geometry, and realistic damping;

4.

verify by drawing review and as-built inspection that all mechanical,
thermal, and cable services below the PFM remain branch-specific and do
not form an unintended
x/y
mechanical bridge;

5.

calculate

T_{q\rightarrow\theta_{x}}
and

T_{q\rightarrow\theta_{y}}
for all six Stage-0 coordinates;

6.

inject measured ground translation, ground rotation, sensor noise,
actuator noise, and control noise into the closed-loop model;

7.

replace the provisional Stage-0 values using
Eq. (
16.13
); and

8.

verify that the quadrature sum of ISI and GAS contributions satisfies
Eq. (
16.12
), with margin, across the observation band.

Subsystem tests will measure actuator coefficients, sensor self-noise,
cross-axis coupling, loaded structural modes, GAS tuning range, and local-loop
noise injection. After assembly, the
x
and
y
transfer functions will be
measured separately and used to construct the differential diagonalization
filters. Lock-acquisition tests will then demonstrate continuous ESA-to-PPA
handover and confirm that the grounded ESA does not produce a measurable
science-band coupling.

The design choices that can be retained at the present stage are therefore:
one common four-stage ISI, one common GAS-4–GAS-1 chain, one common
cryogenic PFM, two independent
x/y
cryogenic payloads
below the PFM, recoil masses at MNIM and IMM, no recoil mass at either ETM, ESA
actuation for acquisition,
and photon-pressure actuation for science operation. Component-level motion
and sensor-noise values remain controlled preliminary requirements until the
full differential-yaw noise budget is completed.

Chapter 17  Test Mass Mirror

17.1
Overview of Test Mass

The test-mass mirror is one of the most important components determining the fundamental performance of CHRONOS.
CHRONOS is a cryogenic torsion-bar gravitational-wave detector designed for precision measurements in the sub-Hz frequency band at a temperature of approximately
4~\mathrm{K}
.
Its test masses therefore play roles that are substantially different
from those of conventional kilometer-scale Fabry–Pérot gravitational-wave interferometers.

The CHRONOS test mass acts as

•

a high-quality optical mirror,

•

a low-loss cryogenic mechanical oscillator,

•

the inertial body defining the torsional response of the detector,

•

an interface between the optical system and the cryogenic suspension.

Consequently, the choice of test-mass material and geometry should be determined by mechanical, optical, thermal, and manufacturing requirements.

The baseline CHRONOS design employs a laser wavelength of

\lambda=1064~\mathrm{\mathrm{nm}},

(17.1)

together with monocrystalline sapphire test masses operated at temperature of approximately

T\simeq 4~\mathrm{K}.

(17.2)

The 1064
\mathrm{nm}
wavelength is selected because mature Nd:YAG laser technology and a wide range of low-noise optical components are available. It also maintains compatibility with optical technologies already developed for precision gravitational-wave interferometers.

At this wavelength, sapphire is the baseline substrate material.
Sapphire provides high mechanical quality factor at cryogenic temperature, high thermal conductivity, good mechanical strength, and optical transparency at 1064
\mathrm{nm}
.

An additional characteristic of the CHRONOS test mass is its large
mechanical dimension. Because the detector measures the rotational motion of a torsion-bar system, the mass distribution and moment of inertia of the test mass constitute central mechanical design parameters. The required dimensions may exceed those practically achievable by a single high-quality sapphire crystal.

CHRONOS therefore considers a modular mass-extension scheme in which
multiple sapphire components are connected using hydroxide-catalysis
bonding (HCB). This technique allows the total torsion-bar length and moment of inertia to be extended beyond the dimensions of a single available crystal while maintaining a rigid all-sapphire mechanical structure. Consequently, the baseline design philosophy is to make the largest monolithic sapphire blocks with minimum necessary HCB interfaces.

This chapter describes the optical, mechanical, and thermal requirements of the CHRONOS test mass and the engineering strategy for constructing the large-scale cryogenic torsion-bar mirror.

17.2
Test-Mass Requirements

From the mechanical point of view, the test mass must provide

•

large mass,

•

large moment of inertia,

•

adequate structural strength,

•

compatibility with the cryogenic suspension system.

As discussed in the later sections, the final torsion-bar dimensions may exceed those available from a single monolithic sapphire crystal. Therefore, the design must allow the use of HCB for mass extension without introducing excessive mechanical dissipation. The substrate must retain

•

a high mechanical quality factor
Q
at cryogenic temperature

because mechanical losses in the substrate, bonded interfaces, and attached structures directly contribute to thermal noise. The dimensions of the sapphire components must therefore be chosen not only to realize the required total mass and moment of inertia, but also to preserve sufficiently low mechanical loss in the complete assembled structure.

The optical requirements include

•

low absorption and scattering,

•

high refractive-index homogeneity,

•

small transmitted wavefront distortion,

•

compatibility with high-quality optical polishing.

The dielectric coating must likewise provide the required reflectivity
while maintaining low optical absorption and sufficiently low mechanical loss at cryogenic temperature.

From the aspect of thermal properties, the substrate must have

•

high thermal conductivity

in order to efficiently remove heat deposited by absorbed laser power and to suppress temperature gradients in the test mass. It is also desirable to have

•

a small thermal expansion coefficient

for reducing thermally induced deformation, stress, and long-term drift of the mechanical geometry.

Finally, all materials, bonded interfaces, coatings, and suspension
attachments must remain compatible with operation in

•

ultra-high-vacuum,

•

cryogenic.

The test-mass design is therefore determined by a combined optimization of mass distribution, moment of inertia, mechanical quality factor, optical quality, thermal transport, structural strength, and manufacturability.

17.3
Mass and Moment of Inertia

CHRONOS measures the rotational response of a suspended torsion-bar system. For the rotational degree of freedom
\theta
, the equation of motion can be written as

I\ddot{\theta}(t)+\Gamma_{\theta}\dot{\theta}(t)+\kappa_{\theta}\theta(t)=\tau_{\rm sig}(t)+\tau_{\rm exc}(t),

(17.3)

where
I
is the effective moment of inertia,
\Gamma_{\theta}
is the rotational damping coefficient,
\kappa_{\theta}
is the torsional spring constant, and
\tau_{\rm sig}
and
\tau_{\rm exc}
denote signal and excess torques.

Above the torsional resonant frequency, the angular displacement generated by an external torque is approximately

\theta(\omega)\simeq-\frac{\tau_{\rm exc}(\omega)}{I\omega^{2}}.

(17.4)

A large
I
therefore suppresses angular motion produced by parasitic
torques such as fluctuations of actuator force and radiation pressure of the main interferometer laser.

The role of a large test mass in CHRONOS is therefore different from that of a large mirror aperture in a kilometer-scale Fabry–Pérot detector. Although optical clipping loss remains as a constraint, the dimensions of the CHRONOS test mass are primarily determined by its required mechanical mass distribution. The optimum dimensions must consequently be derived from the complete torsion-bar dynamics rather than from the beam radius alone.

17.4
Baseline Substrate Material: Sapphire

17.4.1
Material Properties

Monocrystalline sapphire is adopted as the baseline substrate material for the CHRONOS test mass. The primary reason is that sapphire provides a favorable combination of optical, mechanical, and thermal properties for cryogenic operation while remaining compatible with the baseline laser wavelength of 1064
\mathrm{nm}
.

From the optical point of view, sapphire is transparent at 1064
\mathrm{nm}
and can be processed to make high-quality optical surfaces
[
118
]
. It is also compatible with dielectric high-reflectivity coating technologies, making it suitable for use as a precision interferometer mirror. This allows CHRONOS to retain the mature 1064
\mathrm{nm}
laser and optical technology widely used in gravitational-wave interferometry.

In terms of mechanics, sapphire exhibits a high elastic modulus and high structural strength, which are important for constructing a large torsion-bar test mass
[
119
]
. More importantly, its mechanical quality factor
Q
can become very high (
\sim 10^{8}
) at cryogenic temperature
[
120
]
. This property is essential for CHRONOS because mechanical dissipation in the test mass directly contributes to thermal fluctuations expressed by the fluctuation–dissipation theorem.

Sapphire also has excellent cryogenic thermal properties. Its thermal conductivity increases strongly at low temperature, allowing heat generated by optical absorption to be efficiently transported away from the illuminated region
[
118
,
3
]
. At the same time, its thermal expansion coefficient becomes very small, reducing thermally induced deformation and stress in the test mass
[
3
]
. These characteristics are particularly advantageous for maintaining the mechanical and optical stability required for sub-Hz measurements.

Table
17.1
summarizes the mechanical and thermal properties of sapphire in 300 K and 20 K
[
119
,
118
,
3
,
120
]
.

Another important consideration is technological maturity. Sapphire test masses and associated fabrication techniques have already been
developed in the context of cryogenic gravitational-wave detectors, providing experience in crystal production, precision polishing, coating, suspension, and cryogenic operation. This existing technological basis reduces the development risk compared with
a completely new substrate technology.

For these reasons, sapphire provides the most balanced solution for the current CHRONOS design. It enables the interferometer to remain based on mature 1064
\mathrm{nm}
optical technology while simultaneously providing the high mechanical quality factor, thermal conductivity, mechanical strength, and cryogenic compatibility required for the large torsion-bar test mass.

17.4.2
Crystal Orientation

Sapphire is an anisotropic crystal whose optical, mechanical, and thermal properties depend on the crystallographic orientation. In particular, the crystallographic
c
-axis defines the optical axis of
sapphire and therefore plays an important role in determining the optical birefringence and the propagation of the interferometer beam through the substrate.

In the present CHRONOS design, the sapphire blocks are assumed to be oriented such that the
c
-axis is parallel to the interferometer beam direction. This configuration provides a simple and well-defined reference geometry for the optical design and is also the orientation adopted in the current mass-extension study.

The crystal orientation is also relevant to the mechanical properties of the test mass. Because sapphire has direction-dependent elastic properties, the resonant frequencies and deformation patterns of the internal mechanical modes can change with the orientation of the crystal relative to the torsion and suspension axes. The mode shape and crystal orientation may affect effective mechanical loss. Therefore, the orientation of the sapphire cannot be determined from optical considerations alone.

The thermal properties of sapphire are also anisotropic.
The heat flow from the illuminated region toward the suspension and the resulting temperature distribution can depend on the relative direction of the crystal axes. This becomes important in the cryogenic test mass, where absorbed optical power must be efficiently transported to the suspension system.

For CHRONOS, the baseline configuration therefore adopts the
c
-axis
parallel to the interferometer beam, while the final orientation will be verified together with the complete mechanical and cryogenic design. In particular, the effects of crystal orientation on optical birefringence, internal-mode structure, mechanical dissipation, and heat transport must be evaluated using the final test-mass and suspension geometry.

17.5
Fabrication of Crystals

17.5.1
Constraint on crystal size

One of the major technological challenges in the development of the CHRONOS test mass is the fabrication of sufficiently large sapphire components while maintaining the optical and mechanical quality required for cryogenic precision measurements. Because the torsion-bar geometry requires a large mass distribution and moment of inertia, the dimensions of the sapphire components are determined
not only by the optical beam size but also by the mechanical design of the test mass.

Large monocrystalline sapphire can be produced using crystal-growth
techniques such as the Heat Exchange Method (HEM). In the HEM process, the thermal environment of the melt and the seed crystal
is carefully controlled during growth and cooling. This makes it possible to produce large sapphire crystals while suppressing
internal stress and reducing crystalline defects. Such large-volume crystal-growth technology is therefore particularly important for CHRONOS, where the dimensions of the test mass can approach or exceed those of conventional optical substrates.

However, the maximum size of the grown sapphire boule does not directly determine the maximum usable size of a CHRONOS test-mass component. The usable volume depends strongly on the crystallographic orientation required for the optical and mechanical design, because the desired optical surface must be cut with a specific orientation relative to the crystal axes. Regions containing inclusions, defects, excessive residual stress, or refractive-index inhomogeneity must also be excluded from the final optical substrate.

Optical requirements further reduce the practically usable dimensions. The selected material must provide sufficiently uniform refractive index and sufficiently low absorption over the region sampled by the interferometer beam. At the same time, additional material must generally be retained during machining to allow the final dimensions, surface orientation, and optical figure to be produced by precision grinding and polishing. The achievable test-mass dimensions are therefore determined by the combination of crystal growth, crystallographic orientation, optical quality,
machining, and polishing rather than by the raw boule diameter alone.

For CHRONOS, the relevant quantity is consequently not the largest sapphire crystal that can be grown, but the largest low-loss and optical-quality sapphire component that can be fabricated with the required crystal orientation. This distinction is particularly important for the mass-extension design, because the practical dimensions of an individual sapphire component determine the numbers of blocks and bonded interfaces required to construct the complete torsion bar.

17.5.2
Optical Homogeneity

For a substrate through which the optical field propagates,
inhomogeneity of refractive index distort the transmitted wavefront. For a substrate thickness
l
and refractive-index variation
\delta n
, the optical-path variation is approximately

\delta_{\rm OPL}\simeq l\,\delta n.

(17.5)

On the other hand, we set a requirement of the surface non-uniformity at 1/10 of the laser wavelength
\lambda
. A representative wavefront-quality requirement can therefore be expressed as

\delta_{\rm OPL}<\frac{\lambda}{10}.

(17.6)

For example, where

l=0.20~\mathrm{m},\qquad\lambda=1064~\mathrm{nm},

(17.7)

this corresponds approximately to

\delta n<<5\times 10^{-7}.

(17.8)

The precise requirement depends on the optical role of the mirror. For an input optic through which significant optical power propagates,
bulk homogeneity is particularly important. For a highly reflective end surface, surface figure, scattering, and coating uniformity can instead dominate the optical requirement.

17.6
Large-Scale Test-Mass Architecture

Ideally, the CHRONOS torsion-bar test mass would be fabricated as a single monolithic sapphire structure. A monolithic configuration is mechanically attractive because it avoids additional interfaces that can introduce dissipation, alignment errors, and local stress concentrations.

In practice, however, realizing the full CHRONOS torsion-bar geometry from a single sapphire crystal is difficult. The achievable size of a single crystal is limited not only by the size of crucible, but also by the availability of sufficiently large defect-free material with the required crystallographic orientation and optical quality. Even when a sufficiently large raw crystal can be produced, additional constraints are placed on the dimensions of the final component by machining, handling, precision optical-surface fabrication, and cryogenic integration. The suspension interface must also be incorporated without compromising the mechanical quality of the torsion bar.

For these reasons, a fully monolithic test mass may not be the most practical solution for the final CHRONOS detector. An alternative is to construct the torsion bar from several sapphire components and assemble them into a single mechanical structure while preserving sufficiently low mechanical loss.

CHRONOS therefore considers hydroxide-catalysis bonding as the baseline technique for such mass extension. This approach allows the total torsion-bar length and mass distribution to exceed the dimensions of an individual sapphire component, while retaining
an all-sapphire structure over most of the mechanical volume. The resulting design strategy is to use the largest practical monolithic sapphire components and introduce bonded interfaces only where they are necessary to achieve the required overall geometry.

17.6.1
Hydroxide-Catalysis Bonding

Hydroxide-catalysis bonding is a precision joining technique that forms a thin and mechanically rigid bond between highly polished surfaces. It has been developed for optical and mechanical systems in which high dimensional stability and low mechanical dissipation are required
[
121
]
.

For CHRONOS, HCB provides a practical method for extending the dimensions of the torsion-bar test mass beyond those achievable with a single sapphire component. By bonding several sapphire blocks together, the total bar length, mass distribution, and moment of inertia can be adjusted independently of the maximum dimensions of an individual crystal. This is particularly useful when the mechanically preferred torsion-bar geometry exceeds the size of the available optical-quality sapphire substrate.

The mass-extension design is determined by several coupled parameters.
The total torsion-bar length and mass determine the overall geometry, while the moment of inertia about the suspension axis governs the rotational mechanical response. On the other hand, the number of sapphire blocks and cross section are relevant to properties of the bonding layers, thus to the effective mechanical loss of the assembled structure. In particular, the number of sapphire blocks determines the number of bonded interfaces. The bonding area and bonding-layer thickness control how much of the mechanical strain energy is stored in the HCB regions. The elastic energy stored in the HCB and the intrinsic mechanical losses of both substrate and bonding materials determines the effective mechanical loss.

This introduces an important trade-off in the test-mass design.
Increasing the number of bonded blocks makes it possible to construct a longer and mechanically larger torsion bar, but every additional interface introduces a region with substantially larger mechanical loss than the sapphire substrate. The HCB mass-extension scheme must therefore be designed so that the required bar dimensions and moment of inertia are achieved with as the minimal number of bonded interfaces as possible and the least elastic-energy participation in the bonding layers.

In this sense, HCB is not introduced simply as a convenient assembly
technique. It is an integral part of the mechanical design of the CHRONOS test mass, and its geometry must be optimized together with the dimensions of the sapphire blocks, the suspension configuration, and the internal mechanical modes of the complete torsion bar.

17.6.2
Reference Sapphire Block

The current mass-extension study assumes cuboid sapphire blocks whose crystallographic
c
-axis is aligned with the interferometer beam
[
122
]
. This study assume the dimension of a single cuboid as

220~\mathrm{mm}\times 220~\mathrm{mm}\times 200~\mathrm{mm}.

(17.9)

This assumption is based on the maximum available sapphire size of approximately

\phi 250~\mathrm{mm}\times 150~\mathrm{mm},

(17.10)

according to the procurement information used for the mass-extension analysis.

These dimensions should not be interpreted as final procurement
specifications. The available crystal size must be re-evaluated for the final crystallographic orientation and optical-quality requirement.

17.6.3
Reference Mass-Extension Geometry

The current reference CHRONOS torsion bar consists of five sapphire blocks. In the study of extended test masses, structures consisting of

N_{\rm block}=3,\,5,\,7,\,9,\,11,\,13

(17.11)

blocks were considered. Appearances of these extended bars are shown in Fig.
17.1
.

17.7
Mechanical Loss

17.7.1
General formalism

Mechanical dissipation is one of the principal requirements for the
CHRONOS test mass. The mechanical quality factor is related to the loss angle approximately by

\phi\simeq\frac{1}{Q}.

(17.15)

The loss angle represents the structural damping scales of a bulk.

According to the fluctuation–dissipation theorem, the thermal-noise
power spectral density associated with the damping scales
schematically as
[
21
]

S_{\rm th}\propto T\phi\simeq\frac{T}{Q}.

(17.16)

The corresponding amplitude spectral density scales as

S_{\rm th}^{1/2}\propto\sqrt{\frac{T}{Q}}.

(17.17)

As a consequence, low temperature alone is therefore not sufficient: the complete suspended mechanical structure must also retain a high mechanical
Q
.

17.7.2
Cryogenic Quality Factor of Sapphire

One of the main advantages of sapphire for CHRONOS is its extremely low mechanical dissipation at cryogenic temperature. Since the mechanical loss angle is inverse of
Q
, a high
Q
is essential for suppressing thermally driven mechanical fluctuations of the test mass.

Cryogenic measurements of sapphire have demonstrated mechanical quality factors in the range of approximately

Q\sim 10^{7}-10^{8},

(17.18)

with values as high as about

Q\simeq 2.5\times 10^{8}

(17.19)

reported near
4.2~\mathrm{K}
for internal mechanical modes of sapphire test masses
[
120
]
. These results demonstrate that sapphire can retain exceptionally low mechanical loss in the temperature range relevant to CHRONOS.

However, the quality factor is not a unique material constant.
The measured value depends on the mechanical mode, crystal quality,
crystallographic orientation, surface condition, suspension method, and residual stress. For a large CHRONOS torsion bar, the relevant mechanical dissipation is therefore that of the complete assembled structure rather than that of an isolated sapphire sample.

This distinction becomes particularly important when mass extension is
introduced. Although the sapphire substrate itself can have a very small loss angle, the HCB interfaces and suspension-related structures can store elastic energy and reduce the effective quality factor of the mechanical modes. The final mechanical performance must therefore be evaluated mode by mode, including the sapphire blocks, bonded interfaces, bridge structure, and suspension coupling.

For CHRONOS, the practical requirement is consequently to preserve the
intrinsically low cryogenic loss of sapphire after the complete test mass has been assembled. This requires both finite-element evaluation of the strain-energy distribution and experimental ring-down measurements of the full cryogenic structure.

17.7.3
Mechanical Loss of the HCB Extension

The HCB interfaces introduce an additional mechanical-loss contribution. In the current finite-element model, the HCB layer is assumed to have an uniform thickness of

t_{\rm bond}=100~\mathrm{nm}

(17.20)

over the bonded surfaces
[
123
]
.

The representative material properties of sapphire and HCB used in the analysis are shown in Table
17.3

[
124
,
123
,
125
,
126
]
.

Table 17.3:

Representative material properties used in the current mass-extension FEA.

Material

Density

Young’s modulus

Poisson ratio

Mechanical loss

[kg/m
3
]

[GPa]

Sapphire

4000

400

0.29

4\times 10^{-9}

HCB

1075.7

18.5

0.16

0.11

The intrinsic mechanical loss assumed for the HCB material is thus many orders of magnitude larger than that of sapphire. However, the HCB volume is relatively small. Contribution of the bonding to the effective mechanical loss of the whole torsion-bar structure is scaled by its elastic-energy participation.

The elastic energy stored in each material domain is evaluated from the stress tensor
\sigma_{ij}
and strain tensor
\epsilon_{ij}
as

U=\iiint\frac{1}{2}\sum_{ij}\sigma_{ij}\epsilon_{ij}\,dV.

(17.21)

The effective mechanical loss is then expressed as

\phi_{\rm eff}=\frac{U_{\rm B}}{U_{\rm S}+U_{\rm B}}\phi_{\rm B}+\frac{U_{\rm S}}{U_{\rm S}+U_{\rm B}}\phi_{\rm S},

(17.22)

where
U_{\rm B}
and
U_{\rm S}
are the elastic energies stored in the bonding and sapphire domains, respectively.

Defining the bonding participation ratio

p_{\rm B}=\frac{U_{\rm B}}{U_{\rm S}+U_{\rm B}},

(17.23)

Eq. (
17.22
) becomes

\phi_{\rm eff}=p_{\rm B}\phi_{\rm B}+(1-p_{\rm B})\phi_{\rm S}.

(17.24)

The current FEA gives bonding participation ratios approximately of order

p_{\rm B}\sim 10^{-5}

(17.25)

for the relevant internal modes.

Consequently, even though the intrinsic HCB loss is large, the effective loss of the complete structure remains approximately in the

\phi_{\rm eff}\sim 10^{-6}

(17.26)

range in the present model. This demonstrates that the principal design strategy is to minimize the elastic strain energy localized in the HCB regions.

17.8
Internal Mechanical Modes

17.8.1
Resonant Frequencies

The mechanical effect of the HCB interfaces depends strongly on the internal mode shape of the torsion bar. Each elastic mode produces a different strain distribution across the sapphire blocks and the bonded interfaces, and therefore stores a different fraction of
its elastic energy in the HCB regions. As a result, the effective mechanical loss of the assembled test mass is inherently mode-dependent.

The present analysis focuses on the lowest internal resonances
corresponding to vertical bending, horizontal bending, and twisting of the extended torsion bar as shown in Fig.
17.2
. For the reference five-units configuration, the calculated resonant frequencies are approximately

f_{\rm VB}=666.6~\mathrm{Hz},

(17.27)

f_{\rm HB}=1237.7~\mathrm{Hz},

(17.28)

and

f_{\rm TW}=1398.9~\mathrm{Hz}.

(17.29)

Figure 17.2:

Shape of the three lowest internal modes of the CHRONOS torsion bar with five units. (a) vertical bending mode at 666.6 Hz, (b) horizontal bending mode at 1237.7 Hz, (c) twisting mode at 1398.9 Hz. The mode shape was obtained by COMSOL Multiphysics 5.4.

These resonant frequencies are determined by the geometry of the assembled torsion bar and therefore change as the mass-extension configuration is modified. In particular, increasing the number of sapphire blocks increases the total length of the bar and reduces the frequencies. Thus, extending the test mass changes not only the total mass and moment of inertia, but also the internal elastic dynamics of the structure.

This introduces an important mechanical trade-off in the mass-extension design. A longer torsion bar provides a larger moment of inertia, while at the same time requiring additional bonded interfaces and lowering several internal resonance frequencies. The final geometry must therefore be chosen so that the required mechanical size and inertia are achieved without bringing mechanically relevant internal modes too close to the detector observation or control bands.

The importance of a given internal mode cannot be assessed from its resonance frequency alone. Because the interferometer senses the motion of the optical surface at the beam position, the coupling of each elastic mode to the optical readout depends on its deformation pattern and effective mass. Likewise, the additional dissipation introduced by the HCB depends on the elastic-energy participation of the bonded regions for that particular mode. The internal-mode analysis must therefore evaluate the mode shape, resonant frequency, effective mass, and effective mechanical loss together.

For the mass-extended configurations, these quantities are obtained by
finite-element analysis (FEA) of the complete bonded structure using COMSOL Multiphysics 5.4
[
127
]
. The elastic energy stored in the sapphire and HCB regions is evaluated by Eq. (
17.22
) for each mode, and the corresponding mode-dependent effective loss is calculated from their energy participation. The surface displacement is additionally weighted by the interferometer beam profile in order to determine the effective mass relevant to the optical readout.

17.8.2
Effective Mass

The displacement of an internal mode sensed by the interferometer is not equal among the modes. For the
n
-th mechanical mode, an effective mass
m_{n}
can be defined by equating the energy of the surface displacement sensed by the optical beam to that of the full elastic mode:

\frac{1}{2}m_{n}x_{n}^{2}\omega_{n}^{2}=\frac{1}{2}\iiint_{\rm bar}\rho\left(\epsilon_{n,x}^{2}+\epsilon_{n,y}^{2}+\epsilon_{n,z}^{2}\right)dV\,\omega_{n}^{2},

(17.30)

giving

m_{n}=\frac{\displaystyle\iiint_{\rm bar}\rho\left(\epsilon_{n,x}^{2}+\epsilon_{n,y}^{2}+\epsilon_{n,z}^{2}\right)dV}{x_{n}^{2}}.

(17.31)

Here the
\omega_{n}
is the angular resonant frequency of
n
-th mode,
\rho
is the density of the substrate, and
\varepsilon_{n,i}
(
i
=
x
,
y
,
z
) is the strain along
i
-axis at
n
-th mode. The
x_{n}
is the optical-weighted surface displacement seen by the interferometer beam which is calculated by

x_{n}=\frac{\displaystyle\iint_{\rm surface}P(\bm{r})\epsilon_{n,z}\,dS}{\displaystyle\iint_{\rm surface}P(\bm{r})\,dS},

(17.32)

where the Gaussian beam profile is represented as

P(\bm{r})=\exp\left(-\frac{2\left|\bm{r}\right|^{2}}{\sigma^{2}}\right).

(17.33)

using the maximum power amplitude
P_{0}
, the beam radius
\sigma
, and the position vector
\bm{r}
of the mesh from the center of the beam.

The resonant peaks that appear in the bar thermal noise spectrum are suppressed by scaling of the inverse effective mass. This formulation makes explicit that the mechanical relevance of an internal mode depends on both its global deformation and the local surface motion sampled by the interferometer beam.

As a summary of the FEA study of the resonant modes of the torsion bar, Table

shows resonant frequencies of the three lowest modes with their energy participation, effective mechanical loss, and effective mass.

17.9
Reduction of Bonding Area

The nominal HCB model assumes that the bond covers the entire facing surface between adjacent sapphire blocks. However, full-area bonding is not necessarily optimal from the viewpoint of mechanical dissipation.

For a fixed bonding-layer thickness, the elastic energy stored in the HCB region, and hence the effective mechanical loss, is approximately
proportional to the total bonded area
A_{\rm bond}
. Reducing
A_{\rm bond}
therefore provides a direct strategy for decreasing
p_{\rm B}
and hence
\phi_{\rm eff}
. The lower limit of
A_{\rm bond}
is instead set by mechanical strength.

17.9.1
Static Strength Requirement

The present engineering analysis adopts

\tau_{\rm max}=0.16~\mathrm{MPa}

(17.34)

as a reference allowable shear stress
[
121
]
.

The innermost HCB interface experiences the largest load because it supports all of the outer blocks. We define index of HCB layers by
i=1
at the innermost interface and ascending to the outer. For an torsion bar made of
N_{\rm block}
units, the minimum required bonding area of the

i
-th interface counted from the center is estimated as

A_{{\rm bond},i}\geq\frac{M_{\rm block}\left[\frac{N_{\rm block}-1}{2}-i+1\right]g}{\tau_{\rm max}},

(17.35)

where

i\leq\frac{n-1}{2}.

(17.36)

The mass of individual block adopted in the present calculation is

M_{\rm block}=38.72~\mathrm{kg}.

(17.37)

The mass of the bonding material itself was neglected.

17.9.2
Required Bonding Area

Table
17.5
summarizes the total required bonding area on one side of the torsion bar.

Table 17.5:

bonding area.

For the reference five-units test mass,

A_{\rm bond}^{\rm req}\simeq 7.1\times 10^{3}~\mathrm{mm^{2}},

(17.38)

whereas the nominal full-area configuration provides

A_{\rm bond}^{\rm nominal}=6.4\times 10^{4}~\mathrm{mm^{2}}.

(17.39)

Thus, according to the simple static shear-strength criterion, only
approximately 11% of the nominal area is required. This suggests that reduced-area or patterned HCB interfaces could substantially reduce the effective mechanical loss.

17.9.3
Constraints on Reduced-Area Bonding

The minimum bonding area determined from the static shear-strength
requirement does not necessarily correspond to the optimum mechanical
design. Although reducing the bonded area can decrease the elastic-energy participation of the HCB region and thereby lower the effective mechanical loss, excessively small bonding regions may introduce other mechanical effects that are not captured by a simple static-load estimate.

In particular, reducing the bonded area leaves larger portions of the facing sapphire surfaces unbonded.These regions may experience local contact or friction, which could introduce additional dissipation. At the boundaries of the bonded regions, the mechanical load can also become spatially concentrated, producing local stress concentrations and modifying the strain distribution of the assembled structure. As a result, the internal elastic-mode shapes and their effective mechanical losses may differ from those predicted for a uniformly bonded interface.

Cryogenic operation introduces an additional consideration. During cooldown, thermal contraction of the sapphire blocks and the bonded
regions can generate local deformation and stress near the HCB interfaces. Such deformation may affect the relative alignment of the sapphire blocks and may also modify the mechanical boundary conditions of the internal modes. These effects become increasingly important when the bonded area is strongly reduced or when the bonding pattern becomes geometrically asymmetric.

The final bonding geometry must therefore be determined by balancing the reduction of HCB-related mechanical loss against the requirements for mechanical strength, structural stability, and alignment reproducibility. Finite-element analysis should be used to evaluate the stress distribution and internal-mode deformation for candidate bonding patterns, while mechanical-strength tests are required to confirm that the joints retain sufficient load capacity.

The optimized structure must also be verified experimentally through
ring-down measurements of bonded test samples and assembled structures. Measurements at both room temperature and cryogenic temperature are important for determining whether the reduced-area bonding introduces additional dissipation that is not included in the nominal material-loss model. Repeated thermal cycling is also required to confirm that the bond strength, mechanical loss, and relative alignment remain stable after successive cooldown and warm-up cycles.

The reduced-area HCB design should therefore be regarded as a coupled
mechanical optimization problem rather than a simple minimization of the bonding area.

17.10
Suspension-Fiber Interface

The test mass must provide a mechanically low-loss interface to the
cryogenic suspension. The CHRONOS detector employs crossed torsion bars. Directly attaching the suspension fibers to both bars creates a geometrical problem near the crossing point because the two suspension fibers would occupy overlapping regions.

A bridge-type sapphire structure is therefore considered as an interface between the torsion bar and its suspension fiber. The bridge is attached to the main sapphire structure using HCB and allows one of the crossed torsion bars to be vertically displaced relative to the other as shown in Fig.
17.3
.

Figure 17.3:

Configuration of cross torsion bar with bridge structure to hold suspension fibers.

This configuration makes it possible to realize the crossed geometry while maintaining independent response of each bar.

However, the bridge modifies the elastic boundary conditions of the torsion bar and therefore changes the internal resonant frequencies. The largest change occurs for the vertical-bending mode. For example, at the five-units configuration, the vertical-bending resonant frequency increases from 666.6 Hz to approximately 1007.0 Hz with the bridge.

The horizontal-bending mode is less strongly affected. For five or more blocks, its frequency shift is less than approximately 30 Hz, and the difference decreases for longer bars.

The suspension interface should therefore be included directly in the final FEA model rather than treated as an independent structure added after the torsion-bar geometry has been optimized.

17.11
Reduction of Bonded Interfaces

The most direct method for reducing HCB mechanical loss is to reduce the number of bonded interfaces. If larger monolithic sapphire components become available, the same dimension of the whole torsion-bar could be achieved by fewer joints.

The present mass-extension analysis considered hypothetical blocks with twice the nominal longitudinal dimension, referred to as “2U” blocks. The reduced number of HCB interfaces decreases the effective mechanical loss.

For the horizontal-bending mode, representative results are shown in
Table
17.6
.
