The analysis therefore establishes the following mechanical-design
principles:

1.

maximize the dimensions of each monolithic sapphire component;

2.

minimize the number of HCB interfaces;

3.

minimize the total HCB area consistent with sufficient structural strength;

4.

place interfaces in regions with low elastic-energy participation
for mechanically relevant modes;

5.

include the bridge and suspension interfaces in the same mechanical optimization.

The HCB concept should therefore not be interpreted as a preference for high segmentation. It is a practical method for achieving the required overall dimensions when a fully monolithic sapphire test mass cannot be manufactured.

17.12
Cryogenic Thermal Properties

The thermal properties of the substrate affect both intrinsic thermal
fluctuations and the extraction of heat deposited by absorbed laser power.

Sapphire has very high thermal conductivity at cryogenic temperature.
Around several tens of kelvin, the thermal conductivity of high-purity
sapphire can reach approximately
[
118
]

\kappa\sim 10^{3}-10^{4}~\mathrm{W\,m^{-1}\,K^{-1}},

(17.40)

depending on temperature, purity, crystal orientation, and sample size. A representative value around
20~\mathrm{K}
is

\kappa\sim 4\times 10^{3}~\mathrm{W\,m^{-1}\,K^{-1}}.

(17.41)

This high conductivity strongly suppresses temperature gradients across the test mass and facilitates heat extraction through the cryogenic suspension.

17.12.1
Thermal Expansion

The thermal expansion coefficient of sapphire decreases strongly at low temperature, making it particularly suitable for cryogenic precision measurements. A representative low-temperature expression for the linear thermal expansion coefficient along the crystallographic
c
-axis is

\alpha_{c}=7.5\times 10^{-13}T^{3}\quad[\mathrm{K^{-1}}],

(17.42)

within the temperature range of 1-100 K
[
3
]
. The rapid decrease of
\alpha_{c}
toward cryogenic temperature strongly
suppresses dimensional changes of the sapphire test mass produced by
temperature fluctuations.

This small thermal expansion is particularly advantageous when combined with the high thermal conductivity of cryogenic sapphire.
The high thermal conductivity suppresses temperature gradients within the test mass, while the small thermal expansion coefficient reduces the mechanical deformation produced by the remaining temperature anisotropy. As a result, thermally induced changes in the dimensions of the test mass, temperature-gradient-induced stress, and thermoelastic deformation can all be strongly reduced.

These properties are important not only for maintaining the optical surface geometry but also for preserving the mechanical stability of the torsion-bar system. Temperature anisotropy can modify the stress distribution in the test mass and its interfaces and can consequently shift the frequencies of internal mechanical modes. Slow thermal drifts may also change the equilibrium geometry of the suspended torsion bar and its alignment with respect to the interferometer
beam.

For CHRONOS, which targets precision measurements in the sub-Hz frequency band, such slowly varying thermal effects must be carefully controlled. The combination of small thermal expansion and high thermal conductivity therefore provides an important advantage of sapphire for maintaining both the dimensional and mechanical stability of the cryogenic test mass.

17.13
Optical Absorption

Optical absorption in the test mass converts part of the circulating laser power into heat and can therefore affect both the thermal state and the optical performance of the mirror. For CHRONOS, one should distinguish between absorption in the sapphire substrate and absorption in the high-reflectivity coating, because the two heat sources are distributed differently within the optic and can produce
different thermal responses.

Bulk absorption deposits heat throughout the optical path inside the
sapphire substrate, whereas coating absorption is concentrated close to the mirror surface. As a result, the corresponding temperature gradients, thermoelastic deformation, and thermo-refractive effects are not identical. The two contributions should therefore be characterized and specified independently when evaluating the cryogenic heat load and the resulting wavefront distortion.

The allowable optical absorption is set by the optical power and cryogenic
thermal budget of CHRONOS. Both substrate and coating absorption contribute
to the heat deposited in the test mass. Their spatial distributions determine
how that heat flows through the sapphire and into the cryogenic suspension.
Optical absorption therefore requires a coupled optical and thermal analysis.

17.13.1
Bulk Absorption

The optical absorption of sapphire depends strongly on crystal purity,
impurity concentration, crystal-growth conditions, and post-growth thermal treatment.

Annealing at approximately 1450-1600
∘
C in oxygen or air can reduce optical absorption. This effect is associated with reduction of impurity and defect states which are responsible for absorption at the interferometer wavelength. The CHRONOS design should therefore specify optical absorption together with the material grade and post-growth treatment.

17.13.2
Thermal Lensing

Optical absorption in the substrate produces a local heat load and can
generate both thermoelastic deformation of the mirror surface. It leads to thermo-refractive distortion of the transmitted optical wavefront. The magnitude of this thermal-lensing effect depends on the absorbed optical power, the temperature dependence of the refractive index, and the efficiency with which the deposited heat is transported through the substrate.

The thermo-refractive contribution can be represented schematically by

\delta s\propto\frac{\alpha_{\rm abs}\left(dn/dT\right)P}{\kappa},

(17.43)

where
\alpha_{\rm abs}
denotes the optical absorption coefficient,

dn/dT
represents the temperature dependence of the refractive index,

P
is the relevant optical power, and
\kappa
is the thermal conductivity of the substrate. This scaling shows that thermal lensing is enhanced by optical absorption and thermo-refractive coupling, while efficient thermal transport suppresses the temperature gradients responsible for wavefront distortion.

Sapphire is particularly favorable in this respect at cryogenic temperature. Its thermal conductivity becomes very large at low temperature, while the thermo-refractive coefficient is substantially reduced compared with its room-temperature value as summarized in Table
17.1
. As a result, heat deposited by optical absorption is efficiently distributed through the substrate, and the resulting refractive-index gradient is strongly suppressed. Thermal lensing is therefore expected to be much smaller under cryogenic operation than in a comparable room-temperature sapphire optic.

In addition to the wavefront distortion, a balance between the cooling power and the total heat load is relevant constraint as well. Explicitly writing, the absorbed power must remain below the heat that can be transferred out through the test mass and suspension,

P_{\rm abs}<P_{\rm cryo}^{\rm max}.

(17.44)

The allowable absorption must therefore be evaluated for the actual optical and cryogenic configuration of CHRONOS. The circulating optical power and beam radius determine the spatial heat deposition, while the substrate thickness and bulk absorption determine the amount of heat generated inside the sapphire. Coating absorption provides an additional heat source near the optical surface. The resulting temperature distribution is then determined by the thermal
conductivity of the sapphire, the thermal conductance through the suspension, and the operating temperature.

The final thermal-lensing requirement should therefore be derived from a coupled optical and thermal model of the complete test mass rather than from the substrate absorption coefficient alone.

17.14
Alternative Substrate: Crystalline Silicon

Crystalline silicon is an important alternative substrate for future
cryogenic upgrades of CHRONOS. At low temperature, silicon can exhibit extremely small mechanical dissipation together with excellent thermal properties, and it is therefore an attractive candidate for a next-generation cryogenic test mass.

The main limitation for the present CHRONOS design is its optical
transparency. Silicon is not sufficiently transparent at the baseline wavelength of 1064
\mathrm{nm}
, and its use would therefore require operation at a longer wavelength, typically around 1550
\mathrm{nm}
or beyond. Such a change would not be limited to the test-mass substrate itself. It would require a corresponding redesign of a substantial fraction of the optical system, including the laser source, frequency-control system, electro-optic modulation, optical isolation, beam splitters,
photodetectors, mode-cleaning optics, dielectric coatings, balanced-homodyne readout, and photon-pressure calibration system.

The substrate material and laser wavelength must therefore be regarded as a coupled design choice rather than as independent parameters. For the present CHRONOS configuration, sapphire operated with a 1064
\mathrm{nm}
interferometer remains the baseline because it combines
suitable cryogenic properties with mature optical technology. A silicon test mass operated with a longer-wavelength interferometer remains an important future upgrade path if further reduction of mechanical or coating-related losses becomes necessary.

17.15
Baseline Design Strategy

The CHRONOS test-mass design is determined by a coupled optimization of the mechanical geometry, bonded structure, optical requirements, and cryogenic performance. The dimensions of the individual sapphire blocks determine the total number of components required to construct the torsion bar, while the total bar length and mass distribution determine the moment of inertia about the suspension axis. At the same time, increasing the number of blocks introduces additional HCB
interfaces, and the bonding area and layer geometry determine how much
elastic energy is stored in the relatively high-loss bonding regions.

The preferred mechanical strategy is therefore to use the largest practical monolithic sapphire blocks, achieve the required torsion-bar length and moment of inertia with the minimum number of bonded interfaces, and minimize the elastic-energy participation of the HCB regions while preserving sufficient structural strength. The internal mechanical resonances must remain sufficiently separated from the relevant observation and control bands, and the bridge structure and
suspension interfaces must be included directly in the same mechanical
optimization rather than treated as independent attachments.

This mechanical design must simultaneously satisfy the optical and cryogenic requirements of the test mass. The final geometry must provide sufficient optical aperture and wavefront quality, while the sapphire substrate and dielectric coating must maintain sufficiently low absorption and mechanical loss. The absorbed optical power must remain compatible with the available cryogenic heat extraction, and the complete component must remain suitable for precision polishing, coating, handling, and integration with the cryogenic suspension.

The final test mass is therefore not obtained by optimizing a single
parameter such as mass, moment of inertia, or mechanical quality factor. Instead, the block dimensions, total bar length, moment of inertia, number and geometry of HCB interfaces, internal-mode structure, optical quality, and thermal transport must be optimized as a single integrated system.

17.16
Development Program

The development of the CHRONOS test mass will proceed from material
selection and component-level characterization toward validation of the complete cryogenic structure. The first stage is to determine the required mass distribution and moment of inertia from the mechanical design of the torsion-bar system and to combine this requirement with the minimum optical aperture defined by the interferometer beam geometry. On this basis, commercially realizable sapphire dimensions and crystallographic orientations will be surveyed, and candidate substrates will be characterized in terms of optical absorption, refractive-index homogeneity, birefringence, and mechanical loss.

The next stage is the experimental validation of the HCB mass-extension concept. Once representative bonded sapphire assemblies are fabricated, their mechanical quality factors will be measured with the ring-down method. These measurements will be compared with finite-element models based on the elastic-energy participation of the sapphire and HCB regions. The bonding area and interface geometry will then be optimized to reduce mechanical dissipation while maintaining sufficient structural strength. Mechanical-strength measurements will be performed in parallel with ring-down measurements in order to establish the relation between bond geometry, durability, and effective mechanical loss.

The suspension interface will subsequently be incorporated into the test-mass prototype. The sapphire bridge structure will be introduced into the mechanical model and experimentally characterized to determine its influence on the internal mode frequencies and losses. The assembled test mass will then be subjected to cryogenic ring-down
measurements and repeated thermal cycling in order to verify that its
mechanical quality factor, structural stability, and alignment are preserved during cooldown and warm-up.

In the final stage, the test mass will be integrated with the cryogenic
suspension and characterized as a complete suspended payload. The mechanical loss and alignment of the assembled structure will be verified under representative operating conditions. After the mechanical design has been validated, the final optical coating
will be applied and the completed mirror will be characterized for optical absorption, surface quality, and cryogenic mechanical performance.

This staged program is intended to ensure that the final CHRONOS test mass satisfies the mechanical, optical, and cryogenic requirements as a complete integrated structure rather than as a collection of independently optimized components.

17.17
Summary

The CHRONOS test mass is not simply an optical mirror.
It is an integrated optical–mechanical–thermal structure that defines the rotational dynamics of the cryogenic torsion-bar detector.

The baseline material is monocrystalline sapphire operated at approximately 4
\mathrm{K}
with a 1064
\mathrm{nm}
interferometer.

A major engineering challenge is that the required dimensions of the
torsion bar may exceed those practically available from a single
optical-quality sapphire crystal. CHRONOS therefore adopts hydroxide-catalysis bonding as a candidate technology for extending the mass distribution using multiple sapphire components.

The central design trade-off is between increasing the usable dimensions and moment of inertia and, introducing additional mechanical dissipation at the bonded interfaces.

The current reference design consists of five sapphire blocks forming a
1.10
\mathrm{m}
long, 171.32
\mathrm{kg}
torsion bar with a moment of inertia of 19.86
\mathrm{kg\,m^{2}}
.

Finite-element studies show that the mechanical loss introduced by HCB is controlled by the elastic-energy participation of the bonding region. This leads directly to the design strategy of maximizing monolithic sapphire block size, minimizing the number and area of bonds, and locating the bonds in mechanically favorable regions.

The bridge structure required for the crossed suspension geometry must also be regarded as an integral part of the test mass and included in the final mechanical optimization.

This integrated approach defines the development strategy for the
large-scale cryogenic test-mass mirror of CHRONOS.

Chapter 18  Optical Coatings

18.1
Introduction

The optical coatings of the CHRONOS test masses are a central part of the
instrument design, which determines the optical response of the interferometer with a
contribution to the cryogenic heat load via optical absorption and produces Brownian coating noise through mechanical dissipation. These requirements must be treated together rather than optimized independently. Coating
thermal noise is an important limitation in precision gravitational-wave
interferometers, while absorption becomes especially restrictive when absorbed
laser power must be extracted through a cryogenic suspension

[
128
,
129
,
130
]
.

The CHRONOS baseline considered in this chapter is a sapphire substrate with a
high-reflectivity (HR) coating on the interferometer-facing surface and an
anti-reflection (AR) coating on the opposite surface. The coating material
platform in development consists exclusively of silicon nitride
(SiN
x
or SiN) and silicon oxynitride (SiO
x
N
y
, abbreviated SiON) as SiN with relatively high-index and SiON in the material with relatively low-index as shown in Fig.
18.1
.
Both HR and AR designs will be based on measured, process-specific optical and
mechanical properties rather than on nominal bulk values.

The target wavelength for the baseline design is
1064\text{\,}\mathrm{n}\mathrm{m}
; an extension to

1550\text{\,}\mathrm{n}\mathrm{m}
remains of interest. The final numerical requirements for HR
transmission, AR reflectance, allowable absorption, scattering, and cryogenic
mechanical loss will be fixed by the CHRONOS optical and thermal
budgets at the system-level. Until those budgets are frozen, values reported in should be read
as material-development results rather than as demonstrated performance of a
detector-ready coating.

Figure 18.1:
Baseline CHRONOS coating concept. A sapphire test mass carries an HR multilayer on the interferometer-facing surface and an AR coating on the opposite surface.

18.2
Coating Requirements

An acceptable CHRONOS coating must provide the required HR reflectivity and AR
suppression at the operating wavelength and angle of incidence while
maintaining low absorptance, low wide-angle scatter, and low mechanical loss at
the CHRONOS operating temperature and in the relevant frequency band. It must
also exhibit acceptable residual stress, adhesion, surface figure, roughness,
and thickness uniformity on sapphire, together with stability under annealing,
handling, vacuum exposure, and repeated room–temperature-cryogenic thermal
cycling.

In a simplified scaling, the coating contribution to the displacement-noise
power spectral density has the form

S_{x}^{\mathrm{coat}}(f)\propto\frac{k_{\mathrm{B}}T}{fw^{2}}\,\phi_{\mathrm{eff}}(T,f),

(18.1)

where
T
is the temperature,
w
is the beam radius, and

\phi_{\mathrm{eff}}
is an effective loss angle that depends on layer
thicknesses, elastic properties, substrate properties, and the distribution of strain-energy. Equation (
18.1
) is only a scaling
relation; quantitative CHRONOS predictions will use the full multilayer elastic
model. Cryogenic operation reduces the explicit temperature factor but does
not guaranty a proportional noise improvement because amorphous films can
exhibit temperature-dependent dissipation peaks. Mechanical loss must
therefore be measured rather than extrapolated from room temperature.

Absorption is a system-level constraint. It can cause local heating
and thermo-optical distortion and, at cryogenic temperature, adds directly to the
heat that must be removed from the test mass. The HR and AR coatings must
therefore be evaluated together with sapphire bulk absorption and interface
losses.

18.3
SiN–SiON Material Platform

18.3.1
Silicon nitride

SiN
x
is attractive because its refractive index, optical gap, hydrogen
content, defect population, and residual stress can be adjusted through the
deposition parameters and stoichiometry
[
131
,
132
,
133
]
.
This flexibility is useful, but it also means that “SiN” does not imply a
single set of material constants. Si-rich and N-rich films have markedly different optical losses even when deposited by the same general
method. The conditions during the process must consequently be recorded together with the
composition and measured properties.

Whereas hydrogen can passivate under-coordinated sites, but a large population of
hydrogen-terminated bonds may also accompany network termination, voids, weak
bonds, or structural disorder
[
134
,
135
,
136
]
. Neither hydrogen content nor optical-gap position is therefore expected to be a unique
predictor of near-IR absorption.

18.3.2
Silicon oxynitride

SiON provides a compositionally tunable lower-index partner. The replacment of a
fraction of N by O changes the relative populations of Si–N and Si–O bonding
units, allowing the refractive index to vary between nitride- and
oxide-like values. The dependence is generally non-linear and process
dependent; a linear interpolation of refractive index should not be assumed
without measurement. SiON has been demonstrated as a candidate low-index
coating material for cryogenic gravitational-wave mirrors

[
137
,
138
,
139
]
.

Using related Si-based networks for both index components may simplify process integration and offer useful control of interfaces and stress. Nevertheless, the actual index contrast, absorption, mechanical loss, and
thermal properties have been established for the specific SiN and SiON recipes
used by CHRONOS.

18.4
Deposition Strategy

Chemical-vapor-deposition (CVD) routes under investigation includes plasma -
enhanced
CVD (PECVD) and low-pressure CVD (LPCVD) as illustrated in Fig.
18.2
. PECVD permits comparatively low
substrate temperatures and broad compositional control. LPCVD uses thermally
activated surface reactions at reduced pressure and can produce dense,
uniform films over multiple wafers. Typical LPCVD SiN growth from
dichlorosilane and ammonia is represented schematically by

3\,\mathrm{SiH_{2}Cl_{2}}+4\,\mathrm{NH_{3}}\longrightarrow\mathrm{Si_{3}N_{4}}+6\,\mathrm{HCl}+6\,\mathrm{H_{2}}.

(18.2)

The actual film can depart from the stoichiometric Si
3
N
4
and can contain
hydrogen; therefore, it is expressed as SiN
x
(:H) unless its composition has been
independently measured.

The primary surface-processing steps are precursor transport, adsorption,
surface reaction, and desorption of volatile by-products

[
140
,
141
]
. Temperature, total pressure, precursor ratio,
flow, residence time, and wafer position affect growth rate, composition,
stress, and optical properties. LPCVD should not be described a priori as the
“best” process: its higher thermal budget, tensile stress, precursor
chemistry, and compatibility with polished sapphire all require qualification.
The deposition route will be selected from the demonstrated optical, mechanical, and cryogenic performance at the coating-level.

Figure 18.2:
LPCVD setup and reactor design: photograph of the reactor and
schematic of the horizontal hot-wall system used for film deposition.

Figure 18.3:
Illustration of the LPCVD growth sequence, including precursor
transport, adsorption, thermally activated surface reactions, nucleation,
lateral growth, and formation of a continuous film.

18.5
Optical Design with theTransfer-Matrix Method

18.5.1
Single-interface reflection

At normal incidence, the amplitude reflection coefficient between two
non-magnetic media is

r_{12}=\frac{\tilde{n}_{1}-\tilde{n}_{2}}{\tilde{n}_{1}+\tilde{n}_{2}},\qquad R_{12}=|r_{12}|^{2},

(18.3)

where
\tilde{n}=n+i\kappa
is the complex refractive index. A bare
sapphire–vacuum surface therefore produces a non-negligible Fresnel reflection;
the AR coating is required to suppress that reflection and the associated
parasitic interferometers and scattered-light paths.

18.5.2
Quarter-wave starting point

For a conventional Bragg reflector at vacuum wavelength
\lambda_{0}
, the
starting design uses layers satisfying

n_{i}d_{i}=\frac{\lambda_{0}}{4},\qquad\delta_{i}=\frac{2\pi}{\lambda_{0}}n_{i}d_{i}=\frac{\pi}{2}.

(18.4)

Alternating high- and low-index layers then add their interface reflections
constructively. The index contrast controls the number of pairs needed for a
specified transmission. The familiar closed-form quarter-wave reflectance
depends on the incident index, substrate index, pair ordering, and terminating
layer; it is therefore safer to use the transfer-matrix calculation below than
to apply a substrate-independent approximation.

18.5.3
Characteristic matrices

At normal incidence, the characteristic matrix of the layer
i
is

\mathbf{M}_{i}=\begin{pmatrix}\cos\delta_{i}&i\sin\delta_{i}/\tilde{n}_{i}\\
i\tilde{n}_{i}\sin\delta_{i}&\cos\delta_{i}\end{pmatrix},\qquad\delta_{i}=\frac{2\pi}{\lambda}\tilde{n}_{i}d_{i}.

(18.5)

For
N
layers in physical order from the incident medium to the substrate,

\mathbf{M}=\mathbf{M}_{1}\mathbf{M}_{2}\cdots\mathbf{M}_{N}=\begin{pmatrix}A&B\\
C&D\end{pmatrix}.

(18.6)

If the incident medium and substrate have indices
n_{0}
and
\tilde{n}_{s}
, the
input optical admittance and amplitude reflection coefficient are

Y=\frac{C+D\tilde{n}_{s}}{A+B\tilde{n}_{s}},\qquad r=\frac{n_{0}-Y}{n_{0}+Y},\qquad R=|r|^{2}.

(18.7)

The same calculation yields transmission and the field in every layer. At
non-normal incidence, separate TE and TM admittances and angle-dependent phase
thicknesses will be used.

The local absorption density is proportional to the imaginary permittivity and
the local field intensity. For a planar stack,

\frac{P_{\mathrm{abs}}}{S}=\frac{\omega\epsilon_{0}}{2}\int\operatorname{Im}[\epsilon_{r}(z)]\,|E(z)|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}z.

(18.8)

Thus, the absorption of a layer embedded in an HR stack is not obtained by
linearly scaling the absorptance of an isolated film by thickness. Its
contribution depends on
n
,
\kappa
, thickness, wavelength, surrounding
layers, substrate index, and its position in the standing-wave field. This is
why measured single-layer data must first be converted, with an optical model,
to material parameters and then propagated through the complete HR or AR stack.

The quarter-wave design is only a starting point. The CHRONOS optimization may
depart from quarter-wave optical thicknesses to reduce field-weighted
absorption, total coating thickness, mechanical loss, or thermo-optic coupling
while retaining the specified HR transmission and AR reflectance.

18.5.4
SiN–SiON stack architecture

The nominal HR architecture is written as

\displaystyle\mathrm{vacuum}

(18.9)

\displaystyle\downarrow

\displaystyle(HL)^{N}

\displaystyle\downarrow

\displaystyle\mathrm{sapphire}

where
H
denotes a relatively high-index SiN layer,
L
denotes a relatively
low-index SiON layer, and
N
is the number of layer pairs. The actual
structure may include a matching layer at the sapphire interface and a cap
layer at the vacuum surface. Pair ordering, the terminating material, and cap
thickness affect reflectivity, surface electric field, environmental stability,
and absorption. Equation (
18.9
) therefore represents the
baseline architecture rather than a finalized layer sequence.

For an ideal lossless quarter-wave stack with the ordering
(HL)^{N}
, the
optical-admittance transformation gives

Y_{\mathrm{in}}=n_{s}\left(\frac{n_{H}}{n_{L}}\right)^{2N},\qquad R_{N}=\left|\frac{n_{0}-Y_{\mathrm{in}}}{n_{0}+Y_{\mathrm{in}}}\right|^{2},

(18.10)

where
n_{0}
and
n_{s}
are the incident-medium and sapphire indices. Reversing
the pair ordering reverses the admittance ratio. The required pair count thus
depends not only on index contrast but also on the substrate, layer ordering,
termination, and specified HR transmission. The real CHRONOS stack will use
complex indices and the complete transfer-matrix model instead of this ideal
expression.

At
1064\text{\,}\mathrm{n}\mathrm{m}
, the measured SiN indices from the existing campaign
correspond to quarter-wave physical thicknesses of approximately

114.6\text{\,}\mathrm{n}\mathrm{m}
to
135.8\text{\,}\mathrm{n}\mathrm{m}
. This range is illustrative. The SiON
quarter-wave thickness, index contrast, pair count, and total stack thickness
will be calculated after the FY2026 SiON index measurements. A literature
value will not be substituted as though it were a measured CHRONOS SiON recipe.

The standing-wave intensity is largest near the incident surface and generally
decays into an HR stack. The same material can consequently make a different
absorption contribution depending on its depth. The optimized structure may
use non-quarter-wave SiN layers, especially in the high-field region, to reduce
the optical energy stored in the more absorbing material. The SiON thicknesses
and, if necessary, pair count must then compensate for that change so that the
HR transmission remains within specification. Numerical optimization will
minimize field-weighted absorption and effective mechanical loss subject to
constraints on reflection, transmission, total thickness, stress, and
manufacturable layer thickness.

The AR coating will be treated as a separate optical-matching stack rather than
as a shortened HR reflector. A single-layer quarter-wave AR solution generally
requires an index close to
\sqrt{n_{0}n_{s}}
. If the available SiON index does
not provide the required match, a multilayer SiN–SiON design will be used.
Its optimization will include the sapphire index, birefringence, crystal
orientation, polarization, incidence angle, and required wavelength range.

Repeated interfaces introduce effects absent from isolated films. They can
increase scatter or interface absorption, while accumulated stress can curve
the optic or limit adhesion. Layer-thickness errors can shift the HR stop band
and raise the residual AR reflectance. Complete stack demonstrators are
therefore required after single-layer constants are known. Thickness and index
witnesses distributed across the deposition area will allow measured spectral
reflection and transmission to be compared with an as-built transfer-matrix
model rather than only with the nominal design.

18.6
Single-Layer SiN Characterization

The current LPCVD SiN campaign combines spectroscopic ellipsometry,
photothermal common-path interferometry (PCI), and Fourier-transform infrared
(FTIR) spectroscopy. These methods probe complementary quantities and should
not be treated as interchangeable.

\tilde{n}(\lambda)=n(\lambda)+i\kappa(\lambda),\qquad\alpha(\lambda)=\frac{4\pi\kappa(\lambda)}{\lambda}.

(18.11)

to

. This spread demonstrates useful index tunability, but also the

from the choice of the model, surface roughness, fitting range or assumed substrate
properties

]
.

18.6.3
Photothermal common-path interferometry

PCI measures the photothermal response produced by absorbed pump power and can
reach ppm sensitivity
[
147
,
148
]
.
The quantity reported is the absorptance of the complete coated-substrate sample
under the measurement conditions. It is not an intrinsic bulk absorption
coefficient of SiN, and it can include substrate and interface contributions as
well as thin-film interference effects.

Figure 18.5:
Photothermal common-path interferometry system used to measure the
absorptance of the deposited film–substrate samples.

The results of the reported single-layer SiN sample are summarized in
Table
18.4
. At
1064\text{\,}\mathrm{n}\mathrm{m}
, the absorptance reaches 10.2–682.6 ppm. While
\mathrm{SiN}
films achieved remarkably low absorption at
1064\text{\,}\mathrm{n}\mathrm{m}
lowering at
10.2\text{\,}\mathrm{p}\mathrm{p}\mathrm{m}
for the nominal
\mathrm{SiN}_{0.91}\mathrm{H}_{0.02}
sample, so by introducing oxygen into the matrix further enhanced optical performance. Shifting to
\mathrm{SiON}
dropped the
1064\text{\,}\mathrm{n}\mathrm{m}
absorptance into an even lower regime of 1.27–4.54 ppm, reaching a minimum of just
1.27\text{\,}\mathrm{p}\mathrm{p}\mathrm{m}
with nominal
\mathrm{SiO}_{0.74}\mathrm{N}_{0.49}\mathrm{H}_{0.1}
. This sudden reduction shows oxygen incorporation mitigating the residual absorption, positioning
\mathrm{SiON}
as a superior candidate for ultra-low-loss applications. These are directly measured sample absorptances, not predictions for a quarter-wave layer or a complete HR coating.

18.6.4
Correlation between optical absorption and bandgap parameters

For evaluating near IR optical loss mechanisms, the absorption measured at 1064 nm was correlated with the bandgap parameters extracted for the thin films
\mathrm{SiN}_{x}\mathrm{H}_{y}
, as shown in Fig.
18.6
.

Figure 18.6:
Sub-bandgap optical absorption at
\lambda=1064\mathrm{~nm}
(
A_{1064\mathrm{\,nm}}
) as a function of (a)
E_{\mathrm{g}}^{\mathrm{Tauc}}
, (b)
E_{\mathrm{g}}^{\mathrm{Cody}}
, (c)
E_{\mathrm{U}}
, and (d)
E_{\mathrm{g}}^{\mathrm{ideal}}
.

The absorption exhibits a non-monotonic trend across the sample set. The minimum loss (
\sim 10\text{--}18\text{~ppm}
) occurs at intermediate band gaps (
E_{\mathrm{g}}^{\mathrm{Tauc}}\approx 3.98\text{--}4.31\text{~eV}
)
[
143
]
. In unhydrogenated, Si-rich films like
\mathrm{SiN}_{0.74}
, the sharp drop in bandgap (
E_{\mathrm{g}}^{\mathrm{Tauc}}=2.26\text{~eV}
) correlates with a drastic increase in absorption (
682.6\text{~ppm}
) due to the mid-gap states introduced by silicon dangling bonds (
\mathrm{Si}^{0}
) and Si–Si bonds
[
143
,
149
]
. Conversely, elevated absorption in hydrogenated films with wider gaps (e.g.,
\mathrm{SiN}_{0.97}\mathrm{H}_{0.07}
at
192.5\text{~ppm}

[
143
]
) stems primarily from bond-vibrational overtones or hydrogen-induced defects
[
150
]
. The absence of a linear scaling with Urbach energy (
E_{\mathrm{U}}
) further indicates that the optical loss at
1064\text{~nm}
is driven by discrete deep-level state absorption rather than simple band-edge tail disorder
[
146
]
.

18.6.5
FTIR spectroscopy and hydrogen-related bonding

Transmission FTIR shown in Fig.
18.7
probes vibrational absorption associated with local bonding. After substrate correction and spectral fitting, the N–H stretching band near
3323\text{\,}\mathrm{-}

3332\text{\,}\mathrm{c}\mathrm{m}^{-1}
provides a comparative measure of N–H bond
population. Within the measured subset, films with the largest inferred N–H
bond densities also had comparatively large PCI absorptance. The relationship
was not unique: films with similar gap- or hydrogen-related metrics could still
have different near-infrared loss.

Figure 18.7:
FTIR measurement system: instrument photograph and schematic of the
transmission-mode Michelson-interferometer configuration.

The combined result is therefore multivariable. Near-infrared loss reflects
the nominal composition, optical-gap position, Urbach-tail width, hydrogen bonding, local disorder, and optical-field distribution. Independent composition and
hydrogen measurements such as, X-ray photoelectron spectroscopy,
Rutherford backscattering, elastic-recoil detection, or nuclear-reaction
analysis are required before nominal chemical labels are interpreted as
quantitative stoichiometry.

18.7
HR and AR Coatings on Sapphire

18.7.1
High-reflectivity coating

The HR design will use alternating SiN and SiON layers on sapphire. Candidate
stacks will be evaluated with the complex wavelength-dependent indices measured
for the actual deposition recipes. The optimization variables include pair
count, layer ordering, cap and matching layers, individual optical thicknesses,
and permitted composition changes. Some constraints will include transmission,
field-weighted absorption, total physical thickness, effective mechanical loss,
residual stress, and manufacturability.

For each fabricated HR demonstrator, an as-built model will use measured layer
thicknesses and dispersions to predict spectral reflection, transmission, and
the depth-dependent electric field. With direct measurements of reflection,
transmission, PCI absorptance, and scatter, the discrepancies will then be test whether discrepancies originate from thickness error, index error, bulk extinction, interfaces, or
roughness. The result will determine whether the next stack iteration should
change the material recipe, pair count, layer ordering, or individual optical
thicknesses.

The promising single-layer SiN absorptances in Table
18.4
do not
yet demonstrate an acceptable HR coating. A detector-ready result requires a
complete stack fabricated on sapphire, including the cumulative effects of
interfaces, roughness, stress, scatter, and the standing-wave field.

18.7.2
Anti-reflection coating

The rear surface will use a SiN–SiON AR design with substantially fewer layers
than the HR coating. Its function is to reduce the sapphire surface reflection
and thereby, limit parasitic cavities and scattered-light coupling. The design
must use the sapphire index, substrate wedge and incidence geometry, beam
polarization, and required spectral/angular bandwidth. Although fewer layers
usually reduce their mechanical-noise contribution, AR absorption still adds to
the cryogenic heat load and cannot be neglected.

The HR and AR structures will also be modeled together because light
transmitted by the HR coating propagates through the sapphire and encounters the
rear-surface stack. The combined model will include both coatings, substrate
thickness and wedge, coherent or incoherent propagation as appropriate to the
measurement geometry, and measured sapphire absorption.

18.7.3
Sapphire integration

The final optical and mechanical properties depend on the coating sapphire
system. Dedicated witness samples and representative sapphire optics will be
used to measure adhesion, interface absorption, scatter, roughness, figure,
stress, and thickness uniformity. Uncoated sapphire controls are necessary to
separate the substrate absorption from the PCI response. The optical model will
include the measured sapphire dispersion and any relevant birefringence and
crystal orientation.

Differences in thermal expansion and elastic properties among sapphire, SiN,
and SiON can change stress and curvature during cool-down. Representative
coatings must therefore undergo repeated thermal cycles to the CHRONOS operating
temperature, followed by optical, mechanical, and surface re-characterization.

18.8
FY2026 Measurement Plan and Development Roadmap

18.8.1
Measurements scheduled for FY2026

Mechanical-loss characterization and the systematic characterization of SiON
is being scheduled for FY2026 (Japanese fiscal year 2026). They are therefore
listed here as planned measurements, not as completed CHRONOS results.

Mechanical-loss measurements:

The program will first measure the resonant frequencies and quality factors of
uncoated reference resonators. Representative single-layer SiN and SiON films
will then be deposited and the ring-down measurements repeated. Coating
dilution factors will be determined from the measured film thicknesses,
substrate geometry, and elastic modeling in order to infer

\phi_{\mathrm{coat}}(T,f)
. The loss will be mapped from room temperature to
the CHRONOS cryogenic operating range with sufficiently dense temperature
sampling to identify possible dissipation peaks. Several resonant modes will
be measured to test frequency and mode-shape dependence. Residual stress and
curvature will be recorded before and after coating and after each thermal or
annealing treatment. The program will subsequently extend from single layers
to representative SiN–SiON multilayers to test interface and stack effects.

SiON measurements:

The SiON study will establish a controlled series of recipes spanning the
refractive-index range required for the low-index layers. The O, N, Si, and H
concentrations will be verified independently rather than inferred only from
precursor-flow ratios or nominal sample labels. Spectroscopic ellipsometry
will determine the thickness and wavelength-dependent
n
and
\kappa
at and
around
1064\text{\,}\mathrm{n}\mathrm{m}
and
1550\text{\,}\mathrm{n}\mathrm{m}
. PCI will measure coated-sample
absorptance at both wavelengths, accompanied by uncoated-substrate controls,
spatial scans, and repeated measurements. FTIR will characterize
Si–O-, Si–N-, N–H-, and Si–H-related bonding and correlate it with
composition and optical loss. Residual stress, thickness uniformity,
roughness, and scatter will first be measured on witness substrates and then on
sapphire. A transfer-matrix model will be used to extract wavelength-specific
extinction coefficients and predict complete SiN–SiON HR and AR stacks.
Selected recipes will subsequently undergo annealing and cryogenic thermal
cycling, followed by repeated optical, stress, and adhesion measurements.

The FY2026 data will provide the first measured mechanical-loss inputs and the
SiON material data required for integrated HR/AR optimization. A recipe will
not be selected from refractive index or room-temperature absorption alone.

18.8.2
Mechanical and thermal parameters required for final design

Optical optimization will ultimately be combined with measurements of

\phi_{i}(T,f),\quad Y_{i}(T),\quad\nu_{i}(T),\quad\alpha_{i}(T),\quad\kappa_{\mathrm{th},i}(T),

(18.12)

where
\phi_{i}
is mechanical loss,
Y_{i}
Young’s modulus,
\nu_{i}
Poisson’s
ratio,
\alpha_{i}
thermal expansion, and
\kappa_{\mathrm{th},i}
thermal
conductivity. Thermo-refractive coefficients and heat capacity will also be
measured or bounded. The FY2026 ring-down measurements on coated resonators
will be made
over the relevant temperature and frequency ranges, with dilution-factor
analysis used to infer coating loss. Single-layer loss measurements will be
supplemented by measurements of realistic HR and AR stacks because interfaces,
stress, and annealing can change multilayer behavior.

Post-deposition annealing is an explicit optimization variable. It may reduce
some defects or absorption, but it can also change hydrogen bonding, stress,
composition, crystallinity, adhesion, or sapphire figure. Each annealing
condition will therefore be evaluated with the full optical–mechanical test
set rather than selected from absorption alone.

18.8.3
Development sequence

The development begins in FY2026 by establishing reproducible SiON recipes and
independently verifying their composition and hydrogen content. Measurements
of
d
,
n
,
\kappa
, coated-sample absorptance, bonding, uniformity, stress,
and room-temperature mechanical loss will follow, together with spatially
repeated absorption measurements and uncoated-sapphire controls. The resulting
data will be used to infer wavelength specific extinction coefficients with
transfer-matrix models and to predict candidate HR and AR stacks.

The next stage will fabricate SiN–SiON HR and AR demonstrators on sapphire and
measure their transmission, reflection, absorption, scatter, figure, and
stress. Beginning in FY2026, single-layer and multilayer mechanical loss will
be measured from room temperature to the CHRONOS operating temperature, after
which the additional thermal properties required for the final design will be
determined. Annealing and layer thicknesses will then be optimized jointly.
Representative coated sapphire optics will finally be qualified through
cryogenic cycling and repeated optical and mechanical measurements.

18.9
Summary

The CHRONOS baseline is a sapphire test mass coated with SiN–SiON HR and AR
structures. Existing LPCVD SiN measurements demonstrate strong
composition-dependent tunability: the reported film–substrate absorptance
ranges from 10.2 to 682.6 ppm at
1064\text{\,}\mathrm{n}\mathrm{m}
, and the lowest reported value at

1550\text{\,}\mathrm{n}\mathrm{m}
is 7.59 ppm. These measurements identify promising recipes, but they
do not yet constitute the absorption of a quarter-wave layer or a complete HR
coating.

The principal design lesson is that neither refractive index, optical gap,
Urbach energy, nominal composition, nor hydrogen bonding uniquely predicts
near-infrared loss. CHRONOS will therefore combine ellipsometry, PCI, FTIR,
independent composition measurements, transfer-matrix modeling, and cryogenic
mechanical-loss measurements. Final material recipes and layer thicknesses
will be selected from complete HR and AR demonstrations on sapphire under the
optical, mechanical, thermal, and cryogenic conditions relevant to CHRONOS.
The completed coating program will therefore connect the present single-layer
SiN measurements, the FY2026 SiON and mechanical-loss measurements, and the
transfer-matrix stack optimization to a qualified pair of HR and AR coatings
for the sapphire test mass.

Chapter 19  Cryogenic System

The sensitivity of gravitational-wave detectors is fundamentally limited by a combination of intrinsic and environmental noise sources.
Continuous advancements in interferometry, optical design, suspension systems, and control techniques have led to a variety of proposed upgrades aimed at improving detector sensitivity

[
151
,
2
]
.
Among these noise sources, thermal noise arising from Brownian motion remains one of the dominant limitations, particularly in the low-frequency regime

[
21
,
22
,
23
]
.

A direct and effective approach to reducing thermal noise is cryogenic operation.
Lowering the temperature suppresses thermally driven fluctuations associated with mechanical dissipation, in accordance with the fluctuation–dissipation theorem

[
152
]
,
thereby improving the fundamental sensitivity of the detector.
For this reason, next-generation and future gravitational-wave detectors,
such as
LIGO Voyager

[
153
]
,

KAGRA

[
154
]
,
and the
Einstein Telescope

[
155
]
,
incorporate cryogenic operation as a key design element.
In
However, systems that have been optimized for room-temperature operation must be carefully re-evaluated under cryogenic conditions.
Material properties, mechanical response, optical performance, and control behavior all exhibit temperature dependence

[
156
,
157
]
,
requiring comprehensive validation in a low-temperature environment.

In addition to reducing thermal noise in the test mass,
cryogenic operation significantly affects the mechanical loss of optical coatings.
Since coating dissipation is strongly temperature-dependent,
operating at an appropriate cryogenic temperature can lead to substantial improvements in detector sensitivity

[
156
,
157
]
.
Thus, cryogenic technology plays a crucial role not only in thermal noise reduction
but also in enabling improved material performance.

On the other hand, the introduction of a cryogenic system introduces new technical challenges.
Mechanical vibrations originating from cryocoolers,
as well as structural resonances of the cryostat,
can couple to the test mass and degrade detector sensitivity

[
154
]
.
Therefore, minimizing vibration transmission is essential in cryogenic interferometers.
While passive isolation techniques have been widely employed,
they are often insufficient in the low-frequency band.

For future high-sensitivity detectors operating in the sub-Hz regime,
it is necessary to integrate advanced vibration mitigation strategies with thermal design.
In this study, we present the design concept of the cryogenic system for CHRONOS,
focusing on achieving both thermal noise reduction and effective vibration suppression.

19.1
Cryocooler and Heat Link

To realize cryogenic operation for thermal noise reduction,
we developed a dedicated cryogenic system for CHRONOS.
As shown in Fig.
19.1
,
the system consists of a cryogenic chamber coupled to the main interferometer chamber,
enabling cooling of the suspension system and the test mass.

Inside the chamber, a two-stage pulse-tube cryocooler (PTC)
with nominal 50 K and 4 K stages is installed.
Pulse-tube cryocoolers are widely used in low-vibration cryogenic applications
because they contain no moving parts at the cold head,
significantly reducing vibration transmission compared to conventional
Gifford–McMahon (GM) cryocoolers

[
158
,
154
]
.
The radiation shields at 300 K and 50 K suppress radiative heat transfer
from higher-temperature components,
allowing efficient cooling of the inner stages.

Figure 19.1:

Overall configuration of the cryogenic chamber.
The system consists of a two-stage pulse-tube cryocooler,
50 K and 4 K stages, and radiation shields at 300 K and 50 K.
The multi-stage shielding structure suppresses radiative heat inflow
from warm components.

Cooling is provided by a commercial two-stage pulse-tube cryocooler
(Sumitomo Heavy Industries, SRP-082B2-F70LP),
which provides cooling capacities at approximately 50 K (first stage)
and 4 K (second stage).
Such cryogenic architectures are also adopted in KAGRA
for low-vibration mirror cooling

[
154
]
.

The cold heads are thermally connected to each temperature stage via heat links,
while mechanical isolation is maintained through low-conductivity supports.
As shown in Fig.
19.3
,
the 50 K stage is suspended from the 300 K structure
using low-thermal-conductivity polyimide (Vespel®) tubes.
Similarly, the 4 K stage is supported from the 50 K stage using identical structures.
This configuration minimizes parasitic heat conduction
while maintaining mechanical stability.
To suppress residual gas conduction,
vent holes are introduced in the mounting screws
to evacuate trapped gas inside the support tubes.

The heat links play a crucial role in achieving both efficient cooling
and vibration isolation.
Mechanical vibrations generated inside the cryocooler,
such as pressure oscillations of the working gas,
can propagate through rigid thermal connections and couple to the test mass

[
158
]
.
To suppress this effect, we adopt flexible heat links composed of bundles of thin wires
rather than a single rigid conductor.
For the same cross-sectional area,
such a stranded structure provides significantly lower stiffness,
thereby reducing vibration transmission while maintaining high thermal conductivity.
High-purity copper is used for the 50 K stage,
while high-purity aluminum (99.9999%, 6N) is used for the 4 K stage,
taking advantage of their excellent cryogenic thermal conductivity

[
159
]
.

To further reduce radiative heat transfer,
multi-layer insulation (MLI) is applied to both stages.
MLI consists of multiple reflective layers and is highly effective
in suppressing radiation, which dominates heat transfer in vacuum environments

[
159
]
.
In this system, approximately 50 layers are applied to the 50 K stage
and 30 layers to the 4 K stage,
significantly reducing radiative heat load and improving cooling efficiency.

In summary, the cryogenic system is designed to simultaneously achieve
low temperature and low vibration by combining:
(i) multi-stage radiation shielding,
(ii) low thermal-conductivity supports to suppress parasitic heat flow, and
(iii) low-stiffness heat links for vibration isolation.

In addition to passive isolation,
further reduction of residual vibration from the pulse-tube cryocooler
is pursued using active vibration isolation (AVI).
In an active scheme, vibrations are measured using sensors
and suppressed by applying counteracting forces through actuators.
Such feedback-based vibration suppression techniques
are widely employed in precision interferometry

[
2
,
154
]
.
This approach is particularly important for periodic low-frequency vibrations
originating from the cryocooler,
where passive isolation alone is insufficient.

The design and performance of the active vibration isolation system
are described in detail in the following section.

Figure 19.2:

Two-stage pulse-tube cryocooler used in this system.
The first stage cools to
\sim
50 K and the second stage to
\sim
4 K.
The absence of moving parts at the cold head provides low-vibration operation suitable for precision interferometry.

Figure 19.3:

Structure of the cold heads and thermal stages.
Low-conductivity Vespel supports reduce parasitic heat flow,
while heat links provide efficient thermal coupling.
This design balances thermal isolation and mechanical stability.

19.2
Cryogenic Performance

We evaluated the cryogenic performance of the system,
with particular emphasis on quantifying heat loads arising
from thermal conduction and radiation.
To this end, controlled heat inputs were applied to the 4 K and 50 K stages,
and the corresponding temperature responses were measured.
This evaluation is essential for validating the thermal design
and assessing the cooling margin under cryogenic operation

[
159
]
.

During the measurements, the cold heads were thermally decoupled
from each stage to isolate the intrinsic cooling performance
of the cryocooler.
This configuration allows direct evaluation of the response
to externally applied heat loads.
Ceramic heaters were mounted on each stage,
and the injected power was precisely determined
using four-wire measurements of voltage and current.
By varying the applied voltage,
heat loads in the range of approximately
0–25 W for the 50 K stage and
0–2 W for the 4 K stage were introduced.

Figure
19.4
shows the relationship
between the applied heater power and the steady-state temperature
of each stage.
From the slope of these curves,
the effective thermal conductance and radiative heat load were evaluated.
The heat balance can be expressed as

Q_{\mathrm{in}}=Q_{\mathrm{cond}}+Q_{\mathrm{rad}}+Q_{\mathrm{cool}},

(19.1)

where conductive heat flow follows Fourier’s law
and radiative heat load follows the Stefan–Boltzmann relation

[
159
]
.

The measured effective parasitic heat loads
at 4.2 K and 30 K are approximately
0.5 W and 5 W, respectively.
These values are consistent with theoretical estimates
based on the number of MLI layers,
the thermal conductivity of the support structures,
and the heat-link design.
They are also comparable to typical performance levels
of cryogenic systems of similar scale,
such as those implemented in KAGRA

[
154
]
.

To characterize the cooling dynamics,
we measured the cooldown process from room temperature
to operating temperature.
Silicon diode thermometers were used to monitor
the temperature evolution of the cold heads and each stage simultaneously.
The temporal temperature response is shown in Fig.
19.5
.
The system reaches operating temperatures of
4 K and 50 K in approximately 1000 minutes (about 17 hours),
which is consistent with the expected thermal mass
and cooling power of the cryocooler.

Temperature measurements were performed
using calibrated silicon diode sensors
(DT-680B-CU, Lake Shore Cryogenics),
whose temperature dependence is well characterized
in the cryogenic range
[
160
]
.
Four sensors were installed:
one on each of the 4 K and 50 K cold heads,
and one on each of the corresponding stages.
This configuration enables quantitative evaluation
of the temperature gradient between the cold heads and stages,
providing insight into thermal resistance
and heat-transfer efficiency.

These results demonstrate that the cryogenic system
achieves the designed cooling performance
and provides sufficient thermal conditions
for realizing a low thermal-noise environment in CHRONOS.

Figure 19.4:

Thermal loading map of the 4 K and 50 K stages.
The horizontal axis shows the applied heater power, and the vertical axis shows the steady-state temperature.
The slope of each curve provides a measure of the effective thermal conductance and radiative heat load.
A stable thermal response is observed over the full range of applied power.

Figure 19.5:

Temperature evolution during the cooldown process.
The 50 K stage cools first, followed by the 4 K stage.
The system reaches operating temperatures in approximately 1000 minutes (17 hours),
indicating efficient thermal design of the cryostat and heat-link system.

19.2.1
Active Vibration Isolation System

One of the major challenges in cryogenic systems is periodic mechanical vibration
originating from the cryocooler and compressor.
Although PTC exhibit lower vibration compared to
conventional cryocoolers, residual pressure oscillations and gas flow
inside the system generate periodic disturbances,
particularly in the low-frequency band

[
158
,
154
]
.
These vibrations can propagate through heat links and support structures
and couple to the test mass,
potentially limiting the detector sensitivity.

As discussed in the previous section,
passive isolation methods such as bellows and low-stiffness heat links
are effective at higher frequencies,
but their performance degrades in the low-frequency regime

[
2
]
.
For CHRONOS, which targets high sensitivity in the sub-Hz band,
active vibration suppression is therefore essential.

To address this issue,
we developed an active vibration isolation system (AVIS).
A schematic overview of the system is shown in Fig.
19.6
.

The AVIS directly controls the relative motion between the cryocooler
and the ground support structure.
The pulse-tube cryocooler is mounted to the 300 K chamber lid via bellows,
which provide passive vibration isolation.
In addition, six piezoelectric actuators (PZTs)
are installed to actively control the relative displacement
between the cryocooler and the support structure.

The system operates based on feedback control theory.
The static equilibrium position of the cryocooler
is defined as the operating point.
Sensor signals measuring displacement and acceleration are continuously monitored,
and the actuators generate counteracting displacements
with opposite phase,
thereby canceling vibration components.
This approach is particularly effective for suppressing
periodic disturbances originating from the cryocooler.

The system is designed to control six degrees of freedom (6-DOF),
consisting of three translational motions (
x
,
y
,
z
)
and three rotational motions (roll, pitch, and yaw).
By appropriately arranging sensors and actuators,
simultaneous control of all degrees of freedom is achieved,
similar to multi-axis active isolation platforms
used in advanced interferometers

[
2
]
.

A photograph of the installed cryogenic system
is shown in Fig.
19.7
.
The cryocooler is located inside the cryogenic chamber,
while the compressor and drive units are installed externally.
The AVIS is positioned between the cryocooler
and the ground support structure,
actively suppressing vibrations originating from the cryogenic system.

Figure
19.8
shows the detailed configuration of the AVIS.
Multiple rigid stages are attached above the cryocooler
to mount the sensors.
Accelerometers are installed on the outer top stage,
while reflective targets for the photosensors
are mounted on a windmill-shaped plate.
This configuration allows simultaneous detection
of horizontal and vertical vibrations.

The six piezoelectric actuators are arranged between
the top plate and the outer stage,
enabling force application in all degrees of freedom.
This configuration realizes real-time feedback control
of the full 6-DOF motion.

In this section, we have described the overall architecture
and operating principle of the AVIS.
The detailed specifications and performance
of the sensors and actuators
are presented in the following section.

Figure 19.6:

Schematic of the AVIS.
The yellow structure is fixed to the ground and serves as a reference frame,
while the red structure is connected to the cryocooler.
They are coupled via bellows and piezoelectric actuators,
allowing active control of relative motion.
Multiple sensors (accelerometers and photosensors) enable six-degree-of-freedom control.

Figure 19.7:

Photograph of the cryogenic system.
The pulse-tube cryocooler is installed inside the chamber,
and the AVIS is placed between the cryocooler and the ground support structure.

Figure 19.8:

Integrated setup of the AVIS.
Accelerometers (for high-frequency components) and photosensors (for low-frequency components)
measure vibrations, while six piezoelectric actuators generate compensating motion.
Simultaneous control of all six degrees of freedom is achieved.

19.2.2
Photosensors

To monitor the relative displacement between the cryocooler and the ground support structure,
non-contact photosensors are employed in this system.
Since the measurement is performed optically,
the sensors introduce no mechanical loading on the system,
making them particularly suitable for detecting small displacements
in the low-frequency regime
[
2
]
.

As shown in Fig.
19.9
,
the photosensor is a reflective optical device consisting of one LED and two photodiodes (PDs).
Light emitted from the LED is reflected by a target mirror and detected by the PDs.
The relative displacement between the sensor and the target
is inferred from the change in the reflected light intensity,
following standard optical position-sensing principle.

Figure 19.9:

Schematic of the photosensor.
Light emitted from the LED is reflected by a target and detected by photodiodes.
Displacement is measured from the change in reflected light intensity.

The photosensors provide a wide dynamic range
and are effective for monitoring slow drifts and low-frequency vibrations.
In this system, three horizontal and three vertical sensors
are arranged to reconstruct the 6-DOF motion
of the cryocooler,
similar to multi-axis sensing schemes used in active isolation platforms as shown in Fig.
19.10

[
2
]
.

Figure 19.10:

Configuration of the photosensors and coordinate system.
Three horizontal and three vertical sensors are used to reconstruct 6-DOF motion.

Detailed characterization of the sensor performance,
including operation under cryogenic conditions,
is under ongoing study.

19.2.3
Piezoelectric Actuators

Active vibration compensation is achieved using PZTs.
Piezoelectric elements convert applied voltage into mechanical displacement,
providing fast response and high resolution suitable for precision control

[
161
]
.

The AVIS consists of six supporting legs, each equipped with one PZT.
As shown in Fig.
19.11
,
the actuators are arranged with an inclination angle of approximately
15^{\circ}
,
allowing control of three translational and three rotational degrees of freedom.
Such geometric arrangements enable full 6-DOF control
through appropriate transformation between actuator space and Cartesian coordinates.

Figure 19.11:

Arrangement of the piezoelectric actuators.
Six PZTs are symmetrically placed,
enabling control of all six degrees of freedom.

The actuators are installed between the top plate and the support structure,
and apply forces to compensate for the relative displacement
between the cryocooler and the ground.
Control signals are generated by a digital control system
and independently applied to each actuator,
enabling flexible multi-degree-of-freedom control.

19.2.4
Control and Transfer Function

The control performance of the AVIS is characterized
through measurements of the open-loop transfer function.
A swept excitation signal is injected into the control system,
and the system response is analyzed to obtain the frequency response.

The loop dynamics can be described by the standard feedback relation

T(\Omega)=\frac{G(\Omega)P(\Omega)}{1+G(\Omega)P(\Omega)},

(19.2)

where
P(\Omega)
is the plant transfer function
and
G(\Omega)
is the controller transfer function.

Based on the measured Bode plots,
digital filters are designed to compensate for phase delay
and stabilize the feedback loop.
A representative transfer function is shown in Fig.
19.12
.

The unity gain frequency (UGF) is approximately 0.5 Hz,
indicating that the feedback control is effective
in the sub-Hz to 0.5 Hz range.
A phase margin of about
55^{\circ}

ensures stable operation of the control system,
consistent with standard stability criteria.

Figure 19.12:

Open-loop transfer function of the control system.
The unity gain frequency is approximately 1 Hz,
and the phase margin indicates stable feedback operation.

These results demonstrate that the AVIS provides sufficient control bandwidth
to suppress low-frequency vibrations originating from the cryocooler.

19.2.5
Noise Performance

The vibration suppression performance of the AVIS
was evaluated by comparing noise spectra before and after feedback control.

Initially, each degree of freedom was controlled independently,
confirming that noise reduction is achieved
in the frequency range below 1 Hz.
Subsequently, the number of controlled degrees of freedom
was gradually increased,
leading to full 6-DOF simultaneous feedback control.

The system stability during multi-degree-of-freedom operation
was verified through transfer function measurements,
confirming stable control across all degrees of freedom.

Figure
19.13
shows the measured noise spectra
before and after feedback control.
Significant noise reduction is observed in the low-frequency region below 1 Hz,
with improvements seen in all degrees of freedom.
In particular, a pronounced reduction is observed in the
y
direction.

Figure 19.13:

Comparison of noise spectra before and after feedback control.
Significant vibration reduction is achieved below 1 Hz
with full 6-DOF control.

These results demonstrate that the AVIS effectively suppresses
cryocooler-induced vibrations over a broad frequency range,
and is particularly effective in the low-frequency band
relevant for CHRONOS.

Chapter 20  Photon-Pressure Actuator

Low-frequency interferometers require actuators that can maintain
interferometer lock without introducing excess force noise into the test mass. In conventional gravitational-wave detectors, electromagnetic or electrostatic actuators apply forces between the test mass and a nearby recoil mass. Although these schemes provide a large actuation range, motion of the recoil mass can couple seismic motion to the test mass through the actuator. Electromagnetic actuators can additionally couple fluctuations of the environmental magnetic field to the test-mass motion.

CHRONOS therefore adopts a photon-pressure actuator (PPA) as the primary low-noise actuator for the test-mass stage. The PPA transfers photon momentum directly to the torsion-bar test mass using power-controlled laser beams. Since no mechanically-coupled recoil mass is required, the PPA can suppress actuation noise associated with motion of a recoil mass. Photon pressure itself is also intrinsically insensitive to environmental magnetic-field fluctuations. This configuration potentially allows the recoil mass to be omitted from the
test-mass stage, substantially simplifying the final stage of the CHRONOS suspension.

The same optical system can simultaneously operate as a Photon Calibrator (PCal), because both actuation and calibration rely on the same radiation pressure force. A calibrated sinusoidal modulation can be superimposed on the feedback actuation signal without changing the optical configuration. The PPA and PCal can therefore be realized as a two-in-one system for lock acquisition, lock maintenance, actuator characterization, and absolute calibration
[
162
,
163
,
164
,
165
]
. A slight discrepancy can be induced by a tail of the internal resonant peaks, although these peaks are outside the science band. Refer Chap.
24
for overview of calibration scheme.

The CHRONOS PPA uses four independently controlled laser beams incident on the back surface of each torsion-bar test mass. Appropriate combinations of the four beam powers generate longitudinal force, yaw torque, and pitch torque independently. A detailed design study predicts a maximum yaw torque amplitude of approximately
1.0\times 10^{-8}\,\mathrm{N\,m}
and an actuation efficiency
of
6.6\times 10^{-13}\,\mathrm{rad/V}
at 1 Hz. The corresponding yaw actuation noise is estimated to be
5.3\times 10^{-19}\,\mathrm{rad/\sqrt{Hz}}
at 1 Hz, whose differential strain conversion is compared with the updated
reference sensitivity in Sec.
20.6
. When operated as a PCal, the projected systematic uncertainty is approximately
1.14\%

[
165
]
.

20.1
Actuation principle

The basic principle of the photon-pressure actuator is to control the optical power incident on the test mass and thereby control the radiation-pressure force. To actuate several rigid-body degrees of freedom independently while avoiding interference with the main interferometer beam, four auxiliary laser beams are injected onto the back surface of the torsion-bar test mass.

The conceptual arrangement of the beam positions is shown in Fig.
20.1
. The four injection points are approximately symmetrically distributed around the center of mass.

Figure 20.1:

Conceptual arrangement and indexing of the four photon-pressure beams incident on the back surface of the CHRONOS torsion-bar test mass. Independent modulation of the four beam powers provides longitudinal, yaw, and pitch actuation.

We label the four beams by the index
i=1,2,3,4
. The back surface of the torsion bar defines the
x
–
y
plane and the
z
axis is taken along the surface normal. The corresponding unit vectors are denoted by

\mathbf{e}_{x}
,
\mathbf{e}_{y}
, and
\mathbf{e}_{z}
. The position of the
i
-th beam relative to the center of mass is

\mathbf{r}_{i}=\left(r_{i}^{x},r_{i}^{y},0\right).

(20.1)

We parameterize each beam by its optical power
P_{i}(t)
, azimuthal incidence angle
\theta_{i}
, and inclination angle
\phi_{i}
. Assuming specular reflection, the radiation-pressure force applied by the

i
-th beam is

\mathbf{F}_{i}(t)=\frac{2P_{i}(t)}{c}\begin{pmatrix}\cos\phi_{i}\sin\theta_{i}\\
\sin\phi_{i}\\
\cos\phi_{i}\cos\theta_{i}\end{pmatrix},

(20.2)

where
c
is the speed of light.

The net force along the translational direction is

F_{\rm T}(t)=\sum_{i=1}^{4}\mathbf{F}_{i}(t)\cdot\mathbf{e}_{z}.

(20.3)

The corresponding yaw and pitch torques are

T_{\rm Y}(t)=\sum_{i=1}^{4}\left(\mathbf{r}_{i}\times\mathbf{F}_{i}(t)\right)\cdot\mathbf{e}_{y},

(20.4)

and

T_{\rm P}(t)=\sum_{i=1}^{4}\left(\mathbf{r}_{i}\times\mathbf{F}_{i}(t)\right)\cdot\mathbf{e}_{x}.

(20.5)

Different linear combinations of the four beam powers therefore correspond to different rigid-body actuation modes. A common-mode modulation primarily generates longitudinal force, whereas an
antisymmetric modulation between the left and right sides of the bar
generates yaw torque. Similarly, a differential modulation between the upper and lower beams produces pitch torque.

20.2
Mechanical response

The equations of motion for longitudinal translation, yaw, and pitch are

M\ddot{z}(t)+\gamma_{\rm T}\dot{z}(t)+k_{\rm T}z(t)=F_{\rm T}(t),

(20.6)

I_{\rm Y}\ddot{\varphi}_{\rm Y}(t)+\Gamma_{\rm Y}\dot{\varphi}_{\rm Y}(t)+K_{\rm Y}\varphi_{\rm Y}(t)=T_{\rm Y}(t),

(20.7)

and

I_{\rm P}\ddot{\varphi}_{\rm P}(t)+\Gamma_{\rm P}\dot{\varphi}_{\rm P}(t)+K_{\rm P}\varphi_{\rm P}(t)=T_{\rm P}(t),

(20.8)

where
M
is the mass of the torsion bar,
I_{\rm Y}
and
I_{\rm P}
are its moments of inertia about the yaw and pitch axes, respectively,
\gamma_{\rm T}
and
\Gamma_{j}
are damping coefficients, and
k_{\rm T}
and
K_{j}
are the corresponding restoring coefficients.

In the frequency domain, with
\omega=2\pi f
, these equations become

\left(-M\omega^{2}+i\omega\gamma_{\rm T}+k_{\rm T}\right)z(\omega)=F_{\rm T}(\omega),

(20.9)

\left(-I_{\rm Y}\omega^{2}+i\omega\Gamma_{\rm Y}+K_{\rm Y}\right)\varphi_{\rm Y}(\omega)=T_{\rm Y}(\omega),

(20.10)

and

\left(-I_{\rm P}\omega^{2}+i\omega\Gamma_{\rm P}+K_{\rm P}\right)\varphi_{\rm P}(\omega)=T_{\rm P}(\omega).

(20.11)

The resonant frequencies and quality factors are defined as

\Omega_{\rm T}=\sqrt{\frac{k_{\rm T}}{M}},\qquad\Omega_{\rm Y}=\sqrt{\frac{K_{\rm Y}}{I_{\rm Y}}},\qquad\Omega_{\rm P}=\sqrt{\frac{K_{\rm P}}{I_{\rm P}}},

(20.12)

and

Q_{\rm T}=\frac{\Omega_{\rm T}M}{\gamma_{\rm T}},\qquad Q_{\rm Y}=\frac{\Omega_{\rm Y}I_{\rm Y}}{\Gamma_{\rm Y}},\qquad Q_{\rm P}=\frac{\Omega_{\rm P}I_{\rm P}}{\Gamma_{\rm P}},

(20.13)

The mechanical responses in displacement and rotation are

z(\omega)=-\frac{F_{\rm T}(\omega)}{M\left(\omega^{2}-i\Omega_{\rm T}\omega/Q_{\rm T}-\Omega_{\rm T}^{2}\right)},

(20.14)

\varphi_{\rm Y}(\omega)=-\frac{T_{\rm Y}(\omega)}{I_{\rm Y}\left(\omega^{2}-i\Omega_{\rm Y}\omega/Q_{\rm Y}-\Omega_{\rm Y}^{2}\right)},

(20.15)

and

\varphi_{\rm P}(\omega)=-\frac{T_{\rm P}(\omega)}{I_{\rm P}\left(\omega^{2}-i\Omega_{\rm P}\omega/Q_{\rm P}-\Omega_{\rm P}^{2}\right)}.

(20.16)

At frequencies sufficiently above the corresponding rigid-body resonances, the responses approach those of a free mass or free rotor:

z(\omega)\simeq-\frac{F_{\rm T}(\omega)}{M\omega^{2}},

(20.17)

\varphi_{\rm Y}(\omega)\simeq-\frac{T_{\rm Y}(\omega)}{I_{\rm Y}\omega^{2}},

(20.18)

and

\varphi_{\rm P}(\omega)\simeq-\frac{T_{\rm P}(\omega)}{I_{\rm P}\omega^{2}}.

(20.19)

This simple
1/\omega^{2}
mechanical response is particularly useful for
calibration because the applied force is determined from the independently measured optical power.

20.3
Optical configuration

The optical layout of the four-beam photon-pressure actuator is shown in Fig.
20.2
. The system consists of a transmitter module, a beam-steering periscope, and a receiver module. The transmitter module generates and independently controls four laser beams. The periscope determines their injection positions and angles on the test mass, while the receiver module monitors the reflected powers and beam positions.

Figure 20.2:

Conceptual optical layout of the CHRONOS photon-pressure actuator.
Four independently controlled beams are injected onto the back surface of one torsion-bar test mass. The system consists of a transmitter module, a beam-steering periscope, and a receiver module.

A fiber-coupled continuous-wave laser at a wavelength of 1550 nm is used as the optical source. The laser is divided into four independent optical paths. Each path contains an acousto-optic modulator (AOM), whose first-order diffraction power is controlled by an optical follower servo (OFS). An external digital signal is applied to the OFS to provide the actuation command.

Figure
20.3
shows the transmitter module. In each beam path, an in-loop photodetector, denoted by OFSPD, measures the optical power and provides the feedback signal for the OFS. An independent out-of-loop photodetector, denoted by TxPD, monitors the actual transmitted optical power. The TxPD is coupled to an integrating sphere so that the total sampled beam power is measured without changing sensitivity by fluctuation of beam shape. A diffractive beam sampler extracts only a small fraction of each beam for these measurements.

The final mirrors of the transmitter module are remotely adjustable, allowing the four beam positions to be aligned to their designated locations on the test mass.

Figure 20.3:

Conceptual optical layout of the transmitter module of the CHRONOS
photon-pressure actuator. The optical powers of the four beams are independently stabilized using AOMs and optical follower servos.

The beams reflected by the test mass are collected by the receiver module, shown in Fig.
20.4
. The returned optical power is monitored by integrating-sphere coupled to photodetectors, denoted by RxPD. A small fraction of each reflected beam is additionally sampled and directed onto quadrant photodetectors (QPDs) to monitor the beam positions.

Beam-position monitoring is especially important for PCal operation because an uncertainty in the injection position directly propagates to an uncertainty in the applied torque
[
166
]
.

Figure 20.4:

Conceptual optical layout of the receiver module. The reflected optical power is monitored using integrating-sphere photodetectors, while quadrant photodetectors monitor the positions of the four reflected beams.

20.4
Beam-position optimization

The injection positions cannot be determined only by maximizing the
rigid-body torque. Photon pressure can excite internal elastic modes of the torsion bar and produce surface deformation which potentially couples to the interferometer readout.

The CHRONOS torsion bar has prominent internal resonant modes near
667 Hz and 1238 Hz as shown in Fig.
20.5
. The first is predominantly a vertical bending mode, whereas the latter is a horizontal bending mode. The horizontal mode is particularly relevant to the yaw readout because it causes surface displacement approximately along the axis of the main interferometer beam. This effect is canceled out between the left and right side of the bar when the main beam positions are ideally symmetric, but it can couple to the interferometer in the practical case where the main beams are displaced from the nominal position
[
20
]
.

Figure 20.5:

Shape of the three lowest internal modes of the CHRONOS torsion bar with five units. (a) vertical bending mode at 666.6 Hz, (b) horizontal bending mode at 1237.7 Hz, (c) twisting mode at 1398.9 Hz. The mode shape was obtained by COMSOL.

The PPA injection positions are therefore chosen close to the nodal line of the horizontal bending mode. At the same time, the positions are selected to provide the largest yaw lever arm while avoiding bonding layers between the sapphire blocks. The resulting injection points based on the simulated 1238-Hz mode shape are shown in Fig.
20.6
.

Figure 20.6:

Photon-pressure injection positions superimposed on the simulated
horizontal bending mode of the CHRONOS torsion bar near 1238 Hz.
The beam positions are selected close to the nodal line in order to
suppress excitation of the internal elastic mode. The mode shape was obtained by COMSOL.

The representative design parameters are summarized in
Table
20.1
.

Baseline parameters of the CHRONOS photon-pressure actuator.

The design values follow the current CHRONOS PPA study, in which a
10-W 1550-nm laser is divided into four paths and an actuation amplitude of approximately
\pm 1.25
W per beam is available after the AOM power-control stage
[
