]
.

20.5
Actuation range

Using the parameters in Table
20.1
, the maximum
radiation-pressure force generated by one beam is of the order of

F_{i}\simeq\frac{2P_{i}}{c}\simeq 8.3\times 10^{-9}\ {\rm N},

(20.20)

for
P_{i}=1.25
W and near-normal incidence.

Combining the four beams gives the maximum translational force amplitude of

F_{\rm T,max}\simeq 3.3\times 10^{-8}\ {\rm N}.

(20.21)

The corresponding maximum torque amplitudes are approximately

T_{\rm Y,max}\simeq 1.0\times 10^{-8}\ {\rm N\,m},

(20.22)

for yaw and

T_{\rm P,max}\simeq 1.2\times 10^{-9}\ {\rm N\,m},

(20.23)

for pitch.

At 1 Hz, the corresponding actuation efficiencies are approximately

A_{\rm T}\simeq 2.5\times 10^{-13}\ {\rm m/V},

(20.24)

A_{\rm Y}\simeq 6.6\times 10^{-13}\ {\rm rad/V},

(20.25)

and

A_{\rm P}\simeq 1.2\times 10^{-12}\ {\rm rad/V}.

(20.26)

These values provide sufficient actuation authority for maintaining
interferometer lock. The predicted available force is also sufficient for lock acquisition under the present estimate of the CHRONOS seismic motion, although the final requirement must be validated using the measured seismic environment at the experimental site

[
165
]
.

This capability is important for the suspension design because it allows the test-mass stage to operate without a dedicated recoil mass.
Local recoil masses can therefore be retained at intermediate suspension stages where required, while the final torsion-bar test mass can be actuated by mechanically contactless photon pressure.

20.6
Actuation noise

The relevant noise of the photon-pressure actuator enters primarily through fluctuations of the optical power delivered to the test mass.
The principal contributions are intrinsic laser intensity noise,
photodetector shot noise, and electronic noise introduced through the
intensity-control system
[
165
]
.

The beam power is stabilized by a feedback loop consisting of the OFSPD, OFS, and AOM. The power-control loop suppresses the intrinsic laser intensity noise, but noise from the photodetectors, AOM electronics, and digital-to-analog converters can be introduced through the same control path.

A particularly important contribution is the noise of the DAC used to provide the excitation and offset signals to the OFS. For the baseline design, the DAC-related excitation noise is expected to be the dominant actuation-noise contribution. Analog dewhitening between the DAC and OFS is therefore included in the control concept to reduce this noise.

An important property of the four-beam configuration is that the intrinsic laser intensity noise is largely common to the four optical paths. For yaw actuation, fluctuations of the common power on opposite sides of the torsion bar generate torques of opposite sign and therefore cancel to first order. The remaining coupling is determined by mismatches in beam power and beam position
[
20
]
. Independent electronic noise from the four control channels instead adds incoherently. The noise contribution from each component is shown in Fig.
20.7
.

Figure 20.7:

Relative power noise budget of the total noise power injected to the ETM. Normalized by the maximum power
P_{0}=
2.5 W.

At the baseline configuration, the predicted noise amplitudes at 1 Hz are

\delta z_{\rm PPA}\simeq 3.9\times 10^{-19}\ {\rm m/\sqrt{Hz}},

(20.27)

\delta\varphi_{\rm Y,PPA}\simeq 5.3\times 10^{-19}\ {\rm rad/\sqrt{Hz}},

(20.28)

and

\delta\varphi_{\rm P,PPA}\simeq 1.1\times 10^{-18}\ {\rm rad/\sqrt{Hz}}.

(20.29)

The quoted yaw noise is a single-bar angular quantity. If the two bars have
independent equal PPA noise, their differential ASD at 1 Hz is

\sqrt{2}\,(5.3\times 10^{-19})=7.5\times 10^{-19}~\mathrm{rad/\sqrt{Hz}}
.
Using
\theta_{X}-\theta_{Y}=\eta h
,
\eta=0.93656
, this is

8.0\times 10^{-19}~\mathrm{Hz}^{-1/2}
in strain, compared with

3.14\times 10^{-18}
and
1.97\times 10^{-18}~\mathrm{Hz}^{-1/2}
for the
minimum-PI and logarithmic-mean detector budgets at 1 Hz. This establishes
a margin at that frequency under the independence assumption. The full
frequency dependence and inter-bar correlations must be propagated before
claiming a margin across the band. Additional PPA noise is an engineering
allocation, not already included in the six-component reference budget.

The photon-pressure actuator has no direct magnetic coupling. Furthermore, unlike an electromagnetic or electrostatic actuator referenced to a nearby recoil mass, it does not require a mechanically moving local reaction mass. This removes the dominant direct pathways through which seismic motion of recoil-mass and environmental magnetic fluctuations would couple to the actuation force.

Motion of the external PPA optics may nevertheless couple indirectly through beam pointing, beam-position fluctuations, or residual power fluctuations. For this reason, both the transmitted and reflected beam positions are continuously monitored and the optical-power stabilization loop is treated as an essential part of the actuator.

20.7
Operation as a Photon Calibrator

The same optical system can be used as an absolute Photon Calibrator
[
165
]
. In a normal interferometer operation, the dominant component of the beam-power command is used for feedback actuation. A smaller sinusoidal modulation can simultaneously be superimposed on this
signal to generate calibration lines.

Because the radiation-pressure force is directly related to the incident optical power,

F=\frac{2P}{c}\cos\theta,

(20.30)

the applied force can be calculated from independently calibrated optical power and geometry. For rotational calibration, the corresponding torque is determined from the beam positions relative to the center of mass.

The PCal-induced yaw rotation is therefore given by

\varphi_{\rm Y}^{\rm PCal}(\omega)=-\frac{T_{\rm Y}^{\rm PCal}(\omega)}{I_{\rm Y}\left(\omega^{2}-i\Omega_{\rm Y}\omega/Q_{\rm Y}-\Omega_{\rm Y}^{2}\right)}.

(20.31)

At frequencies sufficiently above the yaw resonance,

\varphi_{\rm Y}^{\rm PCal}(\omega)\simeq-\frac{T_{\rm Y}^{\rm PCal}(\omega)}{I_{\rm Y}\omega^{2}}.

(20.32)

The conversion from the calibrated torsion-bar rotation to
strain-equivalent detector response is performed using the interferometer response described in Chap.
3
. Separating the mechanical PPA model from the interferometer response avoids introducing detector-specific optical factors into the definition of the actuator itself.

The integrated PPA/PCal system provides several operational advantages.
First, the absolute actuation coefficient can be monitored without relying on an electromagnetic actuator model. Second, continuous calibration lines can be applied while the same beams are used for interferometer control. Third, yaw and pitch excitation can be generated independently, allowing measurements of angular response and of the offset of the main interferometer beam from the nominal test-mass center.

20.8
Systematic uncertainty

When the photon-pressure actuator is used as a PCal, the dominant systematic uncertainties arise from the absolute optical power, injection positions, incident angles, and mechanical parameters of the torsion bar
[
165
]
.

For yaw calibration, the relevant quantities are the beam powers
P_{i}
,
horizontal lever arms
r_{i}^{x}
, incident angles, and the yaw moment of inertia
I_{\rm Y}
. The resulting uncertainty in the induced yaw angle can be written schematically as

\Delta\varphi_{\rm Y}=\left[\sum_{j}\left(\frac{\partial\varphi_{\rm Y}}{\partial x_{j}}\Delta x_{j}\right)^{2}\right]^{1/2},

(20.33)

where
x_{j}
represents the above parameters.

For the baseline CHRONOS design, the projected total systematic uncertainty when the PPA is operated as a PCal is approximately

\frac{\Delta\varphi_{\rm Y}}{\varphi_{\rm Y}}\simeq 1.14\%.

(20.34)

The uncertainty in the absolute beam power is presently one of the dominant terms. Further improvement is expected from high-accuracy calibration of the integrating-sphere power sensors and from improved measurements of the fabricated torsion-bar geometry and moment of inertia.

Continuous monitoring of the beam position using the QPDs in the receiver module is also essential because an injection-position error changes the lever arm length and can additionally enhance coupling to the internal elastic modes.

20.9
Complementarity with the gravitational calibrator

The Photon Calibrator and gravitational calibrator (GCal) provide
complementary absolute calibration methods for CHRONOS.

The PCal generates force through photon momentum and can inject calibration signals over a broad frequency range. Its actuation frequency is limited primarily by the optical power-control system and by internal resonances of the test mass. It is therefore particularly suitable for continuous calibration lines and for measurements extending to frequencies well above the sub-hertz band.

In contrast, the GCal produces a purely Newtonian gravitational torque and does not rely on optical-power calibration
[
67
]
. It can therefore provide an independent absolute reference at low frequencies, where its rotating-mass system can operate efficiently. See Chap.
21
for the detail of the GCal specified to CHRONOS.

Cross-calibration between the two systems provides an important consistency test. In particular, the GCal response can be used to constrain the absolute scale of the PCal, while the PCal can extend the calibrated detector response to frequencies inaccessible to practical GCal rotation
[
167
]
. The combination of PPA/PCal and GCal therefore establishes a broadband and redundant calibration strategy for CHRONOS.

20.10
Role in the CHRONOS suspension and control system

The photon-pressure actuator plays a different role from conventional local actuators installed at intermediate stages of the suspension.

During initial suspension damping and alignment, electromagnetic actuators can be retained at the upper suspension stages, where their larger actuation range is advantageous. In particular, recoil masses can be deployed for intermediate-mass and marionette-stage control because these actuators don’t directly couple to the test mass.

At the final torsion-bar test-mass stage, however, CHRONOS aims to omit a dedicated recoil mass. Once the interferometer is brought sufficiently close to its operating point, the photon-pressure actuator provides the final longitudinal and angular actuation required for lock acquisition and lock maintenance. The resulting control hierarchy is therefore

\begin{array}[]{c}\mathrm{Actuationof}\\
\mathrm{upper\ suspension}\\
\mathrm{(EM/ESD)}\end{array}\quad\longrightarrow\quad\begin{array}[]{c}\mathrm{Lock\ acquisition\ of}\\
\mathrm{Test\ mass}\\
\mathrm{(PPA)}\end{array}\quad\longrightarrow\quad\begin{array}[]{c}\mathrm{Low\ noise\ operation\ of}\\
\mathrm{Test\ mass}\\
\mathrm{(PPA)}\end{array}

(20.35)

This architecture separates the large-range suspension-control requirement from the ultra-low-noise test-mass actuation requirement.
It is particularly attractive for CHRONOS because the detector targets the sub-hertz band, where seismic and actuator noise become progressively more important.

The photon-pressure actuator is therefore not merely a calibration device, but an integral component of the CHRONOS suspension, interferometer-control, and calibration architecture.

Chapter 21  Torque-coupled Gravitational Calibrator

Precise calibration is particularly challenging for sub-Hz
torsion-bar gravitational-wave detectors, where conventional
force-coupled calibration schemes can provide insufficient
signal-to-noise ratio at low frequencies.
To overcome this limitation, CHRONOS adopts a torque-coupled
gravity field calibrator (GCal)
[
167
]
, in which a rotating quadrupolar
mass distribution generates a deterministic Newtonian gravitational
torque that couples directly to the rotational degree of freedom
of the torsion bars

[
72
,
167
]
.

The central concept is illustrated in
Fig.
21.1
.
In conventional gravity-based calibration schemes developed for
kilometer-scale interferometers, the time-dependent gravitational field
primarily excites translational motion of the test mass.
In contrast, the torque-coupled configuration places the rotating
quadrupole rotor directly beneath the torsion bar so that the
gravity-gradient interaction directly excites the torsional mode.
This direct coupling substantially enhances the calibration signal
in the sub-Hz band.

Because the rotor has quadrupolar symmetry, the dominant gravitational
excitation appears at twice the rotor rotation frequency,

f_{\rm cal}=2f_{\rm rot}.

(21.1)

The resulting signal is therefore a narrow spectral calibration line
whose amplitude can be predicted from the gravitational constant,
the calibration masses, and the measured geometry of the system.

Unlike electromagnetic coil actuators, the excitation mechanism
does not require an actuator-force model.
Compared with photon-pressure calibration

[
162
,
164
]
, the applied excitation is determined directly
by Newtonian gravity and the mass geometry.
The observed detector response still contains the mechanical
susceptibility of the torsion bars; however, in the CHRONOS
calibration band the excitation frequency lies well above the
torsional resonance.
The response therefore approaches the inertial limit and becomes
largely insensitive to uncertainties in the torsional spring constant
and damping.

Figure 21.1:

Conceptual comparison of a conventional force-coupled GCal
(left) and the torque-coupled GCal adopted for CHRONOS (right),
reproduced from the submitted GCal study.

Table
21.1
collects the geometric, mass,
frequency, and correction-factor parameters used in the submitted
GCal and Yukawa studies. The two columns describe distinct source
configurations and should not be combined: the first is the
single-rotor calibration reference, whereas the second is the
long–short differential arrangement used for Newtonian cancellation.

Table 21.1:
Key parameters used in the CHRONOS GCal model

[
67
]
and the submitted differential-gravity
analysis
[
72
]
.
Here
m
is the mass
of each rotor endpoint and

is the mass of each effective

21.1
Geometrical configuration

The geometry of the torque-coupled GCal is shown in
Fig.
21.2
.
A Cartesian coordinate system is defined with the center of the
torsion bar at the origin and the torsion axis along the
z
direction.
The center of the GCal rotor is located below the torsion bar with
vertical separation
h
and may have a small transverse offset

\bm{\rho}=(\rho_{x},\rho_{y},0),

(21.2)

with

\rho_{\perp}^{2}=\rho_{x}^{2}+\rho_{y}^{2}.

(21.3)

To describe the leading quadrupolar interaction, the torsion bar
is represented by two effective masses
M
located at

\pm a
from the torsion axis.
For a distributed torsion bar,
a
is therefore an

effective quadrupole arm length
, rather than simply the
physical half-length of the bar.
Similarly, the GCal rotor is represented by two calibration masses

m
located at radius
b
from the rotor axis.

The characteristic geometrical scale is defined as

R_{0}^{2}=a^{2}+b^{2}+h^{2}+\rho_{\perp}^{2},

(21.4)

and the dimensionless quadrupolar coupling parameter is

\xi=\frac{2ab}{R_{0}^{2}}.

(21.5)

For the CHRONOS geometry, the hierarchy

\rho_{\perp}\ll a,b\ll R_{0}

(21.6)

allows the gravitational interaction to be expanded perturbatively.
The transverse offset produces higher-order corrections and forms
an important contribution to the systematic uncertainty budget.

Figure 21.2:

Geometrical configuration of the torque-coupled GCal.
The effective quadrupole arm length of the torsion bar is
a
,
the rotor radius is
b
, the vertical separation is
h
, and

\bm{\rho}
denotes the transverse offset between the
torsion-bar and rotor axes.

21.2
Gravitational torque

Let
\phi
denote the azimuthal orientation of the torsion bar and

\theta(t)=\omega t

(21.7)

the rotation angle of the GCal rotor.
The relevant relative angle is

\Delta=\phi-\theta.

(21.8)

Summing the Newtonian interaction between the two effective masses
of the torsion bar and the two rotor masses gives a periodic
gravitational potential.
Because of the left–right symmetry of the quadrupolar mass
distribution, odd harmonics cancel, and the leading oscillatory
component appears at
2f_{\rm rot}
.

The corresponding torque can be written as

\tau_{\phi}^{(2f)}(t)=-\tau_{2}\sin(2\Delta),

(21.9)

where

\tau_{2}=\frac{GmM}{R_{0}}\left[\frac{3}{2}\xi^{2}+\frac{35}{32}\xi^{4}+\frac{3465}{4096}\xi^{6}+\mathcal{O}(\xi^{8})\right].

(21.10)

The leading term therefore scales as

\tau_{2}\propto\frac{GmMa^{2}b^{2}}{R_{0}^{5}}.

(21.11)

This relation provides a simple design guideline:
larger calibration masses and rotor radii enhance the gravitational
torque, whereas increasing the rotor–detector separation rapidly
suppresses the calibration signal.

Finite dimensions of the torsion-bar mass distribution introduce
a correction to the idealized point-mass quadrupole model.
For the CHRONOS geometry this effect is represented by the
finite-size correction factor

\eta_{\rm GCAL}=0.9743

(21.12)

as obtained for the finite-size GCal geometry in the submitted
analyses
[
72
]
.
Thus the physical calibration-torque amplitude is

\tau_{\rm GCAL}^{(2f)}=\eta_{\rm GCAL}\tau_{2},

(21.13)

and all torque-to-response conversions below use this corrected
amplitude.

CHRONOS employs two orthogonal torsion bars.
For bar orientations separated by
\pi/2
, the GCal produces
equal-amplitude torques with opposite signs,

\displaystyle\tau_{X}(t)

\displaystyle=\eta_{\rm GCAL}\tau_{2}\sin(2\omega t),

(21.14)

\displaystyle\tau_{Y}(t)

\displaystyle=-\eta_{\rm GCAL}\tau_{2}\sin(2\omega t).

(21.15)

The calibration signal is therefore enhanced in the differential
torsion channel while common-mode excitation is suppressed.

21.3
Mechanical and strain-equivalent response

The angular motion of each torsion bar is described by the standard
driven torsional-oscillator equation

I\ddot{\phi}+\Gamma\dot{\phi}+\kappa\phi=\tau_{\phi}(t),

(21.16)

where
I
is the moment of inertia,
\kappa
is the torsional
spring constant, and
\Gamma
is the damping coefficient.
Introducing

\omega_{0}^{2}=\frac{\kappa}{I},\qquad\Gamma=\frac{I\omega_{0}}{Q},

(21.17)

the exact mechanical susceptibility is

\chi(\Omega)=\frac{1}{I\left(\omega_{0}^{2}-\Omega^{2}+i\omega_{0}\Omega/Q\right)},

(21.18)

where

\Omega=2\omega

(21.19)

is the angular frequency of the calibration signal.

The differential angular response of the two orthogonal torsion bars
is therefore

\Phi_{\rm diff}(\Omega)=2\eta_{\rm GCAL}\chi(\Omega)\tau_{2}.

(21.20)

For gravitational-wave readout, the differential angular response
is related to strain through

\Phi_{\rm diff}=\eta_{g}\left(h_{+}F_{+}+h_{\times}F_{\times}\right),

(21.21)

where

\eta_{g}\equiv\frac{I_{\rm eff}}{I}=0.93656

(21.22)

is the geometrical coupling factor of the torsion-bar detector and

F_{+}
and
F_{\times}
are the antenna pattern functions. This
detector-response factor is distinct from the GCal finite-size
correction
\eta_{\rm GCAL}
defined in
Eq. (
21.12
).
We therefore define the strain-equivalent GCal amplitude as

h_{\rm GCal}(\Omega)=\frac{\Phi_{\rm diff}(\Omega)}{\eta_{g}|F_{\rm eff}|},\qquad|F_{\rm eff}|=\sqrt{F_{+}^{2}+F_{\times}^{2}}.

(21.23)

For CHRONOS, the torsional resonance frequency satisfies

\frac{\omega_{0}}{2\pi}<0.01~{\rm Hz},

(21.24)

whereas GCal operation is considered in the

0.1
–
10~{\rm Hz}
band.
Thus,

\Omega\gg\omega_{0},

(21.25)

and the mechanical susceptibility approaches the inertial limit,

\chi(\Omega)\simeq-\frac{1}{I\Omega^{2}}.

(21.26)

The strain-equivalent calibration signal then becomes

h_{\rm GCal}(\Omega)\simeq-\frac{2\eta_{\rm GCAL}\tau_{2}}{\eta_{g}|F_{\rm eff}|I\Omega^{2}}.

(21.27)

The two factors have different physical meanings and are not
interchangeable. The factor
\eta_{\rm GCAL}
multiplies only the
applied gravitational torque to correct the finite-size GCal model,
whereas
\eta_{g}
appears only in the conversion from differential
angle to gravitational-wave strain. Consequently they enter the
final strain-equivalent amplitude through the ratio

\eta_{\rm GCAL}/\eta_{g}
.

The characteristic scaling

|h_{\rm GCal}|\propto\Omega^{-2}

(21.28)

is a consequence of the inertial response of the torsion bars.
Importantly, this approximation reduces the sensitivity of the
calibration response to uncertainties in the torsional restoring
force and damping and provides a particularly simple absolute
reference in the sub-Hz observation band.

21.4
General Newtonian torque series

The Newtonian contribution can be written to all multipole orders by
taking the long-range limit,
\beta=R/\lambda\rightarrow 0
, of the
kernel expansion derived in the submitted analysis

[
72
]
. In this limit the coefficient

B_{n}(\beta)
reduces to the purely Newtonian coefficient
A_{n}
,

\lim_{\beta\rightarrow 0}B_{n}(\beta)=A_{n},

(21.29)

where

A_{n}=\frac{1}{2^{6n-4}}\binom{4n}{2n}\binom{2n}{n-1}.

(21.30)

The finite-size-corrected Newtonian torque at the fundamental
quadrupolar harmonic is therefore

\tau_{\rm N}(t)=\eta_{\rm GCAL}\frac{GmM}{R_{0}}\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}A_{n}\xi^{2n}\sin(2\Delta).

(21.31)

The first three coefficients are

A_{1}=\frac{3}{2},\qquad A_{2}=\frac{35}{32},\qquad A_{3}=\frac{3465}{4096},

(21.32)

which reproduce Eq. (
21.10
). Equation
(
21.31
) is thus the general
Newtonian expression underlying the truncated calibration formula.

21.5
Expected calibration performance

Figure 21.3:
Coherent calibration SNR per square root of integration time,

|h_{\rm GCal}|/\sqrt{S_{h}}
, using both full-noise operating points from
Chap.
3
. A phase-referenced sinusoid of peak amplitude

|h_{\rm GCal}|
gives
\rho=|h_{\rm GCal}|\sqrt{T/S_{h}}
in stationary
noise. Materials have identical source geometry and density-scaled masses.
The plot does not compare a dimensionless line amplitude directly with an ASD.

For the updated bar parameters and the calibration geometry

a=0.30213
m,
b=0.160
m,
h=2.00
m,
m=1.8842
kg,
and
\eta_{\rm GCAL}=0.9743
, the tungsten signal at 1 Hz is

|h_{\rm GCal}|=1.17\times 10^{-14}.

(21.33)

The coherent SNR per
\sqrt{T}
at 1 Hz is approximately

3.72\times 10^{3}~\mathrm{s}^{-1/2}
at the minimum-PI point and

5.92\times 10^{3}~\mathrm{s}^{-1/2}
at the logarithmic-mean point.
For equal source volumes, SUS304 and A5083 multiply these values by

7.93/19.3
and
2.66/19.3
, respectively. This estimate uses the
inertial response, one-sided noise, and known signal phase; practical
integration also requires stable rotor phase and readout calibration.

The systematic uncertainty of the predicted GCal response can be
evaluated by propagating uncertainties in

h_{\rm GCal}=h_{\rm GCal}(M,m,I,a,b,h,\rho_{\perp},G).

(21.34)

A first-order perturbative error analysis gives a total fractional
systematic uncertainty of approximately

\frac{\sigma_{\rm sys}}{|h_{\rm GCal}|}\simeq 2.4\times 10^{-3}=0.24\%.

(21.35)

The dominant contributions arise from geometrical alignment and
position uncertainties, while uncertainties in the calibration
masses and the gravitational constant are subdominant.
At
1~{\rm Hz}
, the corresponding absolute systematic uncertainty
is of order

\delta h_{\rm GCal}\simeq 2.8\times 10^{-17}.

(21.36)

These conditional estimates motivate the torque-coupled GCal as a proposed
absolute calibration scheme for CHRONOS.
Its principal advantages are the direct gravitational coupling to
the torsional observable, a high-SNR monochromatic calibration line,
an analytically predictable excitation amplitude, and a mechanical
response that reduces to a simple inertial form throughout the
observation band.
The technique therefore provides a robust calibration reference for
precision sub-Hz gravitational-wave measurements.

Chapter 22  Prototype Torsion-Bar System at National Central University

22.1
Overview

A room-temperature prototype of the CHRONOS torsion-bar detector is currently
under development and commissioning at National Central University (NCU),
Taiwan.
The purpose of this prototype is not to reproduce the final sensitivity of
CHRONOS, but to establish the key experimental technologies required for the
future detector, including torsion-bar suspension, multi-degree-of-freedom
sensing and control, interferometric readout, and stable operation of the
optical system.

The prototype consists of two independently suspended torsion bars arranged
orthogonally in a cross configuration.
This geometry provides a test platform for studying the differential rotational
response that will eventually constitute the gravitational-wave readout of
CHRONOS.
At the present stage, particular emphasis is placed on stabilizing the rigid-body
motion of each torsion bar sufficiently to enable stable Michelson
interferometer operation.

Each suspended torsion bar has six rigid-body degrees of freedom:
three translational motions, Length (
L
), Transverse (
T
), and Vertical (
V
),
and three rotational motions, Pitch (
P
), Yaw (
Y
), and Roll (
R
).
The current NCU prototype focuses on active control of five of these degrees of freedom except for
V
.

The prototype provides an important intermediate step between component-level
tests and the future integrated CHRONOS detector.
In particular, the current commissioning program aims to demonstrate that the
relatively large low-frequency motion of a torsion-bar suspension can be
suppressed sufficiently by local control to permit stable interferometric
readout.

22.2
Prototype Instrument

Figure
22.1
shows the photograph of the prototype setup in NCU, and Fig.
22.2
shows its schematic drawing. The mechanical system consists of two torsion bars suspended independently and
oriented approximately orthogonally.
Each bar carries optical mirrors for interferometric and local sensing, as well
as permanent magnets that couple to externally mounted coils used for
actuation.

Figure 22.1:

Prototype cross-torsion-bar system currently under commissioning at
National Central University.

Figure 22.2:

Optical and mechanical layout of the NCU prototype, including
Michelson interferometers, optical levers, shadow sensors,
and coil–magnet actuators.

The suspension is designed such that the yaw motion has a substantially lower
resonance frequency than the other rigid-body degrees of freedom.
This is a defining characteristic of torsion-bar suspensions and provides the
mechanical basis for extending gravitational measurements toward the sub-Hz
frequency region. Mechanical and geometrical parameters of the torsion bar are summarized in Table
22.1
.

Table 22.1:

Parameters of the torsion bars used in the NCU prototype. Center of mass is measured in vertical direction from the upper surface of each bare where the suspension fiber is attached.

Quantities (Unit)

Values (X/Y bar)

Material

Stainless Steel SUS304

-3
]

190

0.29

300

50

Bar height [mm]

50

Mass [kg]

5.16

Moment of inertia about yaw [kg m
2
]

0.045
